# TEMPLE OF GU: CALL OF THE TAÍNO

# INTRODUCTION

## Who I Am and Why This Book Exists

My name is Philip Ryan Deal, though most people simply call me Ryan.

I am Boricua—Puerto Rican—descended from the Taíno people who have lived in the Caribbean for thousands of years. I am also an initiated priest of the African diaspora traditions. My first initiation was in 21 Divisions. I was crowned through Itefa, face-down initiation in Ifá, in Osogbo, Nigeria, within a royal Ọ̀ṣun lineage. I have been initiated into Palo Mayombe, the Kongo-derived tradition of Cuba. I have walked the path of Haitian Vodou. I have trained in these African diasporic traditions for thirty years.

And through all of that training, through all those initiations, through all those years of learning how to speak with spirits and walk between worlds—I kept feeling a pull. A call. A voice that said: Don't forget where you come from. Don't forget the people who were here first. Don't forget the land that birthed you.

That voice was the Zemí. The spirits of Borikén. The Taíno ancestors who have never stopped speaking, even when the world tried to silence them, even when history declared them extinct, even when their own descendants forgot the old ways.

They were calling me home.

This book is my answer to that call.

## What You Hold in Your Hands

This is not a history book. There are scholars who can tell you about Taíno culture better than I can, anthropologists who have spent decades studying artifacts and petroglyphs and the slim records that survived. Go read them. I recommend it. Learn the history.

This is not an anthropology text. I am not trying to reconstruct exactly how the Taíno lived five hundred years ago. That project—noble as it is—is not mine.

This is a living practice manual.

This is a book about how to walk the Taíno spiritual path right now, in the world as it exists today, as a person living in the twenty-first century who wants to build relationship with the Zemí, who wants to honor the ancestors, who wants to connect to the land in a way that is real and reciprocal and transformative.

More specifically, this is an expansion book for the Temple of Gu tabletop roleplaying game—a game that teaches genuine spiritual practice through the structure of collaborative storytelling and dice mechanics.

If you have never played an RPG before, don't worry. I will teach you.

If you have played a thousand RPGs but never thought of them as spiritual technology, buckle up. You're about to discover something new.

The Temple of Gu RPG uses game mechanics—character creation, dice rolls, progression systems, collaborative storytelling—as a framework for practicing real spiritual work. When you play a Taíno Seeker in this game, you are not pretending. You are practicing. You are learning how to make offerings, how to speak to spirits, how to navigate the ethical complexities of walking a sacred path.

And if you do it with sincerity, if you actually perform the practices the game teaches, if you treat the Zemí as real beings rather than fictional constructs—then something extraordinary happens.

The game becomes real. The practice becomes genuine. The transformation becomes inevitable.

This book will teach you how.

## A World Without Colonization

Before we go any further, I need to tell you something crucial about the world this book takes place in:

In the world of the Temple of Gu, colonization never happened.

This is not our timeline. This is not our history. In this world, the Taíno people were never "discovered" by Europeans, never enslaved, never nearly wiped out by disease and violence and systematic cultural destruction.

In this world, the Taíno civilization developed naturally over thousands of years. Cities grew. Technology advanced. Universities were founded. Spiritual practices evolved. The culture thrived.

Modern Borikén—Puerto Rico—is a vibrant, independent island nation with millions of people living in cities and towns, speaking Taíno and Spanish and English, working with solar panels and fiber optics and smartphones, while still honoring the Zemí, still making tobacco offerings, still dancing the areyto, still playing the sacred ball game in the batey.

Why did I make this choice?

Because I am tired of trauma narratives. I am tired of every story about Indigenous people being a story about loss and suffering and barely surviving. Those stories are true. Those stories are important. But they are not the only stories.

I wanted to show what we could have been. What we should have been. What we are, in the deepest part of our souls, underneath all the colonial damage.

I wanted to create a space where Taíno spiritual practice is not an act of defiant recovery but simply... how things are. Where children grow up learning to speak to river spirits the same way they learn to read. Where making offerings to Yúcahu before planting cassava is as normal as checking the weather forecast. Where being Taíno is not a political statement but an identity as natural as breathing.

This is Afrofuturist methodology applied to Indigenous experience. We imagine the world as it should have been, and in imagining it, we begin to build it.

So when you read this book, when you play this game, you are not engaging with historical trauma. You are engaging with possibility. With what we can still become. With the future we are building, one tobacco offering at a time.

## The Structure of This Book

This expansion is organized into seven chapters plus an appendix:

Chapter 1: The Taíno World introduces you to the cosmology, culture, and daily life of the Taíno people in this thriving, uncolonized timeline. You will learn about the three realms (Turey, Atabey, Coaybay), the fundamental truth that everything is Zemí, and how modern Taíno cities blend ancient wisdom with contemporary technology. This chapter paints the picture of the world you will be playing in.

Chapter 2: Walking the Taíno Path teaches you the mechanics of playing a Taíno character. You will learn how to deepen your connection to the Taíno Lineage, how to choose between three regional expressions (Borikén, Quisqueya, Cuba), and how to walk one of four sacred callings: the Bohíque (shaman-priest), the Naboría (sacred servant of the land), the Guatiao (oath-sibling and bridge-builder), or the Caracol Keeper (voice of the storm). You will also learn twelve Attunements—permanent spiritual gifts granted by the Zemí.

Chapter 3: The Great Zemí introduces you to twenty major spirits of the Taíno tradition. You will meet Yúcahu (lord of cassava and abundance), Guabancex (the hurricane mother who teaches through destruction), Maquetaurie Guayaba (the lord of the dead who weighs your life), and many others. Each spirit profile includes personal stories, guidance on how they speak and what they want, and game mechanics for working with them.

Chapter 4: Rituals of Borikén presents fifteen complete Rituals—sacred practices you can perform both in-game and in real life—along with fifteen Sacred Implements (spiritually-charged objects). These are the tools of Taíno spiritual work: the Calling of Cohoba, the Planting of Trigonolitos, the Hurricane Petition, the River Blessing, and more.

Chapter 5: Borikén as Living Land takes you on a journey through the geography of Puerto Rico as sacred landscape. You will explore El Yunque (the rainforest cathedral), Río Camuy (the underworld river), the Seven Sacred Waterfalls, and other sites where the Zemí dwell and the boundary between worlds grows thin.

Chapter 6: Mysteries of the Island provides ten ready-to-run adventures—complete Mysteries with NPCs, plot hooks, branching paths, and rewards. These range from "The Silent Stone" (a Zemí has stopped speaking—why?) to "The Carib Raid" (how do you respond when violence comes to the border villages?). Each Mystery is designed to be playable in one to three sessions.

Chapter 7: For the Guide offers resources for the person running the game—random tables for improvisation, twenty Taíno NPCs with full personalities and stats, ten Carib NPCs (because they are people too, not just antagonists), and guidance on running Taíno-centered campaigns with cultural respect and spiritual authenticity.

Appendix: Resources and Respect includes a Taíno language primer, a comprehensive glossary, recommended reading, and a personal note from me about why this work matters and what I hope you will do with it.

## Who This Book Is For

This book is for anyone who feels the call of the Taíno path.

You do not need to be Puerto Rican to use this book. You do not need to be Indigenous. You do not need to be Caribbean. The Zemí do not check your genealogy. They check your heart.

If you approach with respect, with sincerity, with genuine willingness to learn and practice and build relationship—the path is open to you.

That said, there are responsibilities that come with walking a spiritual path that is not your own by birth:

First, you must honor the living culture. The Taíno people are not extinct. We are here. We are alive. We are rebuilding our traditions, reclaiming our languages, fighting for recognition and rights. If you are going to walk this path, you need to support the real Taíno communities doing this work. Donate. Amplify. Show up.

Second, you must not appropriate. Learning a practice because it speaks to you is not appropriation. Taking sacred elements out of context, commodifying them, claiming authority you have not earned, or using them to profit without giving back—that is appropriation. Don't do it.

Third, you must do the actual work. You cannot play this game casually and expect spiritual results. You have to make the offerings. You have to speak to the Zemí. You have to sit with discomfort and change your life when the spirits ask you to. This is not entertainment. This is practice.

Fourth, you must respect the boundaries. Some things in Taíno tradition—as in all traditions—are not for everyone. Some practices require initiation. Some knowledge is not meant to be shared publicly. I will tell you when we reach those boundaries. When I do, respect them.

If you can commit to these responsibilities, then welcome. The Zemí are waiting for you. The ancestors want to meet you. The path is ready.

## A Note on the Taíno-Carib Conflict

In this book, you will encounter the Carib people—another Indigenous group of the Caribbean who, in this timeline, have an ongoing conflict with the Taíno over territory, resources, and fundamentally different ways of life.

The Taíno are agricultural, settled, community-focused. The Carib are maritime, raiding, individualistic. These differences create natural friction.

But I want to be absolutely clear about something: the Carib are not villains. They are not evil. They are not orcs or goblins or faceless antagonists to be slaughtered.

They are people. They have their own cosmology, their own spirits, their own reasons for doing what they do. They have families. They have honor. They have complex internal politics and fierce loyalty to their own.

The conflict between Taíno and Carib in this book is meant to create story tension, ethical dilemmas, opportunities for growth. It is not meant to justify violence or dehumanization.

Some Mysteries in this book will involve Carib raids. Some will involve negotiation with Carib leaders. Some will involve moments where you have to decide: do I fight, or do I seek understanding?

There is no single right answer. But there is a wrong approach: treating the Carib as less than human.

If you find yourself doing that—if your character starts talking about Carib as "savages" or "animals" or anything that strips away their humanity—stop. Examine that impulse. Ask why you're doing it.

And then do better.

## How to Use This Book

If you are a player—someone who will be creating and playing a Taíno Seeker—read Chapters 1 through 4. That is everything you need to understand the world, create your character, and begin walking the path.

If you are a Guide—someone who will be running the game, presenting Mysteries, embodying the Zemí—read everything. But pay special attention to Chapters 5, 6, and 7. Those are your tools.

If you are both player and Guide (running a solo game or preparing to teach others), read it all, then read it again. This book is dense. There is a lot here. Take your time.

And here is my most important instruction:

Use this book as a doorway, not a prison.

I have given you structure. I have given you mechanics. I have given you stories and spirits and sacred practices. But I have not given you a script.

You will take what I have written and make it your own. You will encounter Zemí I have not listed. You will develop practices I have not described. You will walk paths I cannot imagine.

This is good. This is right. This is how living traditions work.

The Taíno path is not a museum exhibit. It is not a set of rules carved in stone. It is a living river, constantly flowing, constantly changing, carrying the old and creating the new in equal measure.

I am giving you a map to the riverbank. I am showing you how to build a boat. I am teaching you the basics of navigation.

But the journey—the actual movement through the water, the discovery of what lies around each bend, the transformation that comes from being willing to flow—that is yours to experience.

## My Promise to You

I promise you three things:

First, I promise honesty. Everything in this book comes from my genuine understanding and practice. I am not making things up to sound exotic or mystical. I am not inflating my credentials or claiming authority I do not have. I am sharing what I know, as I know it, with the humility of someone who is still learning.

Second, I promise respect. I have worked hard to honor the Taíno tradition, to consult with elders and community members, to present these practices in a way that preserves their integrity while making them accessible. If I have failed anywhere—if something in this book is inaccurate or disrespectful—I want to know. I will listen. I will correct.

Third, I promise that this works. If you do the practices in this book with sincerity, if you make the offerings, if you speak to the Zemí and listen for their responses, if you walk this path with commitment—you will experience something real. Not fantasy. Not imagination. Not self-delusion.

Real relationship with conscious beings who have existed since before humans had language. Real transformation of your own consciousness and character. Real power to heal, to create, to navigate the world with wisdom and grace.

I promise this because I have lived it. Because the Zemí have changed my life in ways I could never have predicted. Because I know—with the certainty that comes from decades of practice—that this path is true.

## An Invitation

So here is my invitation to you:

Come sit with me.

Not physically—though if we ever meet in person, I would be honored to share tobacco and conversation. But spiritually. Heart to heart. Elder to seeker.

I am going to teach you about the Taíno people. About the Zemí. About the practices that connect us to spirits and land and ancestors. About how to walk through the world as if everything is alive—because everything is alive.

I am going to challenge you. To push you. To ask you to do things that feel uncomfortable or strange or too big for who you currently are.

I am going to tell you stories—some from history, some from my own life, some from the living mythology that is still being written by those of us who walk this path.

And I am going to hold space for you to become something new. Something more connected. Something more whole.

The Taíno path is not easy. It requires work. It requires patience. It requires the willingness to be changed by what you encounter.

But if you are ready—if you feel that pull, that call, that sense that there is something more and you want to reach for it—then everything you need is here.

The tobacco is in the pouch. The river is waiting. The Zemí are watching to see if you will show up.

Will you?

The door is open. The path is clear. Your ancestors—whether Taíno by blood or Taíno by choice—are hoping you will walk it.

So take a breath. Say a prayer. Speak your intention aloud:

I am here to learn. I am here to practice. I am here to build relationship with the Zemí.

And then turn the page.

The journey begins.

We return to the root.

Ase. Ase. Ase.

—Philip Ryan Deal

The Temple of Gu

December 2025

# CHAPTER 1: THE TAÍNO WORLD

## We Begin at the Beginning

Come, sit with me.

There is tobacco in the pouch, and the cohoba stool is ready if you need it later, but for now we will simply talk. Elder to seeker. Heart to heart. I will tell you about the people I come from, the land that shaped me, the spirits that walk beside us still.

My name is Philip Ryan Deal, and I am Taíno. Boricua. From the island of Borikén—the island you may have heard called by other names, but which has always been, and will always be, Borikén. The land of the valiant noble lord. The island that speaks.

I am also an initiated priest of the African diaspora—crowned in Ifá at Osogbo, initiated into 21 Divisions, Palo Mayombe, and Haitian Vodou—and those traditions have their own books, their own teachings. But this book—this teaching—is about the people who were here first. The people who never left. The people whose civilization stretches back not hundreds but thousands of years, unbroken, uncolonized, thriving.

The Taíno are my ancestors. And if you are reading this, perhaps they are calling you too.

## The Three Realms: How the World Is Shaped

Before we talk about cities or ceremonies, before we discuss the daily life of the Taíno people, you must understand how we see the shape of reality itself. Because everything we do—from planting cassava to building solar arrays, from smoking tobacco to navigating the internet—is rooted in this fundamental understanding:

The world has three faces. Three realms. Three places where spirit and matter meet.

### Turey: The Sky Realm

Look up. What do you see?

In the daylight, you see the sun—Baba Sun, we call him, the father who feeds all growing things. At night, you see the stars, those distant fires that guide the fishing canoes and the cargo ships alike. You see the moon—Grandmother Moon, who pulls the tides and the blood and the planting times into rhythm.

But Turey is not just the physical sky. Turey is the realm where the great Zemí dwell. The spirits of hurricane and rain. The ancestors who have walked so far into wisdom that they no longer need bodies. The organizing principles of the universe—fertility, abundance, growth, destruction, renewal.

When the Bohíques—our priest-shamans—take cohoba and their spirits rise up through the crown of the head, this is where they go. Turey. The place of vision and vast perspective. The realm of the bird's eye, where you can see the whole pattern laid out like a batey court marked in the sand.

Turey teaches us to lift our gaze. To see beyond the immediate. To understand that we are part of something much larger than our single lives, our single moment.

The Zemí of Turey speak in thunder and in dreams. They send hurricanes to teach us humility and rain to bless the crops. They whisper through the mouths of the Bohíques during ceremony, offering guidance, correction, wisdom.

You cannot live only in Turey—your body would starve, your relationships would wither. But you cannot live without Turey, either. A people who never look up, who never ask the big questions, who never listen for the voice of something greater—those people become lost. Shallow. Brittle.

We look up. We listen. We remember that the sky is not empty space but living realm.

### Atabey: The Earth Realm

Now look down. Feel your feet on the ground.

This is Atabey. Named for the first mother, the source of all waters, the one who gave birth to Yúcahu himself. This is the realm of living things. Of soil and root and leaf and fruit. Of bodies and breath and beating hearts. Of rivers that run to the sea and mountains that hold the rain. Of cities and farms and fishing villages and universities.

Atabey is where we live. Where we work and love and raise children and grow old. Where we plant cassava and harvest it and grind it and make it into bread. Where we build houses and roads and solar farms and fiber optic networks. Where we gather in the batey to play the sacred ball game, where we dance the areyto until dawn, where we argue and reconcile and learn to live together.

This is the realm of relationship. Human to human. Human to land. Human to river, to mountain, to tree, to stone.

Everything in Atabey has spirit. Everything. The cohoba tree that provides the vision powder—it is alive, conscious, willing to share its gift if you approach with respect. The cassava root that feeds us—she is generous, patient, asking only that you tend her well and waste nothing. The stones we carve into Zemí figures—they already have spirit; we are simply giving that spirit a face, a form, a way to speak to us more clearly.

The rivers remember everything that happens along their banks. The mountains hold the history of the people in their bones. The old-growth trees in El Yunque—they were seedlings when our great-great-grandmothers were young, and they will shelter our great-great-grandchildren's children.

In Atabey, the Zemí walk beside us. The river spirit watches as you bathe. The mountain spirit feels your footsteps on the trail. The stone you carry in your pocket hums with quiet presence. You are never alone in Atabey. Never just one isolated thing. You are always in relationship, always part of the great web of living and conscious beings.

Modern Taíno life happens here, in Atabey. Our cities—San Juan with its university and hospitals and markets, Ponce with its art and music, Mayagüez with its research centers and fishing fleet—these are built in Atabey, with Atabey, honoring the spirits of place even as we lay down roads and raise up buildings.

We do not see technology as separate from nature. Solar panels honor the sun spirit by accepting his gift more fully. Wind turbines dance with the breath of Guabancex. The fiber optic cables that run beneath the sea—they carry light, they carry information, they carry connection, and the sea Zemí appreciate this. It is communication. It is relationship. It is not extraction but exchange.

This is the middle realm. The place of balance. Too much time in Turey and you forget to eat. Too much time in Coaybay and you forget to live. Atabey is where we must learn to walk well.

### Coaybay: The Realm Below

And now—and this is harder—look deeper. Look beneath the surface. Look into the cave where no light reaches. Look into the dark water of the cenote. Look into the place you will go when your body stops breathing.

This is Coaybay. The realm of the dead. The underworld. The place of ancestors and endings and deep, deep rest.

When a Taíno dies—when the breath leaves and the body goes still—the spirit travels to Coaybay. Not immediately. First there are three days of vigil, of singing, of tobacco smoke to guide the way. First there is the journey through the threshold, led by Opiyel-Guobiran, the dog spirit who guards the passage. First there is the accounting, the weighing, the question asked by Maquetaurie Guayaba himself: How did you live? What did you give? What remains of you in the hearts of the living?

And then—rest. Deep rest. The kind of rest that only comes after a long and full life. The ancestors dwell in Coaybay, but they are not gone. They are beneath us, supporting us, like the roots of the great tree that feed the branches. They whisper up through the cave springs. They speak through the cohoba visions. They appear in dreams to warn or bless or simply remind us that we are not the first to walk this path.

Coaybay is not a place of punishment. It is not hell. It is rest and wisdom and the long, slow transformation from individual spirit into collective ancestor. Some who die young, who die with work unfinished—they may linger longer, may visit more often, may need help crossing fully. Some who die old and satisfied—they sink down deep and sleep the good sleep, surfacing only when called by prayer or great need.

The caves are the doorways to Coaybay. Cueva Ventana, Cueva del Indio, the great caverns of Río Camuy—these are not just geological formations. These are thresholds. Sacred places where the boundary is thin. Where you can speak to the dead and they can speak back. Where a Bohíque with the right training can walk between the worlds.

We do not fear Coaybay. Death is not the enemy. Death is the other half of life, the rest after the dance, the silence after the song. But we do not rush toward it, either. We live fully in Atabey, we reach up to Turey for vision, and we honor Coaybay by living well so that when we arrive, we have something to offer.

## The Fundamental Truth: Everything Is Zemí

Now that you understand the three realms, we must speak of the central truth that shapes all Taíno life:

Everything has spirit.

Not metaphorically. Not symbolically. Actually.

The river is conscious. The mountain is aware. The stone in your hand is listening. The cassava plant growing in the field—she knows you. She feels your hands when you harvest her. She appreciates the tobacco you offer in thanks.

This is what we mean when we say Zemí.

Zemí is not a "god" in the way other cultures use that word. Zemí is not separate from the world, looking down from some distant throne. Zemí is in the world. Zemí is the world. Every part of it that has presence, awareness, personality, power.

Some Zemí are vast—Yúcahu, the lord of cassava and fertility, whose blessing ensures the harvest. Guabancex, the hurricane mother, whose lessons are written in storm wind and flood water. Atabey herself, the earth mother, the source of all waters.

Some Zemí are local—the spirit of this particular river, this particular mountain, this particular stone. The Zemí of El Yunque is different from the Zemí of El Toro. The Zemí of Río Grande de Loíza speaks with a different voice than the Zemí of Río Camuy. Each place has its own personality, its own needs, its own gifts to offer.

Some Zemí are small—the cohoba tree that provides vision powder, the hutía that gives its body for food, the coquí whose voice marks the boundaries between day and night.

And here is the part that matters for your practice as a Seeker:

You can be in relationship with Zemí.

Not worship—relationship. Worship implies distance, hierarchy, groveling before something far above you. Relationship implies reciprocity, conversation, mutual respect.

When you approach a river, you can speak to the river Zemí. You can introduce yourself. You can ask permission to bathe, to fish, to take water. You can offer tobacco—the fundamental gift, the sacred bridge between humans and spirits. And the river will answer. Not always in words. Sometimes in feeling—a sense of welcome or warning. Sometimes in signs—the sudden appearance of a heron, the flash of silver fish. Sometimes in direct knowing, the kind that bypasses language entirely and lands in your belly as certainty.

This is not superstition. This is not primitive thinking that "modern" people have outgrown. This is how the world actually works, and the Taíno have never forgotten it.

When we build a road, we ask the land Zemí where it should run. When we construct a building, we make offerings to the spirits of place—tobacco, cassava bread, rum poured into the foundation. When we launch a new solar farm, the Bohíques consecrate the panels, speaking to the sun spirit, establishing relationship, ensuring that this is gift-exchange and not theft.

The University of Borikén—our great center of learning in San Juan—has a Bohíque on staff for every major construction project. Not as decoration. As necessity. Because if you build on top of a sacred site without permission, without proper relationship, the building will fail. Students will feel uneasy. Equipment will malfunction. The land itself will resist.

This is not magic. This is physics. Spirit physics, but physics nonetheless.

## Modern Taíno Life: How We Live Now

You might be wondering: what does a Taíno city look like? How do we balance ancient wisdom with modern needs? What does daily life look like for someone born into this culture in the current year?

Let me paint you a picture.

### San Juan: The Beating Heart

San Juan is the political and spiritual capital of Borikén. Not the largest city—that honor goes to Ponce—but the oldest, the most sacred, the place where the Cacique Supreme maintains the Grand Batey and the Council of Elders meets to guide the island.

The city sits where it has always sat, on the northern coast where the harbor is deep and protected. But it has grown. Spread. Evolved.

The old city—Viejo San Juan, we call it—is still there, still vibrant, still the ceremonial heart. The streets are narrow, paved with stones that remember five hundred years of footsteps. The buildings are painted in bright colors—blue and yellow and pink and green—because the Taíno have always loved color, always understood that beauty is not frivolous but necessary. That a beautiful place creates beautiful thoughts, beautiful actions, beautiful lives.

In the center of Viejo San Juan stands the Grand Batey—a massive ceremonial ball court, open to the sky, surrounded by carved stone Zemí figures that represent the great spirits. This is where the sacred games are played during festival times. Where the Council meets for major decisions. Where the Cacique Supreme addresses the people during crisis or celebration.

Around the Grand Batey are the government buildings, the Hall of Records, the Temple of Atabey (where Bohíques train and the sick come for healing), and the Museum of Memory (where our history is preserved in artifact and story and living practice).

But San Juan is not frozen in time.

Beyond Viejo San Juan, the modern city spreads out in careful balance with the land. Buildings rise, yes—we have towers, research centers, hospitals, apartment complexes that house tens of thousands—but they rise in consultation with the land Zemí. The architects work with Bohíques to determine where structures can be built without disrupting sacred sites. The urban planners ensure that rivers are not buried in concrete but allowed to flow visible and accessible. Parks are not afterthoughts but priorities—green spaces where people can touch earth, where children can climb trees, where the boundary between city and forest remains permeable.

The University of Borikén dominates the eastern part of the city—a sprawling campus of learning where traditional knowledge and cutting-edge research sit side by side. In one building, students study the molecular biology of cassava, working to develop varieties that resist drought and disease. In another building, Bohíque apprentices learn to prepare cohoba, to read petroglyphs, to communicate with Zemí. These are not separate disciplines. The biology students take courses in traditional agriculture. The Bohíque apprentices study ecology and climate science.

We do not see a conflict between knowledge and spirit. All knowledge is sacred. All inquiry is prayer, if done with the right heart.

The city runs on solar and wind and geothermal—we are an island, after all, blessed with constant sun and steady trade winds and volcanic heat still simmering in the mountain depths. The power grid is managed by the Collective of Light, a cooperative that treats electricity not as commodity but as shared resource. Every neighborhood has battery storage for hurricane season. Every home has the option to contribute excess power back to the grid.

Transportation is mixed. Many people still walk—the city is built for walking, with shaded paths and water fountains and rest stops. Many bike—the coastal roads have dedicated lanes, and bike-sharing stations dot every neighborhood. For longer distances, there are buses and trains, electric vehicles that run on the cooperative grid. And for those who need or want them, there are private cars—though these are less common, seen as less virtuous than shared transit.

The harbor is busy with fishing boats and cargo ships and the great canoes—yes, canoes, massive outrigger vessels that can carry fifty people and their goods, still the preferred method of inter-island travel for many. These canoes are works of art, carved from single trees (felled with permission and proper ceremony), painted with petroglyphs and Zemí symbols, captained by navigators who read stars and currents and bird flight as easily as you might read a street sign.

Food comes from the land and sea. Every neighborhood has community gardens. Many apartment buildings have rooftop farms. The markets overflow with cassava and corn and beans, with plantains and yams and tropical fruit, with fresh-caught fish and lobster and conch. Meat is less central to the diet—the Taíno have always been more farmers than herders—but there are hutía (a small rodent, sacred food animal) and chicken and pork, all raised in small-scale farms where the animals are treated with respect and gratitude.

And always, everywhere, there is tobacco. Growing in the gardens. Offered at the shrines. Smoked in prayer. Given as gift between friends. Tobacco is the bridge. Tobacco is the fundamental offering. You cannot understand Taíno spirituality without understanding tobacco's role—not as addiction or vice, but as sacrament.

### Ponce: The City of Art and Lions

Ponce, on the southern coast, is larger than San Juan, more spread out, more industrial. This is where the great workshops are—the craftspeople who make the sacred implements, the artists who paint and sculpt and weave, the engineers who design the solar arrays and wind farms.

Ponce is loud and colorful and proud. The people there will tell you they are the true heart of Borikén, that San Juan is all politics and ceremony but Ponce is where things actually get made. There is truth to this. Ponce produces.

The southern coast is drier than the north, hotter, more suited to certain crops—cotton grows well there, and cacao, and the special tobacco used for cohoba. The Ponce region is also where most of the island's geothermal plants are located, tapping the volcanic heat that still simmers beneath the central mountains.

Ponce has its own batey, its own Council, its own Bohíques. There is friendly rivalry between Ponce and San Juan, expressed in ball games and poetry competitions and debates over the best way to prepare cassava bread. This rivalry is healthy. It keeps both cities sharp.

### Mayagüez: The Western Gateway

Mayagüez, on the west coast, is the center of fishing and sea trade. The port is deep, the harbor well-protected, and from here the great canoes launch on their journeys to the other islands—Quisqueya to the west, Cuba to the north, the Lesser Antilles to the east.

Mayagüez is where you go if you want to study navigation, maritime law, ocean ecology, or relationship with the sea Zemí. The Institute of Agüeybaná (named for the great sea protector) is based here, training captains and fishermen and marine biologists in a curriculum that blends traditional wayfinding with modern technology.

The people of Mayagüez are said to be more reserved than Ponce, more contemplative than San Juan. They spend their lives looking at the horizon, listening to the rhythm of waves, learning to read weather patterns in cloud shape and wind direction. This creates a particular temperament—patient, far-seeing, comfortable with uncertainty.

### The Mountain Towns: Where the Bohíques Train

Between the coastal cities lie the central mountains—the cordillera—steep and green and perpetually wrapped in mist. This is where the rainforest grows thick, where the rivers begin, where the old ways are preserved most purely.

The mountain towns are small—a few hundred people, maybe a thousand at most. Life here is quieter, slower, more directly connected to land and weather and the turning of seasons. These are farming communities, primarily—cassava and coffee and plantains grown on steep terraces, tended with the same methods used for centuries because those methods work, because the land responds to them, because the Zemí appreciate continuity.

But the mountains are also where the Bohíques train. Where young people called to the shamanic path come to apprentice with elders, learning to prepare cohoba, to read omens, to communicate with Zemí, to heal sickness of body and spirit, to guide the dead, to maintain the sacred balance.

There are three major training centers:

The House of Yúcahu (in the town of Jayuya, central mountains) focuses on agricultural spirituality, healing, and working with plant Zemí.

The Cave of Maquetaurie (near Arecibo, northern mountains) specializes in death work, ancestor communication, and underworld journeying.

The Peak of Guabancex (high in the Luquillo range, near El Yunque) trains those called to work with weather, transformation, and the fierce lessons of the storm.

Apprenticeship lasts years—typically seven, though some take longer. It is not easy. It is not romantic. It involves hard work, discomfort, confronting your own darkness, serving your teacher with humility, going without sleep during ceremony, fasting, purging, sitting alone in caves or on mountaintops until the spirits speak.

But those who complete the training emerge changed. Bohíques are respected, honored, sought out for counsel. They serve their communities as healers, advisors, ceremonialists, bridges between the human and spirit worlds.

And here is something important: you do not need to be a Bohíque to walk the Taíno spiritual path. The Bohíques are specialists, yes, but every Taíno person—every person, regardless of background, who chooses to walk this path—can have relationship with the Zemí. Can make offerings. Can pray. Can listen.

Spirituality is not the exclusive domain of priests. It is the birthright of every person who has breath and heart and willingness to pay attention.

### Technology in Service of Spirit

You might be wondering: how does a culture that values ancient wisdom integrate modern technology? Isn't there a conflict?

Only if you believe that technology is inherently soulless. We do not.

Remember: everything has spirit. The silicon in the computer chips—that is earth, transformed by lightning (electricity) and human intention into a form that can think, calculate, remember. The satellites circling overhead—they are eyes in Turey, allowing us to see weather patterns, track hurricanes, communicate across vast distances. The fiber optic cables—they carry light, they carry language, they are the neural network of collective consciousness.

Technology is not the enemy of spirit. Greed is. Extraction is. Domination is. Disrespect is.

But technology used well—technology created in relationship with the Zemí, technology that serves community rather than profit, technology that enhances connection rather than replacing it—that is sacred work.

The Taíno approach to technology is simple:

First, ask permission. Before mining silicon, before laying cable, before launching satellites—ask the relevant Zemí. The earth spirits, the sky spirits, the spirits of place. Make offerings. Establish relationship. Ensure that this is gift-exchange, not theft.

Second, design for harmony. Solar panels that mimic leaf patterns. Wind turbines that move like royal palm trees in the breeze. Buildings that incorporate natural ventilation and living walls. Technology that fits into the landscape rather than dominating it.

Third, share the benefits. No private ownership of essential infrastructure. Power, water, internet—these are managed by cooperatives, by the commons. Everyone contributes. Everyone benefits. The strong support the weak. The abundant share with the scarce.

Fourth, maintain the human connection. Technology should enhance relationship, not replace it. We have phones and internet, yes, but we also have face-to-face gatherings, communal meals, areyto dances where the whole neighborhood comes together to sing and move and tell stories. We do not allow screens to substitute for presence.

Fifth, remember that all tools are temporary. Hurricane season reminds us. When the storm comes—and the storm will come—the power goes out, the internet fails, the roads flood. And then we fall back on what is eternal: relationship with land, with Zemí, with each other. The ability to find fresh water, to prepare food without electricity, to navigate by stars, to treat injury with plant medicine. These skills are not obsolete. They are foundation.

## The Shape of Taíno Society

Who governs? Who decides? How is power distributed in a culture that has grown and evolved for thousands of years without outside interference?

The Taíno have never been anarchists—we have structure, hierarchy, leadership. But we have also never been autocrats. Power is distributed. Decisions are made in council. Leaders serve at the consent of the led.

### The Caciques: Leaders of Place

Every region has a Cacique—a chief, a leader, the one who coordinates and decides and bears final responsibility. In the small mountain towns, the Cacique might govern two hundred people. In the cities, they govern hundreds of thousands.

Caciques are not elected, exactly, but neither is the position simply inherited. When a Cacique dies or steps down, the Council of Elders meets to discern who should serve next. They consider lineage—the family of the previous Cacique is given weight. They consider temperament—is this person patient, wise, able to listen? They consider calling—has this person shown signs of being chosen by the Zemí for leadership?

And then they ask the people. Not a vote in the modern democratic sense, but a discernment. Do the people trust this person? Will they follow? Is there peace or resistance?

If the answer is yes—if the Elders agree and the people consent—the new Cacique is installed in ceremony. They sit on the sacred stool (duho). They receive the stone of office (a specially carved Zemí). They make vows before the community and the spirits. And then they serve.

How long? As long as they are able and willing and effective. Some Caciques serve for decades. Some for only a few years before recognizing the work is beyond them and stepping aside with honor.

The Cacique's role is coordination, not domination. They do not make laws by decree. They facilitate councils. They ensure that disputes are heard and resolved. They maintain relationship with neighboring regions. They represent their people to the Cacique Supreme. They ensure that roads are maintained, that schools are staffed, that the sick are cared for, that sacred sites are protected.

### The Cacique Supreme: Leader of the Island

Above the regional Caciques sits the Cacique Supreme—the leader of all Borikén. This position is more hereditary, typically passing within a single lineage, but still requires consent of the Council of Elders and acceptance by the people.

The current Cacique Supreme is a woman—Cacica Guanina of the House of Atabey. She is sixty-three years old, daughter of the previous Cacique Supreme, trained from childhood for this role. She is respected, loved, occasionally feared. She has led Borikén through three major hurricanes, two droughts, and the ongoing tension with Carib raiders along the eastern border.

The Cacica Supreme does not rule alone. She is advised by the Council of Elders—twenty-one Bohíques, scholars, warriors, and farmers chosen for their wisdom. She meets with the regional Caciques quarterly to hear concerns and coordinate responses. She makes final decisions on matters that affect the whole island—trade agreements, military action, major infrastructure, sacred law.

But she cannot act capriciously. If the Council loses faith in her, they can call for discernment. If the people rise up in protest, she must listen. Power in Taíno society is never absolute. It is always held in tension, always accountable.

### The Bohíques: Keepers of the Sacred

Parallel to the political structure runs the spiritual structure, and the two are distinct but intertwined.

The Bohíques are not rulers. They do not govern territories or command armies. But they are essential. They are the ones who maintain relationship with the Zemí, who perform the ceremonies that keep the island in balance, who heal the sick, who guide the dead, who read the omens and advise the Caciques.

Every town and city has at least one Bohíque. Larger places have several. They are supported by the community—given housing, food, resources—in exchange for their service. Some Bohíques focus on healing. Some on divination. Some on ceremony and ritual leadership. Some on teaching and training the next generation.

The Bohíques also form their own council—the Circle of Vision—which meets annually to discuss spiritual matters, to refine practices, to address questions of sacred law. If a Cacique wants to build on potentially sacred land, the Circle of Vision must approve. If a new ritual is proposed, the Circle evaluates it. If there is conflict between regions, the Circle may be asked to mediate.

The relationship between political and spiritual leadership is complementary. The Cacique asks, "What is practical? What serves the community's material needs?" The Bohíque asks, "What is sacred? What maintains relationship with the Zemí?" When both questions are honored, the society thrives.

### The Naborías: The Common People

Most people are neither Caciques nor Bohíques. They are Naborías—the common people, the farmers and fishers and craftspeople and teachers and engineers who do the daily work that keeps society functioning.

To be Naboría is not to be lesser. It is to be the foundation. The society rests on the shoulders of those who plant cassava, who catch fish, who build houses, who raise children, who show up day after day to do unglamorous but essential work.

Naborías have voice. They participate in community councils. They can challenge decisions. They can propose changes. A Naboría with a grievance has the right to bring it before the Cacique. A Naboría with wisdom to share can address the Council of Elders.

And any Naboría can choose a path of deeper service. Can apprentice to become Bohíque. Can train as a warrior or healer or teacher. Can rise to leadership if they have the gifts and the calling.

There is mobility in Taíno society. There is opportunity. But there is also recognition that not everyone is called to lead, and that is fine. The cassava farmer who feeds her community is doing sacred work. The fisherman who brings home conch and snapper is honoring the sea Zemí with his labor. The mother who raises her children with patience and wisdom is shaping the future.

All work is sacred if done with the right heart. All roles are necessary. All people are Zemí.

### The Nitaínos: The Noble Class

Between the common people and the Caciques sit the Nitaínos—the noble class, the administrators and military leaders and large landholders who manage territories and coordinate complex work.

The Nitaínos are typically from old families, lineages that have served the community for generations. But nobility is not just inherited—it is also earned. A Naboría who shows exceptional leadership, who manages a major project successfully, who serves with distinction in war or crisis—that person can be elevated to Nitaíno status by the Cacique.

The Nitaínos organize the large-scale work—planting and harvest across multiple farms, construction of roads and aqueducts, defense of borders, trade expeditions to other islands. They ensure that resources are distributed fairly, that projects are completed on time, that the Cacique's decisions are implemented effectively.

Like all leadership roles, Nitaínos are accountable. A Nitaíno who abuses power, who hoards resources, who fails in their responsibilities—that person can be stripped of status and returned to common life. Nobility is privilege, yes, but it is also burden. Greater expectation. Greater scrutiny. Greater consequence for failure.

## The Sacred Practices: How We Walk the Path

Theory is one thing. Practice is another. You can understand the three realms intellectually. You can know the structure of Taíno society. But until you actually do the work—until you make offerings, until you speak to the Zemí, until you participate in ceremony—the knowledge remains abstract.

So let me tell you about the practices that shape Taíno spiritual life. The things we actually do.

### Tobacco: The Fundamental Offering

If you learn nothing else from this teaching, learn this: tobacco is sacred.

Not the commercial cigarettes, full of chemicals and addiction. Not the casual habit of the stressed-out worker. But real tobacco—grown with intention, harvested with prayer, dried and cured and offered with respect.

Tobacco is the bridge between worlds. The smoke rises—carrying prayers, carrying gratitude, carrying messages to the Zemí in Turey. The scent spreads—calling attention, marking sacred space, creating an atmosphere where spirit can more easily manifest.

When you approach a river to ask permission to bathe, you offer tobacco. When you enter a cave to consult the oracle, you offer tobacco. When you plant cassava, when you begin a journey, when you ask for healing, when you celebrate a birth, when you mourn a death—you offer tobacco.

The protocol is simple:

Hold the tobacco—dried leaf, or prepared as cigar, or loose in a pouch. Speak your intention aloud. Who are you? What are you asking? What do you offer in return? Then place the tobacco—at the base of a tree, in the river water, on a stone altar, in the fire, buried in the earth. Or smoke it, breathing the prayers into the air.

The Zemí appreciate tobacco. They understand this language. They recognize the offering as gift, as respect, as the acknowledgment that relationship requires reciprocity.

You do not need to be Bohíque to offer tobacco. You do not need special training. You need only sincerity, respect, and tobacco itself.

Every Taíno home has tobacco. Every person carries a small pouch. To be without tobacco is to be spiritually unprepared, like leaving the house without water in the desert.

### Cohoba: The Vision Medicine

Cohoba is different. Cohoba is not for daily use. Cohoba is powerful, dangerous, transformative medicine that should only be approached with training and proper preparation.

Cohoba is made from the seeds of the Anadenanthera tree. The seeds are harvested, roasted, ground into powder, mixed with lime (from crushed shells) to activate the alkaloids. When inhaled—blown into the nostrils through a special tube, or snuffed from a carved tray—cohoba sends the spirit soaring into Turey.

What you experience in cohoba is not hallucination. It is not "getting high" in the recreational sense. It is vision. You see the true forms of things. You speak with Zemí directly. You receive guidance, warnings, teachings. You may travel to other realms. You may see the future. You may commune with ancestors.

But cohoba is not gentle. It is forceful. Violent, even. The initial experience is often nausea, dizziness, purging. Your body rejects it before your spirit accepts it. And then—if you surrender, if you trust, if you maintain your center—the visions come.

Only trained Bohíques work with cohoba regularly. Only those who have been taught the proper protocols, the right prayers, the safety measures. Because cohoba can also break you open in ways you are not ready for. Can show you things you cannot integrate. Can leave you lost if you do not have strong enough grounding.

For ordinary people—for those of us who are not called to the Bohíque path—cohoba is still available, but only in ceremony, only with proper guidance. A Bohíque might administer cohoba to a Seeker who is facing a major life decision, who needs vision to choose their path. Or during the coming-of-age ceremony, when a young person transitions to adulthood. Or in cases of severe illness, when the sick person needs to consult directly with the healing Zemí.

Cohoba is precious. Cohoba is powerful. Cohoba is not to be used casually or recreationally. But for those who approach it with respect, with preparation, with proper support—cohoba opens doors that cannot be opened any other way.

### The Trigonolito: Anchor Stones of Spirit

Walk into any Taíno home and you will see them—the three-pointed stones. Trigonolitos. Small or large, plain or carved, resting on altars or shelves or in gardens.

These are not decorations. These are Zemí.

The three points represent the three realms—Turey, Atabey, Coaybay. The stone itself is the anchor—the physical form that allows a specific Zemí to dwell in a specific place. When you carve a trigonolito, when you consecrate it with tobacco and prayer and offerings, you are inviting a Zemí to make a home there. To be accessible. To be present.

Some trigonolitos are ancient—carved by ancestors centuries ago, passed down through families, still active, still inhabited by the same spirits. Some are new—carved last week by a student at the University, consecrated by a Bohíque, given to a friend as blessing.

The stone chooses you as much as you choose it. You might walk along a riverbank and see a stone that calls to you. That stone already has spirit—you are simply recognizing it, giving it form, making the relationship explicit.

Or you might work with a carver—someone trained in the sacred art of shaping stone. You tell them what you need—protection, healing, guidance, fertility. They meditate, they pray, they choose the right stone from the right place. They carve the three points. They carve symbols—petroglyphs that speak to the specific Zemí you are calling. And then they hand it to you, and you complete the consecration with your own tobacco, your own prayers, your own offerings.

Once consecrated, the trigonolito becomes a permanent portal. That Zemí lives there now. You can speak to the stone and the Zemí hears you. You can make offerings at the stone and the Zemí receives them. You can ask for help and the Zemí responds—not always in the way you expect, not always immediately, but always in the way that serves your highest growth.

Some people have one trigonolito—their primary Zemí, their closest ally. Some have several—different Zemí for different purposes. The Bohíques have many—a whole altar covered in stones, a whole community of spirits dwelling in close relationship.

The trigonolito is one of the most fundamental tools of Taíno spirituality. Simple in form. Profound in function. A bridge between worlds made of earth and intention.

### The Batey: Sacred Play

Every town has one. Every neighborhood, really. The batey—the ball court, the ceremonial ground, the place where the community comes together to play.

The sacred ball game—batu, we call it—is ancient. Older than cities. Older than writing. Two teams, a rubber ball, goal posts at either end of a rectangular court. The rules are complex—you cannot use hands or feet, only hips and shoulders and head to move the ball. The game is fast, physical, demanding. Injuries happen. Glory happens. Ecstasy happens.

But batu is not just sport. It is ritual. The ball represents the sun, moving across the sky from east to west. The court represents the world, bounded by the four directions. The players are enacting cosmic drama—the struggle between light and dark, order and chaos, life and death.

Before a major decision—before war, before treaty, before beginning a large construction project—the question is often brought to the batey. Two teams form, representing the two options. They play. The winning team's position is taken as the will of the Zemí.

This might sound arbitrary—deciding important matters by ball game. But it is not. The players are in a state of flow, of heightened awareness, of connection to something greater than individual will. The game reveals what logic cannot—the deep current, the true direction, the choice that aligns with cosmic pattern.

And beyond the divination function, batu serves another essential purpose: it keeps the community strong. It teaches cooperation, strategy, physical prowess. It creates bonds between players and between spectators who cheer for their team. It channels aggression into acceptable form—better to fight on the ball court than in the streets.

Major games are festivals. The whole community gathers. There is music—drums and rattles and flutes. There is dance—the areyto, the sacred circle dance where stories are told in movement and song. There is food—cassava bread and roasted fish and tropical fruit piled high on woven mats. There is tobacco smoke rising, prayers offered, Zemí invoked.

The batey is where the profane and sacred meet. Where play becomes prayer. Where the community remembers that we are not isolated individuals but a web, a collective, a living organism that needs joy and movement and shared purpose to thrive.

## The Sacred Year: How Time Moves

The Taíno calendar is lunar, marked by the moon's cycle, but adjusted to keep pace with the solar seasons. Thirteen moons in a year, each with its own character, its own focus, its own ceremonies.

I will not detail all thirteen here—that would fill a book itself. But let me tell you about the major festivals, the times when the whole island comes together in sacred observance.

### The Planting Moon (Early Spring)

When the dry season ends and the rains return, we plant. Cassava, primarily—the foundation of our food system. But also corn, beans, squash, the sacred trinity that grows together, supports each other, feeds the people.

Planting is ceremony. The Bohíques bless the fields. Tobacco is offered to Yúcahu, the lord of cassava. The first cassava stick (the cutting from which new plants grow) is planted by the eldest woman of the community, because cassava is feminine, generous, patient.

Everyone participates—Caciques alongside Naborías, scholars alongside farmers. To plant is to participate in creation. To put seed into earth and trust that the Zemí of soil and rain and sun will collaborate in transformation.

### The Hurricane Moon (Late Summer/Early Fall)

Hurricane season is sacred time. Not because we worship storms—we do not. But because hurricanes teach essential lessons: humility, preparation, community, letting go.

Before the storms arrive, there is the Great Securing. Every house is checked—roofs reinforced, shutters tested, supplies stockpiled. Every community opens its storm shelters—the strongest buildings, well-provisioned, capable of housing hundreds if necessary. Every sacred site is protected—trigonolitos brought inside, ceremonial objects secured, the batey drained to prevent flooding.

And then we wait. We watch the sky. We listen to the radio, to the weather reports, to the Bohíques who read the omens in bird flight and wind direction.

When the storm comes—and every few years, a big one comes—we shelter. We ride it out together. We sing to keep spirits up. We pray for protection. We make offerings to Guabancex, asking not that she spare us (she has her own reasons, her own purposes) but that we survive, that we learn, that we grow stronger through the ordeal.

After the storm—the Great Rebuilding. The whole community works together. Clearing debris. Repairing damage. Checking on the vulnerable—elders, sick, isolated. This is when you see Taíno culture at its finest: everyone helps, no one is left behind, the strong support the weak without question.

Hurricane season reminds us that we are not in control. That nature is more powerful than any human system. That our technology, our cities, our pride—all of it can be humbled in a single night of wind and water.

This is sacred teaching. We honor it.

### The Harvest Moon (Late Fall)

When the cassava is ready—thick roots swollen with starch—we harvest. Again, everyone participates. Again, it is ceremony.

The first cassava harvested is offered to Yúcahu. Buried at the base of the sacred ceiba tree in the center of the batey. Given back to the earth that gave to us.

Then comes the processing—digging, washing, grating, pressing to remove the poison (raw cassava contains cyanide; this is why relationship matters—the plant provides food, but you must know how to prepare it safely). The cassava pulp is dried, ground into flour, made into bread.

The harvest festival is days of celebration. Feasting, dancing, games, gratitude. The Bohíques lead ceremonies of thanksgiving. The Cacique distributes food to those who have none—the sick, the old, the unlucky whose crops failed.

Harvest Moon teaches abundance and sharing. We have enough. We always have enough, if we share fairly.

### The Ancestor Moon (Winter Solstice)

The shortest day, the longest night. The time when the boundary between Atabey and Coaybay grows thin. When the dead walk close to the living.

This is when we honor the ancestors. Every family cleans their home altars. Every community gathers at the sacred caves. Offerings are made—tobacco, cassava bread, rum (yes, we make rum from sugarcane, and the ancestors appreciate it). Names are spoken—calling those who came before, thanking them, asking their blessing.

The Bohíques perform the Ceremony of the Canoe—ritually guiding the recently dead (those who died in the past year) on their final journey to Coaybay. This is emotional work. Hard work. But necessary. The dead must be released so they can rest. The living must let go so they can continue.

Ancestor Moon is also when young people undergo the coming-of-age ceremony—the transition from child to adult, marked by ordeal, by vision quest, by cohoba ceremony under Bohíque supervision. This is when your adult name is given. When your path begins to reveal itself.

The moon teaches us that death is not the end. That our ancestors are still with us, supporting us, guiding us. That we are links in an unbroken chain stretching back to the beginning and forward to the ending.

## The Closing of This Teaching

We have covered much ground, you and I. The three realms. The nature of Zemí. The shape of modern Taíno life. The structure of society. The sacred practices. The turning of the year.

But this is only the beginning. This is only the frame. The actual walking of the path—that comes through practice, through relationship, through showing up day after day with tobacco and open heart and willingness to listen.

The Zemí are waiting for you. The rivers want to speak with you. The mountains are ready to teach. The ancestors are hoping you will call.

But they will not force themselves upon you. They will not grab you by the shoulders and shake you awake. They will wait. Patient. Eternal. Present.

The choice is yours.

Will you approach the river and offer tobacco?

Will you carve your trigonolito and consecrate it with prayer?

Will you show up at the batey and learn to move the sacred ball?

Will you sit at the feet of a Bohíque and ask to learn?

This path is not easy. It requires work, discipline, humility. It requires you to question your assumptions, to face your fears, to change your habits. It requires you to remember that you are not the center of the universe—you are one small part of a vast web of relationships, and those relationships require tending.

But if you do the work—if you really, truly commit—you will never be alone again. You will walk in relationship with Zemí who have existed since before humans could name them. You will carry the wisdom of ancestors who survived hurricanes and droughts and the endless challenges of being alive. You will belong to a people whose culture stretches back millennia and forward into a future we are building together.

This is the Taíno world.

This is the path I am offering you.

Will you walk it?

Tobacco is in the pouch. The river is waiting. The first step is yours to take.

We return to the root.

Ase. Ase. Ase.

# CHAPTER 2: WALKING THE TAÍNO PATH

## The Question Before You

So now you know the shape of the world—the three realms, the nature of Zemí, how the Taíno people live in the modern age. You have the map. You understand the territory.

But understanding is not the same as walking.

Reading about cassava is not the same as planting it, tending it, harvesting it, grinding it into flour with your own hands until your arms ache and the kitchen smells like earth and root.

Reading about the river Zemí is not the same as standing at the water's edge at dawn with tobacco in your hand, voice shaking slightly as you introduce yourself for the first time, waiting to see if the spirit will welcome you or turn you away.

Reading about the sacred ball game is not the same as the shock of the rubber ball against your hip, the burn in your lungs as you chase it across the court, the roar of the crowd, the moment when you and your teammates move as one body, one mind, one purpose.

Theory prepares you. Practice transforms you.

This chapter is about practice. This is where we move from "knowing about" to "being." This is where you step onto the path and begin to walk it—not perfectly, not without stumbling, but with intention and commitment and the willingness to learn from every mistake.

I will teach you three things in this chapter:

First, I will show you how to deepen your connection to the Taíno Lineage itself—the spiritual inheritance, the relationship with the land and the Zemí that shapes how you move through the world. This is not just cultural identity. This is spiritual technology. The Taíno way of seeing and being has been refined over thousands of years. It works. But it requires understanding and practice.

Second, I will introduce you to four sacred callings—four Archetypes that represent different paths of service within the Taíno tradition. You have already met the general Archetypes in the core Temple of Gu teachings (the Healer, the Seer, the Warrior-Poet). Now you will meet the specifically Taíno expressions of these callings. The Bohíque who walks between worlds. The Naboría who serves through humble work. The Guatiao who binds communities through sacred oath. The Caracol Keeper who speaks through storm and shell.

Third, I will teach you twelve Attunements—permanent gifts of the spirit, ways that your relationship with the Zemí can change you at the deepest level, granting abilities that seem impossible until they become simply... what you do.

But before we begin, I need you to understand something crucial:

This path is not for spectators.

You cannot walk the Taíno path as a tourist, collecting experiences like souvenirs, taking what feels good and discarding what challenges you. The Zemí see your heart. They know when you are sincere and when you are playing. They know when you are willing to do the hard work of transformation and when you just want the aesthetic, the romance, the Instagram version of spirituality.

If you want that—if you want something easy, something that fits neatly into your existing life without requiring you to change—then close this book now. Go find a meditation app. Buy some crystals. Light some sage and call it a day.

But if you want real power—not the power to dominate others, but the power to transform yourself, to heal your community, to walk in relationship with conscious forces that have existed since before humans had names for them—then keep reading.

The path is open. The Zemí are watching. Your ancestors are waiting to see if you will honor them with your commitment or disappoint them with your half-measures.

Which will it be?

## The Taíno Lineage: Deepening Your Connection

In the core Temple of Gu teachings, you learned that the Taíno Lineage grants you the gift of Spirit of the Zemí—the ability to ask a simple yes-or-no question of a stone, river, mountain, or other Zemí and receive an answer in the form of a single word that appears in your mind.

This is real. This works. I have used this gift countless times—standing before a stone, asking "Should I take this path?" and hearing in my mind, clear as a bell, a single word: Wait. Or Go. Or Trust.

But that is only the beginning. That is the foundation, the first step, the way you learn that the Zemí are not metaphor but actual conscious beings willing to communicate if you approach with respect.

Now I will teach you how to go deeper.

### The Three Expressions of Taíno Lineage

The Taíno people are not monolithic. We are not one island, one culture, one way of being. The Caribbean is vast—dozens of islands, each with its own character, its own history, its own relationship with the Zemí.

When you claim the Taíno Lineage, you must choose which expression of that lineage calls to you most strongly. Think of it like choosing a dialect, or a particular family recipe for the same fundamental dish. The core ingredients are the same, but the flavor, the emphasis, the way it sits on your tongue—these differ.

There are three primary expressions:

Borikén Style (Puerto Rico): This is the path of the rainforest and the mountain, of El Yunque's constant mist, of rivers that run year-round. Borikén Seekers are known for deep emotional intelligence, for working with water Zemí and plant Zemí, for healing practices that involve herbs and river baths. The Borikén style emphasizes Suuru (patience) and Iwa (integrity). When a Borikén Seeker asks the Zemí for guidance, they often receive answers related to emotional states, to timing, to the slow work of growth.

Quisqueya Style (Hispaniola—modern Haiti and Dominican Republic): This is the path of the two-faced island, where mountains meet plains, where sweet water rivers empty into the sea. Quisqueya Seekers are known for their work with dual forces—life and death, salt and fresh, mountain and coast. They are mediators, translators, bridge-walkers. The Quisqueya style emphasizes Oye (insight) and Foriti (perseverance). When a Quisqueya Seeker asks the Zemí for guidance, they often receive answers that reveal hidden connections, that show how opposites create wholeness.

Cuba Style: This is the path of the long island, the dragon stretched out between ocean and gulf. Cuba Seekers are known for their fierce independence, their warrior spirit, their refusal to be dominated. They work with fire Zemí and wind Zemí, with the spirits of tobacco and cane. The Cuba style emphasizes Foriti (perseverance) and Oye (insight). When a Cuba Seeker asks the Zemí for guidance, they often receive answers related to action, to courage, to what must be fought for and what must be defended.

These are not hard boundaries. A Borikén Seeker can learn from Quisqueya practices. A Cuba Seeker can work with water Zemí. But your primary expression shapes your instincts, your first responses, the lens through which you see the world.

Which calls to you?

Close your eyes. Breathe. Ask yourself: am I drawn to the rainforest's patient growth, to the meeting of opposites, or to the fierce protection of independence? The answer that rises first—before your thinking mind can justify or rationalize—that is your truth.

### Enhancing Spirit of the Zemí

Once you have chosen your expression, you can deepen your ability to communicate with the Zemí. The basic gift allows yes-or-no questions and single-word answers. But with practice, with relationship, with time, this ability grows.

Here is how it works:

At first, you can only ask the Zemí you are physically touching or standing before. You must have your hand on the stone, your feet in the river, your body pressed against the trunk of the tree. This is necessary. This is right. You are learning their language. You are building trust.

But as you practice—as you make regular offerings, as you show up consistently, as you demonstrate that you are not just using the Zemí for your own purposes but genuinely seeking relationship—the distance can grow.

After your first Breakthrough (when you complete your first Mystery and advance on your path), you can ask Zemí from across a room. You can stand at the edge of the forest and ask the forest spirit. You can look at a mountain from miles away and ask the mountain.

After your third Breakthrough, you can ask Zemí that you have previously established relationship with even when they are not physically present. If you have been visiting the same river for months, making offerings, introducing yourself, learning its moods—you can close your eyes in your home and ask that river spirit a question, and they will answer. This is not telepathy. This is relationship so strong that physical distance becomes irrelevant.

After your fifth Breakthrough, the single-word answers can expand to short phrases—three to five words that convey more nuance. Instead of just "Wait," you might hear "Wait for rain first." Instead of just "Go," you might hear "Go, but bring tobacco."

And after your tenth Breakthrough—if you reach that level of mastery, if you walk this path that long and that deep—you can have full conversations with the Zemí. Not always. Not casually. But in moments of great need or great ceremony, you can ask complex questions and receive detailed guidance, whole sentences, complete instructions.

This is the gift of the Taíno Lineage. This is what we offer: not domination over the spirit world, but communication with it. Not power to command, but relationship that allows collaboration.

The Zemí do not serve us. We do not serve them. We work together. We are partners in the great dance of keeping the world in balance.

### The Obligation of the Lineage
But here is what you must understand: with this gift comes responsibility.

The Zemí are not vending machines. You cannot just walk up, ask your question, get your answer, and leave. That is transactional. That is extractive. That is the colonial mindset wearing spiritual clothing.

If you are going to walk the Taíno path, you must commit to Ebo—to reciprocity, to giving back, to maintaining relationship.

This means:

Regular offerings. Not just when you need something. Not just when you're in crisis. But consistently, rhythmically, as part of your practice. Tobacco at minimum. Cassava bread when you can. Rum on special occasions. Time and presence always.

Listening as much as asking. The Zemí will speak to you even when you're not asking questions—through dreams, through signs, through the sudden appearance of a bird or the unexpected sound of wind. Pay attention. Notice. Respond.

Protecting what is sacred. If a river spirit tells you that a particular stretch of water is hurting, you have an obligation to try to help—whether that means picking up trash, advocating against pollution, or simply bearing witness to the damage and offering comfort to the spirit.

Teaching others. The Taíno path is not a secret tradition reserved for the worthy. It is a living tradition that needs to be passed on. If you receive gifts from the Zemí, you have a responsibility to share what you've learned with those who come after you.

This is not burden. This is honor. This is what it means to be Taíno—to be part of an unbroken chain of people who have walked this land, who have spoken to these spirits, who have kept the relationships alive for thousands of years.

You are not separate from that chain. You are a link in it. Act accordingly.

## The Four Sacred Callings

Now we come to the Archetypes—the paths of service, the callings that shape how you walk through the world and what gifts you develop along the way.

In the core Temple of Gu teachings, you learned about ten general Archetypes: Healer, Chronicler, Seer, Mediator, Artisan, Warrior-Poet, Code-Smith, Bridge-Walker, Truth-Speaker, Dream-Weaver.

These are real. These are valid. Any of them can be walked within the Taíno Lineage.

But there are also four Archetypes that are specifically Taíno—callings that emerge from our particular culture, our particular relationship with the Zemí, our particular understanding of what it means to serve.

These four are: the Bohíque, the Naboría, the Guatiao, and the Caracol Keeper.

You do not have to choose one of these. You could be a Taíno Healer. A Taíno Seer. A Taíno Code-Smith working with silicon spirits while honoring Yúcahu in your code.

But if one of these four calls to you—if you read the description and something in your chest says yes, that, that is what I am meant to do—then listen to that voice. It is the Zemí speaking through your own knowing.

### The Bohíque: Walker Between Worlds

The Bohíque is the priest-shaman of the Taíno people. Not a ruler. Not a commander. Not someone who stands above the community demanding obedience. But someone who stands between—between the human and spirit worlds, between the living and the dead, between what is known and what is mysterious.

The Bohíque serves by:

Healing the sick. Not just physically—though herbs and massage and practical medicine are part of the work—but spiritually. The Bohíque can see when sickness is caused by Imbalance, by broken relationship with Zemí, by unprocessed grief or rage or fear. The Bohíque addresses the root, not just the symptom.

Guiding the dead. When someone dies, the Bohíque performs the three-day vigil, the tobacco offerings, the songs that help the spirit find its way to Coaybay. The Bohíque is the one who speaks to Opiyel-Guobiran, the dog spirit, and asks for safe passage. The Bohíque is the one who ensures that the dead do not linger as restless ghosts but complete their journey.

Consulting the Zemí. When the community faces a major decision—where to build, when to plant, whether to go to war—the Bohíque takes cohoba, enters vision state, and asks the great Zemí for guidance. This is not casual work. This is dangerous, difficult, sometimes terrifying work. But it is necessary.

Training the next generation. The Bohíque teaches apprentices—those who show the calling, the sensitivity, the willingness to do the hard work. This teaching is not gentle. It is rigorous, demanding, sometimes harsh. Because the work is real and the consequences of doing it poorly are severe.

The Bohíque does not seek power. The Bohíque seeks service. And through that service, power comes—not as something grasped, but as something given by the Zemí in recognition of faithfulness.

#### The Bohíque's Initiation Path

If you choose to walk the path of the Bohíque, this is what you will learn:

**Starting Practices (available immediately):**

Tobacco Blessing: You can consecrate tobacco for ceremonial use. This tobacco, when offered, is more potent—the Zemí recognize it as coming from trained hands. When you offer blessed tobacco, you may ask the Guide to give you +1 to your roll on any Ritual or Ebo that uses tobacco. This does not cost anything. This is simply recognition of your training.

Spirit Diagnosis: When you are treating someone who is sick or struggling, you can spend time in quiet observation—watching their energy, listening to their words, feeling the air around them—and ask the Guide: "Is this sickness spiritual, physical, or both?" The Guide will tell you honestly. This allows you to know whether herbs will help or whether deeper spiritual work is needed.

**First Breakthrough Practice (learned when you complete your first Mystery):**

Cohoba Preparation: You can now prepare cohoba—the vision powder—for yourself and others. This is advanced work. Dangerous work.
