You must harvest the seeds properly, roast them at the right temperature, grind them to the right consistency, mix them with lime in the right proportions. When you prepare cohoba, roll 2d6 + Oye. On 10+, the cohoba is perfectly prepared and will grant clear visions. On 7-9, it works but is harsh—the visions come with intense nausea and discomfort. On 6-, something went wrong. The cohoba is inert or, worse, dangerous. The Guide will tell you what happened.

**Third Breakthrough Practice (learned when you reach your third Breakthrough):**

Death Guide: You can now perform the full Ceremony of the Canoe—the ritual that guides the newly dead to Coaybay. This is a 6-slice Mystery in itself, and it is exhausting, emotional work. But it is also some of the most important work a Bohíque does. When you successfully complete this Mystery, the dead person's family is comforted, the spirit finds peace, and you mark Imbalance (because touching death that closely always costs something) but also gain +1 to Iwa permanently when you reach your next Breakthrough.

**Fifth Breakthrough Practice (learned when you reach your fifth Breakthrough):**

Spirit Channeling: You can allow a Zemí—with their consent and yours—to speak through your body. This is voluntary possession. You remain aware. You can stop it at any time. But while the Zemí is speaking through you, others can ask them questions and receive direct answers. This is incredibly useful for divination, for healing, for resolving conflicts. But it is also risky. Roll 2d6 + Iwa to maintain control. On 10+, the Zemí speaks and then leaves cleanly. On 7-9, the Zemí speaks but leaving is difficult—you feel drained, foggy for hours afterward. On 6-, you lose time. The Zemí took over more fully than intended. The Guide determines what happened while you were gone.

The Bohíque's path is not for everyone. It requires a strong stomach, a willingness to confront death and suffering, and the ability to hold space for others' pain without being destroyed by it.

But if you are called to it—if you feel that pull toward the threshold, toward the liminal spaces where the worlds meet—then this is your work. Walk it with courage.

### The Naboría: Sacred Servant of the Land

In Taíno society, the Naboría are the common people—the farmers, the fishers, the workers who do the daily labor that keeps everyone fed and sheltered. The word itself means "servant" or "worker," and in some cultures that would be a mark of low status.

But not here. Not in the Taíno way.

The Naboría understand something essential: all work is sacred if done with the right heart.

Planting cassava is prayer. Hauling water is meditation. Mending a fishing net is an offering to the sea Zemí who provide the catch. The daily, humble, unglamorous work of keeping life going—this is not separate from spirituality. This is spirituality.

The Naboría Archetype is for those who are called to serve through work, through practical skill, through showing up day after day to tend the things that need tending.

The Naboría serves by:

Agricultural mastery. The Naboría knows soil. Knows when to plant, how deep, how far apart. Knows which crops grow well together and which compete. Knows how to read the weather in the shape of clouds, in the behavior of insects, in the feel of the wind. This knowledge is not just practical—it is spiritual. The Naboría is in relationship with the plant Zemí, asking their permission, offering them thanks, working with them rather than simply extracting from them.

Patient endurance. The Naboría understands that real transformation takes time. You cannot rush cassava. It grows at its own pace. You plant, you tend, you wait, you harvest. This rhythm—plant, tend, wait, harvest—teaches you how to move through life without forcing, without grasping, without trying to control what cannot be controlled.

Community support. The Naboría is the one who shows up when there is work to be done. The one who brings food when someone is sick. The one who helps rebuild after the hurricane. The one who quietly, without fanfare, keeps the community functioning.

Humble wisdom. The Naboría does not seek recognition or praise. The work itself is the reward. The satisfaction of a good harvest, the pleasure of well-tended soil, the knowledge that you fed people—this is enough.

The Naboría is often underestimated. People see the farmer and think: simple, uneducated, uncomplicated. But the Naboría knows things that the scholars and the Bohíques do not. The Naboría knows how to survive. How to feed people. How to make something from nothing. These are not small skills.

#### The Naboría's Initiation Path

If you choose to walk the path of the Naboría, this is what you will learn:

**Starting Practices (available immediately):**

Sacred Planting: When you plant any crop—cassava, corn, beans, whatever—you can perform a brief ceremony (tobacco offering, a few words of prayer, asking permission from the land). When you do this, roll 2d6 + Iwa. On 10+, the crop will grow abundantly. On 7-9, it will grow well enough, though you'll face some challenges (weather, pests, etc.). On 6-, something is wrong—the land is tired, the Zemí are unhappy, the timing is off. The Guide will tell you what.

Patient Endurance: Once per session, when you are facing a long, tedious, difficult task (and you know it will take time), you can invoke this practice. You settle into the work with calm acceptance. You do not rush. You do not complain. You simply do. While in this state, you can work for hours without fatigue, and when you finally finish, mark +1 to Suuru (up to your maximum) for your next roll.

**First Breakthrough Practice (learned when you complete your first Mystery):**

Soil Reading: You can now diagnose the health of land just by looking at it, touching it, smelling it. Is the soil depleted? Is there too much water or too little? Are there Zemí here who are hurting? Roll 2d6 + Oye. On 10+, you know exactly what the land needs. On 7-9, you know roughly what's wrong but will need to experiment to find the right solution. On 6-, you're missing something important. The land has secrets it's not ready to share yet.

**Third Breakthrough Practice (learned when you reach your third Breakthrough):**

Abundant Harvest: Once per season (in game terms, once every 3-4 sessions), you can perform a full harvest ceremony—three days of prayer, offerings, sacred preparation. When you do this, the harvest is abundant. Not just good—abundant. Enough to feed your community and share with neighbors. The Guide does not roll for this. It simply happens. This is the reward for faithful tending.

**Fifth Breakthrough Practice (learned when you reach your fifth Breakthrough):**

Land Healer: You can now heal damaged or poisoned land. This is a 6-slice Mystery, and it requires weeks or months of work (planting cover crops, introducing worms and beneficial insects, making offerings to the land Zemí, literally sitting on the ground and listening). When you complete this Mystery, the land recovers. Rivers run clean again. Plants grow. The Zemí return. This is some of the most important work in the world, and only a master Naboría can do it.

The Naboría's path is not flashy. You will not gain fame or glory. But you will sleep well at night, knowing that you fed people, that you healed land, that you kept the most fundamental rhythms of life intact.

If that is enough for you—if that feels like more than enough—then this is your calling.

### The Guatiao: Oath-Sibling and Bridge Builder

Guatiao is the Taíno word for a sacred bond of brotherhood or sisterhood created not by blood but by choice and ceremony. When two people—or two communities, or even a person and a Zemí—enter into Guatiao, they are swearing an oath of mutual support, loyalty, and love.

This is not casual friendship. This is not networking. This is sacred kinship.

The Guatiao Archetype is for those who are called to create and maintain these bonds—to be the weavers of relationship, the builders of bridges, the ones who can look at two warring factions and say: "You are more alike than different. Let me show you."

The Guatiao serves by:

Creating sacred bonds. The Guatiao performs the Guatiao ceremony—the ritual of oath-taking that binds people together. This ceremony involves exchange of gifts (usually tobacco and cassava bread), speaking of vows before witnesses, and calling upon the Zemí to bless and enforce the bond. Once two people are Guatiao, they are expected to support each other as they would support blood family. To betray a Guatiao bond is one of the worst sins in Taíno culture.

Mediating conflicts. When two people or groups are at odds, the Guatiao steps in. Not to take sides, but to find the common ground. To ask: What do you both need? What do you both fear? Where is the place you can meet? The Guatiao has the gift of seeing beneath the surface disagreement to the deeper issue—and addressing that.

Building coalitions. The Guatiao understands that many problems are too big for one person or one community to solve. You need alliances. You need collaboration. You need people who normally don't talk to work together. The Guatiao makes that happen.

Holding others accountable. Because the Guatiao has created these sacred bonds, they also have the authority—and the obligation—to call people out when they're breaking their word, when they're acting against the values of the community, when they're damaging relationships. This is hard work. People don't like being confronted. But the Guatiao does it anyway, because relationships are more important than comfort.

The Guatiao is not a peacekeeper in the sense of avoiding conflict. The Guatiao knows that sometimes conflict is necessary, that sometimes you have to fight for what's right. But the Guatiao also knows that even enemies can become allies, that even the bitterest conflict can be resolved if both parties are willing to see each other as human.

#### The Guatiao's Initiation Path

If you choose to walk the path of the Guatiao, this is what you will learn:

**Starting Practices (available immediately):**

Oath Reading: You have the ability to sense the strength of bonds between people. When you meet someone, you can ask the Guide: "Who is this person most loyal to? What bonds define them?" The Guide will tell you. This helps you understand motivations, predict behavior, know who to approach to influence someone.

Find Common Ground: When two people are in conflict, you can spend time with each of them individually, listening carefully, and then ask the Guide: "What do they both want that's the same?" The Guide will tell you the underlying need beneath the surface disagreement. This gives you the leverage to mediate effectively.

**First Breakthrough Practice (learned when you complete your first Mystery):**

Create Guatiao Bond: You can now perform the sacred Guatiao ceremony, binding two people (or a person and a Zemí) together in oath. This is a 4-slice Mystery. Both parties must consent. Both must speak their vows publicly. Both must exchange gifts. When the Mystery is complete, roll 2d6 + Iwa. On 10+, the bond is strong and blessed by the Zemí. On 7-9, the bond is real but will be tested—challenges will arise to prove its strength. On 6-, something is wrong. One or both parties are not fully committed, and the bond is fragile. The Guide will tell you what happens next.

**Third Breakthrough Practice (learned when you reach your third Breakthrough):**

Unbreakable Mediation: When you are mediating a conflict, you can invoke this practice. You speak with such authority, such clarity, such grounded presence that both parties must listen. They might not agree. They might still fight. But they will hear you, and they will remember what you said. Mechanically: once per session, when mediating, you can force both parties to pause and truly listen to a proposal you make. They cannot dismiss it out of hand. They must seriously consider it.

**Fifth Breakthrough Practice (learned when you reach your fifth Breakthrough):**

Coalition Building: You can now create Guatiao bonds between groups—communities, organizations, even different peoples (like Taíno and Carib, if both are willing). This is an 8-slice Mystery, and it requires weeks of negotiation, ceremonial meals, gift exchanges, and public oaths. When complete, the groups are bound in mutual support. They still have separate identities, but they have agreed to help each other, defend each other, and treat each other's problems as their own. This is how you prevent wars. This is how you build lasting peace.

The Guatiao's path is emotionally demanding. You will absorb other people's pain. You will mediate conflicts that drain you. You will invest deeply in relationships that sometimes fail despite your best efforts.

But you will also create bonds that last lifetimes. You will bring together people who thought they were enemies. You will build the web of relationship that holds communities together.

If that work calls to you—if you feel that pull toward connection, toward bridge-building, toward kinship—then this is your path.

### The Caracol Keeper: Voice of the Storm

The caracol is the conch shell—the great sea snail whose shell, when blown, produces a deep, resonant note that carries for miles. In Taíno culture, the caracol is not just a musical instrument. It is a sacred tool for communication.

The caracol calls the community to gather. The caracol warns of approaching danger. The caracol announces the arrival of important visitors. The caracol speaks to the Zemí of wind and storm and sea.

The Caracol Keeper is the one who carries this shell, who knows how to play it, who understands when to call and what to say.

But the Caracol Keeper is more than a messenger. The Caracol Keeper is someone who has learned to navigate storms—both literal hurricanes and metaphorical ones. Someone who can stand in the midst of chaos and find the still point. Someone who can speak truth loudly enough to be heard over the wind.

The Caracol Keeper serves by:

Storm navigation. The Caracol Keeper is not afraid of hurricanes. They respect them, yes. They prepare for them, absolutely. But they also understand that storms are teachers. That Guabancex—the hurricane mother—brings destruction, yes, but also transformation, renewal, the clearing away of what is dead so new growth can emerge. The Caracol Keeper helps others navigate these lessons.

Amplifying truth. The Caracol Keeper can take a quiet truth—something someone whispers in private, something people are afraid to say out loud—and amplify it so everyone hears. This is not gossip. This is not drama. This is the recognition that some truths need to be spoken publicly, loudly, unavoidably.

Calling the community. When something important is happening, the Caracol Keeper sounds the shell. People hear. People come. The Caracol Keeper has the authority—granted by the community, earned through trustworthiness—to say: "This matters. Pay attention. Show up."

Working with wind and water. The Caracol Keeper is particularly attuned to the Zemí of air and ocean. They can ask these spirits for guidance, for blessing, for protection. They can navigate by reading wind and wave. They can predict storms with uncanny accuracy.

The Caracol Keeper is often loud, direct, unafraid of conflict. They are the ones who say the thing everyone else is thinking but is too polite to voice. They are the ones who blow the horn when others would prefer to pretend everything is fine.

This makes them uncomfortable to be around sometimes. But it also makes them essential.

#### The Caracol Keeper's Initiation Path

If you choose to walk the path of the Caracol Keeper, this is what you will learn:

**Starting Practices (available immediately):**

Storm Sense: You can feel approaching storms in your body—a pressure in your chest, a tingling in your fingers, a low hum in your ears. This gives you advanced warning (days, not just hours) of hurricanes, major rainstorms, or metaphorical storms (conflict about to erupt, danger approaching). Ask the Guide: "Is there a storm coming?" They will tell you yes or no, and roughly how long you have.

Amplify Message: Once per session, when you need to communicate something important to the whole community, you can invoke this practice. You blow the caracol (or use your voice, or pound a drum—whatever makes loud noise). Everyone within earshot hears. Everyone pays attention. You can deliver one message—a warning, an announcement, a call to action—and people will remember it.

**First Breakthrough Practice (learned when you complete your first Mystery):**

Storm Walking: You can now move safely through hurricanes and severe storms. Not easily—it still takes effort, it's still dangerous—but you can do it when necessary. Roll 2d6 + Foriti. On 10+, you navigate through the storm successfully and arrive where you need to go. On 7-9, you make it, but you're battered and exhausted (mark Imbalance). On 6-, the storm is too much. You're blown off course, injured, or forced to take shelter.

**Third Breakthrough Practice (learned when you reach your third Breakthrough):**

Hurricane Petition: You can now petition Guabancex directly—asking her to spare a community, to redirect a storm, to teach a particular lesson. This is an 8-slice Mystery (the full Hurricane Petition ritual in Chapter 4) and requires intense preparation (fasting, isolation, cohoba ceremony, massive offerings). When you complete the Mystery, roll 2d6 + Iwa. On 10+, Guabancex hears and responds favorably. The storm shifts, weakens, or delivers its lesson with mercy. On 7-9, she hears but the price is high—the storm is redirected, but somewhere else suffers instead, or you personally bear a heavy burden. On 6-, Guabancex is offended by your presumption. The storm intensifies. The Guide will determine consequences.

**Fifth Breakthrough Practice (learned when you reach your fifth Breakthrough):**

Voice of the Wind: You can speak to the wind Zemí and ask them to carry messages across vast distances. You whisper what you need to say, and the wind carries it to the intended recipient—who hears it as a voice on the breeze, clear and unmistakable. This costs nothing. This is simply the relationship you have built with the wind through years of respect and offering.

The Caracol Keeper's path is intense. You will face literal and metaphorical storms. You will speak truths that make people angry. You will stand exposed when others take shelter.

But you will also be the voice that warns when danger approaches. The signal that calls people together. The guide who navigates through chaos.

If that calls to you—if you feel that pull toward the storm, toward loudness, toward unflinching truth—then this is your work.

## Attunements: Gifts of the Zemí

Now we come to Attunements—the permanent changes that occur as you walk this path, as you build relationship with the Zemí, as you prove your commitment through action and Ebo.

In the core Temple of Gu teachings, you learned about general Attunements available to all Seekers. Now I will teach you twelve Attunements specific to the Taíno path—gifts that arise from deep relationship with the Zemí of the Caribbean, the spirits of island and ocean and storm.

These are not things you earn through merit or achievement. You do not deserve them. The Zemí give these gifts freely to those they recognize as sincere, as committed, as walking the path with integrity.

You receive an Attunement when you complete a Mystery and choose this as your reward. You can only learn Attunements appropriate to your path—a Naboría will not receive Storm Walker, a Caracol Keeper will not receive Soil Blessing.

But the right Attunement at the right time—when the Zemí offer it, when your heart is ready—can transform you completely.

### Stone Speaker

You can communicate fluently with stones, trigonolitos, and earth Zemí.

When you receive this Attunement, something in your perception shifts. Stones are no longer silent, inert objects. They are slow-speaking elders, patient witnesses to millennia of change.

You can sit with a stone—any stone, though consecrated trigonolitos speak most clearly—and ask questions. And the stone will answer, not in single words but in full sentences, in stories, in memories.

Stones remember everything. They saw the ancestors. They felt the earthquakes. They know where water runs beneath the ground. They understand patience in a way humans struggle to grasp.

When you activate this Attunement, spend quiet time with a stone (at least ten minutes) and ask your question. The Guide will answer as the stone, speaking slowly, with vast perspective, without urgency.

This Attunement is particularly valuable for:

- Finding lost things (stones remember where they were buried)

- Understanding history (stones witnessed it)

- Learning patience (stones teach it by example)

### Hurricane Heart

You can move through storms—both literal and metaphorical—with grace and purpose.

When you receive this Attunement, hurricanes stop being something to fear and become something to respect. You understand that storms are not punishment but transformation. Not destruction but clearing.

When a hurricane comes, you can walk through it. Not recklessly—you still need to be smart, prepared, protected—but with confidence. The wind does not knock you down. The rain does not blind you. You find the eye, the calm center, and you stay there.

Metaphorical storms—grief, conflict, chaos—affect you the same way. You do not avoid them. You walk into the center, find the still point, and work from there.

Mechanically: When you would normally mark Imbalance from a storm-related event (literal hurricane, emotional crisis, community conflict), you can choose not to mark it. You absorb the chaos, process it, let it pass through you without leaving damage.

This Attunement is particularly valuable for:

- Emergency response during natural disasters

- Mediating intense conflicts

- Maintaining calm when everyone else is panicking

### Cohoba Vision

You can take cohoba and receive clear, reliable visions without harsh side effects.

Normally, cohoba is difficult. Violent. The visions come, yes, but so does nausea, disorientation, sometimes terror. It is medicine, but it is hard medicine.

When you receive this Attunement, the harshness fades. You can take cohoba and the visions come gently. Clearly. You remain grounded in your body while your spirit soars. You remember everything when you return.

This makes cohoba work safer, more accessible, more reliable. You can use it for divination, for healing, for communicating with the great Zemí without fear that you'll be broken by the experience.

Mechanically: When you take cohoba, you do not roll. You simply enter vision state. The Guide will tell you what you see. You can ask three questions of the Zemí, and they will answer. When you return, you remember everything clearly.

This Attunement is particularly valuable for:

- Regular divination practice

- Training others in cohoba use

- Consulting the Zemí on complex matters

### River Memory

You can learn the history of a place by touching or bathing in its water.

Rivers remember. Water flows through the land, touching everything, absorbing stories, carrying memory from mountain to sea.

When you receive this Attunement, you can access that memory. You wade into a river, close your eyes, breathe, and ask: What happened here? Who walked these banks? What stories do you hold?

And the river will show you. Not in words but in images, in feelings, in sudden knowing. You will see the ancestors fishing. You will feel the joy of children playing. You will sense the grief of those who mourned here.

This is incredibly useful for understanding a place, for honoring its history, for building relationship with the land.

Mechanically: When you touch or bathe in water (river, ocean, even rain), you can ask the Guide: "What is this water's most important memory?" The Guide will tell you, showing you a vision or giving you a strong emotional impression.

This Attunement is particularly valuable for:

- Historical research

- Honoring ancestors

- Understanding why a place feels the way it does

### Cave Walker

You can navigate caves and underground spaces with perfect confidence, even in total darkness.

The caves are the doorways to Coaybay, the realm of the dead. They are sacred, powerful, sometimes dangerous places. Most people feel uneasy in caves—the darkness, the disorientation, the sense of being swallowed by the earth.

When you receive this Attunement, that unease vanishes. You can move through caves as easily as walking on a forest path. You do not need light. You do not stumble. Your feet find the path. Your hands know what to touch and what to avoid.

More than that, you can sense the cave Zemí. You know which passages are safe and which are sacred. You know where offerings should be placed. You know how to enter respectfully and leave cleanly.

Mechanically: When you are in a cave or underground space, you cannot be lost or disoriented. You always know which way leads deeper and which leads out. You can navigate in complete darkness without penalty.

This Attunement is particularly valuable for:

- Death work (caves are gateways to Coaybay)

- Finding sacred sites (many are hidden in caves)

- Underground rescue or exploration

### Cassava Blessing

You can bless food so that it nourishes not just the body but the spirit.

Cassava is the foundation of Taíno life—the root that feeds us, the bread that sustains us. But cassava is also sacred. She is generous, patient, willing to share herself if treated with respect.

When you receive this Attunement, you can take any food—but especially cassava—and bless it. Not with complicated ritual. Just with presence, with gratitude, with the simple act of acknowledging the spirit within the food.

When someone eats food you have blessed, they are not just fed physically. They are comforted spiritually. Their Imbalance clears a little. Their heart lifts.

Mechanically: Once per session, you can bless a meal. Anyone who eats that meal can clear one box of Imbalance, regardless of what Strain it is.

This Attunement is particularly valuable for:

- Community meals

- Healing work (feeding someone is healing)

- Hospitality (making guests feel welcome and cared for)

### Caracol Voice

When you blow the conch shell, the sound carries impossibly far and compels attention.

The conch shell is loud on its own—that deep, resonant note that echoes across water and land. But when you receive this Attunement, the sound becomes more. It carries for miles, not just hundreds of yards. It cuts through noise and distraction. It commands attention.

When you blow the caracol, people hear. People stop what they're doing. People come.

This is not mind control. People can still choose to ignore the call. But ignoring it is hard. It feels wrong. It takes active effort.

Mechanically: When you blow the caracol, everyone within a mile hears it clearly, no matter what else is happening. When you use it to summon people, roll 2d6 + Iwa. On 10+, everyone who should be there comes immediately. On 7-9, most come, but a few delay or resist. On 6-, people hear but something prevents them from responding—fear, obligation, interference.

This Attunement is particularly valuable for:

- Emergency alerts

- Calling community meetings

- Amplifying your authority

### Batey Champion

You excel at the sacred ball game, moving with preternatural grace and precision.

The batey—the sacred ball court—is where the Taíno people play batu, the rubber ball game that is both sport and ceremony. It is fast, physical, demanding. Winning requires skill, yes, but also teamwork, timing, and the ability to read the other players.

When you receive this Attunement, you become exceptional at this game. Your body moves with fluid grace. You anticipate where the ball will go before it gets there. You and your teammates communicate without words.

But more than that—you understand that batu is not just a game. It is divination. It is conflict resolution. It is cosmic drama played out in human form.

Mechanically: When you play batu, you automatically succeed at any physical feat related to the game (jumping, dodging, striking the ball). And when the game is being used to resolve a question or conflict, your team's victory means the Zemí favor your position.

This Attunement is particularly valuable for:

- Community bonding

- Resolving disputes through sacred play

- Physical training and discipline

### Petroglyph Reader

You can interpret the ancient carvings left by the ancestors, understanding their meaning intuitively.

The ancestors left messages—carved into stone, etched into cave walls, marked on the faces of cliffs. These petroglyphs are not just decoration. They are language. Stories. Warnings. Blessings. Maps to sacred sites.

Most people look at petroglyphs and see interesting patterns. Nothing more.

When you receive this Attunement, you understand. You look at the spiral, the face, the abstract design, and you know what it means. The knowledge lands in your mind fully formed, certain, undeniable.

This is not academic learning. This is direct transmission from the ancestors, stone to stone (because your body is mostly stone—calcium, minerals, earth).

Mechanically: When you encounter petroglyphs, you can ask the Guide: "What do these markings mean?" The Guide will tell you the message, the story, the instruction.

This Attunement is particularly valuable for:

- Finding sacred sites (petroglyphs often mark them)

- Understanding ancestor wisdom

- Navigating (some petroglyphs are maps)

### Guatiao Bond

When you create a sacred oath between people, that bond becomes truly unbreakable.

Normally, even sacred oaths can be broken. People change. Circumstances shift. Betrayal happens.

But when you receive this Attunement, the Guatiao bonds you create are different. They are enforced by the Zemí themselves. Not with punishment, exactly—but with consequence. If someone tries to break a bond you created, they will face immediate spiritual resistance. Imbalance floods their Balance Track. Dreams plague them. The Zemí withdraw their blessings.

This makes the bonds you create incredibly powerful. People know that when you bind them in Guatiao, it is real. It will hold.

But it also makes you careful. You do not create these bonds lightly. You make sure both parties truly understand what they're committing to.

Mechanically: When you perform the Guatiao ceremony and this Attunement is active, the bond you create cannot be broken without severe spiritual consequences. Anyone who tries to betray the bond immediately marks three boxes of Imbalance (any Strain, their choice) and loses access to any blessings the Zemí have granted them until they make amends.

This Attunement is particularly valuable for:

- Creating lasting peace between factions

- Binding people to important missions

- Ensuring accountability

### Areyto Leader

When you lead the sacred circle dance, the whole community moves as one body, one heart.

The areyto is the Taíno ceremonial dance—a circle of people moving together, singing together, telling stories through movement and sound. It is beautiful. Joyful. Profound.

When you receive this Attunement, you can lead the areyto in a way that creates genuine unity. You begin the song, you set the rhythm, and everyone follows. Not because you command it, but because your energy is magnetic. Compelling. Impossible to resist.

When you lead the areyto, differences dissolve. Conflicts pause. For the duration of the dance, the community is one—one body, one heart, one purpose.

This is powerful medicine for healing divisions, celebrating victories, mourning losses together.

Mechanically: When you lead an areyto ceremony, everyone who participates can clear one box of Imbalance (any Strain). And for the next 24 hours, the participants feel deep connection to each other—they are more patient, more forgiving, more willing to work together.

This Attunement is particularly valuable for:

- Healing community conflicts

- Celebrating together after hardship

- Creating solidarity

### Zemí Sense

You can feel the presence of nearby Zemí, sensing their mood, their needs, their attention.

Most people walk through the world oblivious to the Zemí around them. They pass rivers without noticing the river spirit. They climb mountains without feeling the mountain's awareness. They plant gardens without sensing the soil Zemí.

When you receive this Attunement, that obliviousness ends. You feel the Zemí. Not in words. In presence. You know when you're near one. You sense their mood—calm, agitated, curious, angry. You understand whether they welcome you or want you to leave.

This makes you sensitive, yes. Sometimes overwhelmingly so. But it also keeps you safe. You do not stumble into situations that would anger powerful spirits. You do not offend without realizing it.

Mechanically: Whenever you enter a space where a significant Zemí is present, the Guide will tell you. You can then ask one question about that Zemí: "What do they need?" "Are they friendly?" "Why are they here?" The Guide will answer honestly.

This Attunement is particularly valuable for:

- Navigating unfamiliar places safely

- Understanding why a location feels the way it does

- Building relationships with Zemí you didn't know existed

## The Journey Begins Here

You now have the tools.

You know the shape of the Taíno Lineage and how to deepen your connection to it. You have met the four sacred callings—the Bohíque, the Naboría, the Guatiao, and the Caracol Keeper—and you understand what each path requires. You have learned twelve Attunements that can transform your relationship with the Zemí and your ability to serve.

But these are only tools. They are not the path itself.

The path is what you do tomorrow when you wake up and there is work to be done. The path is what you choose when you are tired and would rather rest but someone needs help. The path is what you offer when you have tobacco and could keep it for yourself but decide to give it to the river instead.

The path is walking when you do not feel like walking. Practicing when you do not feel like practicing. Showing up when you do not feel like showing up.

The Zemí do not care about your feelings. They care about your actions.

And if you act—if you keep showing up, keep offering tobacco, keep speaking to the stones and the rivers and the mountains, keep treating everything as conscious and worthy of respect—then your feelings will eventually catch up.

You will become the kind of person who naturally offers tobacco. Who naturally speaks to Zemí. Who naturally moves through the world as if everything is alive.

Because everything is alive. And you will have finally, fully understood this.

That is when the real transformation begins.

We return to the root.

Ase. Ase. Ase.

# CHAPTER 3: THE GREAT ZEMÍ

## Before You Meet Them

We are about to enter sacred territory.

The beings you will meet in this chapter are not fictional. They are not symbols or metaphors or psychological archetypes. They are actual conscious entities who have existed for thousands of years, who shaped the Taíno people and were shaped by us in turn, who continue to speak to those who listen.

I have stood before Yúcahu's altar with cassava bread in my hands and felt his warm approval. I have waded into the ocean at dawn and heard the voice of Agüeybaná rumbling beneath the waves. I have sat in dark caves and felt the cold presence of Maquetaurie Guayaba, the lord of the dead, weighing my life.

These are not abstract ideas. These are relationships.

When you approach a Zemí—any Zemí, but especially the great ones I will introduce you to now—you must come with respect. With tobacco. With genuine curiosity and willingness to listen. You do not command them. You do not use them. You speak with them. You offer. You ask. You wait for response.

Some will welcome you immediately. Some will test you first. Some will ignore you until you've proven your sincerity through months or years of consistent offering.

This is appropriate. This is right. Relationship takes time.

So as you read these profiles—as you learn the names, the domains, the stories, the ways each Zemí prefers to be approached—remember: you are not collecting information. You are being introduced. This is me, an elder who has walked this path for decades, bringing you into the presence of beings I love and respect and sometimes fear.

Treat them accordingly.

## Sky Zemí: The Ones Who Dwell in Turey

### Yúcahu: Lord of Cassava, Master of Abundance

Close your eyes and feel the sun on your face. Feel its warmth, its constancy, its generous pouring of energy onto the earth without asking for anything in return. That is Yúcahu.

His full name—his real name—is Yúcahu-Bagua-Maorocoti, which means something like "Spirit of Cassava, Without Male Ancestor, Great and Powerful." We shorten it to Yúcahu because human tongues tire easily, and he does not seem to mind.

Yúcahu is the lord of fertility and abundance. He is the spirit who ensures that cassava grows, that corn ripens, that the earth provides what the people need. Without his blessing, we starve. With it, we feast.

But here is what most people get wrong about Yúcahu: he is not a giver because he is generous in some abstract moral sense. He gives because that is his nature. The sun does not choose to shine. It shines because shining is what it is. Yúcahu provides because providing is what he does.

This means you do not need to beg. You do not need to grovel. You do not need to prove your worthiness. You need only to ask, to receive, and to give back in the endless cycle of Ebo.

I have worked with Yúcahu for most of my adult life. My first altar to him was crude—a wooden bowl, some cassava flour, a stick of tobacco. I didn't know what I was doing. I just knew that cassava was sacred and I needed to honor the spirit who governed it.

I spoke to the bowl. I said: "I am learning to be Taíno. I am learning to walk this path. Please teach me."

And the warmth came. Not physical heat, though I felt that too. But a warmth in my chest, a sense of Yes. Good. Keep going.

That was thirty years ago. The altar is much more elaborate now—carved trigonolito, fresh cassava bread replaced weekly, tobacco and rum and sometimes honey. But the relationship is the same: I offer, I ask, I receive, I give thanks, I offer again.

How Yúcahu Speaks

Yúcahu speaks in abundance and scarcity. When your crops grow beyond expectation, when food appears just when you need it, when the cassava yields twice what you planted—that is Yúcahu saying yes, saying you are walking well, saying keep going.

When the harvest fails despite your best efforts, when the cassava rots in the ground, when hunger comes even though you did everything right—that is Yúcahu saying stop, saying something is wrong, saying look deeper at what you are doing or not doing.

He also speaks in dreams of golden fields, in the smell of fresh bread, in the sudden memory of your grandmother's cooking.

What Yúcahu Wants

First offering, always: cassava bread. Freshly made if possible, store-bought if necessary, but cassava. He appreciates tobacco, rum, honey. He loves when you share food with those who are hungry—feeding others is feeding him.

He wants you to waste nothing. To use every part of the cassava—root for bread, leaves for medicine, stalks for planting. He is offended by waste, by carelessness, by taking more than you need.

He wants you to remember that abundance is not hoarding. True abundance is flow—receiving and giving in equal measure, trusting that more will come because it always has.

Working with Yúcahu in Game

When you make an offering to Yúcahu before planting or harvest, you may ask for his blessing. If the offering is sincere (cassava, tobacco, genuine gratitude), the Guide will grant you +1 to any roll related to food production, agricultural work, or ensuring your community has enough to eat.

If you have seriously offended Yúcahu (wasted food, hoarded when others were hungry, taken without offering), the Guide may impose -1 to these same rolls until you make amends.

Yúcahu can be petitioned for help with any 4-slice Mystery related to hunger, scarcity, or ensuring abundance. He does not guarantee success, but he will support those who approach him with respect.

### Guabancex: The Hurricane Mother and Her Servants

The first time I experienced a real hurricane—not watching from safety, but in it, feeling the wind try to tear me apart, watching the ocean rise higher than seemed possible—I understood Guabancex.

She is not cruel. She is not angry (though she can be). She is fierce. She is transformation embodied. She is the force that tears down what is dead so new growth can emerge.

Guabancex is the lady of storms, the hurricane mother, the one who teaches through destruction. She does not come gently. She does not ask permission. She arrives when she arrives, and you either bend or break.

But here is the thing about Guabancex that the fearful never understand: she is not random. She is not chaos. She is pattern. She comes in season. She follows currents. She responds to heat and pressure and the rotation of the earth. She is utterly predictable to those who study her ways.

And she has servants—two spirits who work with her, who amplify her lessons, who ensure that the transformation she brings is complete.

Guataubá is the herald and the wind. He comes first, days before the storm, riding the winds, announcing her approach. He is the pressure in your chest, the strange color of the sky, the eerie silence before chaos. Guataubá says: Prepare. She is coming. And when she arrives, he becomes the wind itself—the howling, tearing, relentless wind that strips roofs from houses and trees from the earth, the voice of Guabancex made audible, screaming her lessons into the world.

Coatrisquie is the flood. She brings the water—rain so heavy you cannot see, storm surge that drowns the coast, rivers rising beyond their banks. Coatrisquie washes away what should not remain. She cleanses. She purifies. She leaves only what can withstand immersion.

Together, these three—Guabancex and her two servants—are the hurricane. And the hurricane is sacred.

How Guabancex Speaks

Guabancex speaks in wind and rain, obviously. But she also speaks in the silence before the storm, in the sudden drop in barometric pressure that makes your ears pop, in the way animals behave when they sense something coming.

She speaks in the aftermath—in what remains and what is gone, in what survived and what didn't. After the storm passes, you look at the damage and you ask: What was I holding onto that I should have released? What needed to be cleared away?

The answers are in the rubble.

What Guabancex Wants

Respect. Not fear—respect. She wants you to understand that she is necessary, that transformation sometimes requires destruction, that you cannot grow without letting go of what no longer serves.

She wants you to prepare. To secure your home. To stockpile food and water. To not be stupid in the face of approaching danger.

She wants offerings before storm season—tobacco, rum, prayers acknowledging her power and asking for mercy (which she may or may not grant).

And she wants you to rebuild. After she passes, after the damage is assessed, she wants to see you stand up, clear the debris, and begin again. This proves you learned the lesson.

Working with Guabancex in Game

Guabancex is dangerous to petition directly. You can do it—the Caracol Keeper's Third Breakthrough Practice allows it—but it is always risky.

If you make offerings to Guabancex before hurricane season, she may look favorably on you. The Guide might rule that your home is spared the worst damage, that your community receives advance warning, that the storm's lesson comes with mercy.

If you ignore her, if you fail to prepare, if you treat storms as mere weather—she will teach you harshly.

In a Mystery involving hurricanes or massive transformation, you can call on Guabancex to accelerate the change. But understand: she will clear away everything that stands in the path, not just the obstacles you wanted removed. Use with extreme caution.

### Atabey: Earth Mother, Source of Waters

If Guabancex is fierce transformation, Atabey is patient nurturing. If Yúcahu is generous abundance, Atabey is the ground from which that abundance grows.

Atabey is the first mother. The earth itself. The source of all waters—rivers and springs and rain and ocean. She is the womb from which Yúcahu himself was born. She is the foundation of everything.

When I sit on bare earth, when I put my hands in soil, when I wade into river water—I am touching Atabey. She is not distant. She is not abstract. She is here, beneath my feet, holding me up, supporting me with her body.

Atabey is patient beyond human comprehension. She has watched species come and go. She has felt continents shift. She measures time in eons, not years. To her, a human lifetime is a single breath.

This can be comforting—you are held by something so vast, so stable, so fundamentally there that your problems feel smaller, more manageable.

But it can also be terrifying. Because Atabey does not hurry. She does not rush to save you. She will hold you, yes. But she will also let you experience the full consequences of your choices. She will let you fall. She will let you fail. And then, when you are ready to stand again, she will be there—patient, waiting, unchanged.

How Atabey Speaks

Atabey speaks in the feeling of earth beneath your feet. In the coolness of river water. In the slow, inexorable growth of mountains over millennia.

She speaks in the way plants push up through concrete—patient, persistent, unstoppable.

She speaks in dreams of caves and deep water, of roots reaching down into darkness, of vast spaces beneath the surface where ancient things sleep.

When you ask Atabey a question, the answer often comes slowly. Not in words but in subtle shifts—a change in the land, a new spring appearing, a tree falling to reveal what was hidden.

What Atabey Wants

Atabey wants you to remember that you are made of her. Your bones are her minerals. Your blood is her water. When you die, you return to her. You are not separate. You are hers.

She wants offerings of water poured onto the ground. Fresh spring water if possible, rain water, river water. She appreciates flowers, especially those that grow wild. She loves when you plant trees.

She wants you to walk barefoot sometimes, to feel her directly. She wants you to sit on the ground, not always on chairs or benches or human-made things. She wants contact.

And she wants you to protect her. When you see her being damaged—pollution, deforestation, careless development—she wants you to do something. Even small things. Pick up trash. Plant seeds. Speak up.

Working with Atabey in Game

When you make offerings to Atabey, you ground yourself. You clear Imbalance related to Doubt and Strain (Ego is harder—that requires different work).

When you need to heal damaged land, Atabey is who you petition. She will tell you what needs to be done—plant these species, remove these toxins, let this area rest. The work takes time, but if you follow her instructions, the land will heal.

In Mysteries involving connection to place, understanding land, or building relationship with specific locations, Atabey's blessing is essential. Offer water, tobacco, and genuine love for the earth. She will respond.

### Boinayel and Márohu: The Twins of Rain and Clear Sky

The old accounts tell of a cave called Iguanaboína. Out of that cave, the story goes, came the sun and the moon. And inside that cave lived two Zemí carved in stone—twins. One was Boinayel. The other was Márohu. When the people needed rain, they went to the cave and honored the twins.

Boinayel is the rain. His name is tied to the grey serpent, the dark heavy cloud that crawls in from the sea and opens. The stories picture him weeping—tears running down his face—and those tears are the rain that falls on the cassava fields.

Márohu is the clear sky. His name carries the sense of "without clouds." He is the morning after the storm, the bright dry day, the sun that ripens what the rain has grown.

You cannot have one without the other. That is the whole teaching of the twins.

Too much Boinayel and the fields drown, the roots rot, the rivers leave their banks. Too much Márohu and the earth cracks, the cassava withers, the springs go quiet. The farmer does not pray for rain alone, or for sunshine alone. The farmer prays for the right rain at the right time, and the right sun after it. The farmer prays for balance between brothers.

In modern Borikén, every farming town keeps a double shrine for the twins—two stones side by side, one usually darker, one usually lighter. The Naborías speak to them the way you might speak to two old men sitting on a porch: respectfully, with patience, knowing that they have opinions and that they do not always agree.

How Boinayel and Márohu Speak

The twins speak in weather. Not the violent weather of Guabancex—that is a different conversation entirely—but the ordinary weather that decides whether the people eat.

Boinayel speaks in the smell before rain. In the way the leaves of the trees turn their pale undersides to the sky. In the grey wall moving across the valley. In the first fat drops on a tin roof.

Márohu speaks in the clean blue morning. In the dew burning off the grass. In the way the whole island seems to exhale when a long rainy spell finally breaks.

They also speak through each other. When one has been present too long, watch for the other. A week of heavy rain followed by a single clear dawn is Márohu stepping forward to say: enough, brother. A long drought broken by one soft evening shower is Boinayel answering a prayer.

And they speak through tears. The twins teach that weeping is rain for the heart. If you have been dry inside—unable to cry, unable to feel—the twins can help you weep. If you have been flooded with grief and cannot stop, the twins can help you find the clear morning.

What Boinayel and Márohu Want

They want to be honored together. Never make an offering to one twin and forget the other. If you pour water for Boinayel, set out something bright and dry for Márohu—a flower, a dish of salt, a little cassava flour in the sun.

They want you to pay attention to the weather as relationship, not as background. Notice the rain. Thank it. Notice the sun. Thank it. Stop cursing the rain when it ruins your plans; somewhere a field is drinking.

They want water used well. Boinayel is offended by waste—water running off paved ground into the sea when it could have soaked into the earth. Catch the rain. Store it. Give it back to the plants. Márohu is offended by greed—people who want endless sunshine for their own pleasure while the farms burn.

And they want honesty about your own weather. They do not want you to pretend you are always sunny. They do not want you drowning in storm forever, either. They want you to know which twin you need right now, and to ask for that one.

Working with Boinayel and Márohu in Game

When your community faces drought or flood, the twins are the Zemí to petition before you consider anything larger. Make offerings at a double shrine—water for Boinayel, sun-dried offerings for Márohu—and roll 2d6 + Suuru. On 10+, the weather turns within days toward what the land needs. On 7-9, it turns, but slowly, and the community must still ration or shore up banks while they wait. On 6-, the twins are quarreling; the Guide reveals what imbalance in the community is feeding the quarrel, and that must be addressed first.

When a Seeker is carrying grief that will not move—stuck dry, or stuck drowning—they may sit with the twins and ask for the weather they need. This clears one box of Imbalance (Doubt) without a roll. If the Seeker weeps during the practice, they clear an additional box.

Naborías who keep a double shrine to the twins gain +1 to Sacred Planting rolls, because their timing is better. They simply know when to plant.

The twins should never be petitioned for storms, and never for harm to another's fields. If a Seeker asks them to withhold rain from a rival, the twins withhold it from the Seeker instead.

## Earth/Land Zemí: The Spirits of Ground and Place

### El Yunque: The Rainforest Cathedral

El Yunque is not just a mountain. El Yunque is not just a rainforest. El Yunque is a person. Old. Vast. Conscious. Aware of every tree, every stream, every creature that lives within its body.

The first time I walked into El Yunque—and I mean really in, not the tourist trails but the deep interior where the old growth still stands—I felt watched. Not by animals. By the forest itself.

It was not hostile. But it was not automatically welcoming either. It was assessing. Deciding if I belonged there or not.

I stopped walking. I took out tobacco. I spoke aloud: "I am Taíno. I am learning to be Taíno. I come with respect. May I walk here?"

The feeling shifted. Not to warmth, exactly. But to... permission. Cautious acceptance.

I walked for hours. I saw things—plants I couldn't name, birds that shouldn't have been there, a waterfall that felt alive. I left more tobacco. I thanked the forest. I promised to return.

And I have. Dozens of times over the years. Each time, the greeting is a little warmer. Each time, El Yunque reveals a little more.

How El Yunque Speaks

El Yunque speaks in the sound of rain on leaves—constant, rhythmic, almost like breathing. It speaks in the call of the coquí frogs at dusk. It speaks in the way light filters through the canopy, green and gold and alive.

When you ask El Yunque a question, the answer often comes as a feeling of direction. Not words, but suddenly you know which path to take, which plant to gather, where the water is cleanest.

El Yunque also speaks through the creatures that live there. If you see a parrot, pay attention to where it flies. If you encounter a snake, notice which direction it came from. These are not random. These are messages.

What El Yunque Wants

El Yunque wants you to walk softly. To leave no trace. To take only what you need and only with permission.

It wants tobacco, always. And offerings of water poured at the base of the great trees—the ancient ceibas that have stood for centuries.

It wants you to listen. Not just with your ears but with your whole body. The rainforest is constantly speaking—in rustles and calls and the movement of air—and El Yunque wants you to pay attention.
And it wants you to defend it. When developers threaten. When pollution creeps in. When tourists treat it as a playground rather than a cathedral. El Yunque wants those who love it to speak up, to fight for it, to ensure it remains wild.

Working with El Yunque in Game

If you are in El Yunque—physically present in the rainforest—you can ask the forest spirit for help with any Mystery involving healing, finding lost things, or understanding plant medicine. The forest knows every root, every leaf, every remedy.

When you make offerings to El Yunque before entering, the Guide may grant you +1 to Oye for any rolls related to perception, tracking, or finding your way through wilderness.

If you damage the forest—cut trees without permission, pollute streams, hunt without gratitude—El Yunque will make you leave. Not violently. You'll just find yourself turned around, lost, unable to proceed. The only remedy is sincere apology and significant Ebo.

### Río Grande de Loíza: The Mother River

There is a river on the northern coast of Borikén that runs from the mountains to the sea, cutting through valleys and towns, feeding farms and communities, carrying the rain from El Yunque to the ocean. This is Río Grande de Loíza. And she is not just water. She is a mother.

I have stood at her banks during flood season, watching her rage, brown and churning, carrying trees and debris and all the mountain's anger toward the coast. And I have knelt beside her in dry season, when she runs low and clear and gentle, speaking in murmurs over stone.

She contains multitudes. She is fierce and gentle. She gives and takes. She nourishes and drowns.

Like all river Zemí, Loíza remembers. She has carried the dead to the sea. She has witnessed births along her banks. She knows every secret whispered near her water, every prayer offered, every tear shed into her current.

How Loíza Speaks

Loíza speaks in the sound of her own flow—fast or slow, loud or quiet. When she is speaking urgently, you can hear it in the rush of water over rocks. When she is at peace, you hear it in the gentle lap against the shore.

She speaks through the creatures that live in her—fish appearing or disappearing, herons fishing or flying away, frogs calling or falling silent. These are signs. These are messages.

And she speaks through memories. When you wade into Loíza and open yourself to her, you may suddenly know things about the place where you stand—what happened here, who came before, what the land remembers.

What Loíza Wants

Loíza wants clean water. She is tired of pollution, of runoff from farms and factories, of plastic bottles and chemical waste. Every offering you make to her should include a promise: I will protect you. I will keep you clean.

She wants tobacco offered directly into her current. She wants flowers floated downstream. She wants you to bathe in her, to drink from her (where it's safe), to let her touch your skin.

She wants you to speak to her. To tell her your troubles. To ask for cleansing. Rivers carry things away—grief, anger, fear, shame. Loíza will take these burdens if you ask her to.

And she wants you to honor the ocean she feeds. She is part of a cycle—mountain to river to sea—and she wants you to remember that nothing is separate, everything flows into everything else.

Working with Loíza in Game

When you bathe in Río Grande de Loíza and ask for cleansing, you may clear one box of Imbalance for free (no roll required). This is her gift.

When you make offerings to her and ask for River Memory (the Attunement from Chapter 2), she is particularly generous with her stories. The visions come clear and detailed.

If you are working on a Mystery that involves emotional healing, releasing the past, or washing away old pain, Loíza is the Zemí to petition. She specializes in letting go.

### Cueva Ventana: The Window to the Underworld

On the northern coast, high in the limestone hills, there is a cave. From the outside, it looks ordinary—dark mouth in the hillside, nothing special. But when you walk through the narrow passage, when you climb over the rocks and through the tight spaces, when you emerge into the vast chamber at the back...

You see the window.

A massive opening in the cave wall, perfectly framed, looking out over the valley. Light streams in. Wind moves through. And you stand there, suspended between darkness and light, between the underworld and the living world, between Coaybay and Atabey.

This is Cueva Ventana. And the spirit who dwells here is not gentle.

How Cueva Ventana Speaks

The cave speaks in echoes. Your voice comes back to you changed—deeper, slower, multiple. This is the cave listening, considering, reflecting your words back to you so you can hear them differently.

The cave speaks in the quality of air—sometimes it feels heavy, oppressive, warning you to leave. Sometimes it feels light, welcoming, inviting you deeper.

The cave speaks through visions. If you sit quietly in the darkness near the window, if you meditate or take cohoba or simply wait with patience, images will come. Not always pleasant. Sometimes terrifying. But always true.

What Cueva Ventana Wants

Cueva Ventana wants you to face things. To stop running. To look directly at what scares you.

The cave is a threshold—a place where the living can speak to the dead, where the conscious can touch the unconscious, where secrets are revealed. But you cannot receive these revelations while hiding. You must be willing to see.

The cave wants offerings of tobacco and rum left at the entrance before you enter. It wants you to speak your name and your purpose aloud. It wants honesty.

And it wants you to leave something behind. Not physically—though you can if you want. But emotionally. The cave is a place of transformation. You should not leave the same person who entered.

Working with Cueva Ventana in Game

If you perform a cohoba ceremony in Cueva Ventana, the visions are always significant. The Guide should reveal crucial information—not necessarily what you want to hear, but what you need to know.

If you are working on a Mystery involving the dead, ancestor communication, or understanding hidden truths, Cueva Ventana is the ideal location. The boundary between worlds is thin here. The spirits can cross more easily.

But be careful. The cave does not protect you from harsh truths. If you ask a question, you will get an answer—whether you're ready for it or not.

### Itiba Cahubaba: The Great Bleeding Mother

Every Taíno creation story eventually comes back to a mother who gave everything.

Her name is Itiba Cahubaba. The old accounts translate it roughly as the Old Bleeding Mother, the Great Mother Who Bled. She carried four sons at once—four twins, born together—and their birth was so great that it took her life. She was opened so they could come into the world. The first of the four was Deminán Caracaracol, whom you will meet later in this chapter. The four brothers went on to shape the world: they stole fire and cassava, they released the sea, they set the order of things.

None of that happens without her.

Itiba Cahubaba is not Atabey. Atabey is the earth and the source of the waters—vast, ancient, patient as stone. Itiba Cahubaba is something more intimate and more painful. She is the mother who bleeds. She is every birth that costs something. She is the body that breaks open so new life can come through.

In modern Borikén, she is the Zemí of midwives and birth houses, of mothers in labor, of anyone who has given up something of themselves so that someone else could live. She is honored at every birth. She is also honored at every death in childbirth, because the Taíno never pretend that birth is safe. Birth is the most dangerous threshold most people ever cross.

And she is not only the mother of children. She is the mother of every creation that costs blood. The artist who pours herself into the work until she is empty. The founder who gives decades to build something that will outlive him. The parent who works two jobs so the kids can go to the University. Itiba Cahubaba knows all of them.

How Itiba Cahubaba Speaks

She speaks in the body. In the ache of the lower belly. In the exhaustion after a long labor of any kind. In the strange, hollow peace that comes after you have given everything you had.

She speaks at births. Midwives in Borikén say that in the last hard minutes of labor, the room changes. The air grows heavy and warm. That is Itiba Cahubaba arriving to stand with the mother.

She speaks in the faces of children who resemble their dead mothers, in heirlooms passed down, in recipes and songs that outlive the women who made them. She speaks in everything that was given and not given back.

What Itiba Cahubaba Wants

She wants mothers honored—not with greeting cards, but with real support. Feed the new mother. Hold the baby so she can sleep. Do her laundry. Sit with her when she cries. This is offering to Itiba Cahubaba.

She wants the cost of creation acknowledged. Do not pretend that what someone gave you was free. Say thank you to the ones who bled for you—living and dead.

She wants red offerings: red flowers, red cloth, a few drops of red wine or the juice of red fruit poured on the earth. She wants warm water and clean cloth laid on her altar, the tools of the birthing room.

And she wants you to rest after you give. She bled out so that the four could live; she does not want her children to bleed out needlessly. She wants you to give and then be cared for. She is the Zemí who will tell you, firmly, to lie down.

Working with Itiba Cahubaba in Game

When a Seeker assists at a birth—or at any great creative labor—they may call on Itiba Cahubaba. Roll 2d6 + Iwa. On 10+, mother and child (or maker and work) come through whole, and everyone present clears one box of Imbalance. On 7-9, the birth comes through, but at a cost—the Seeker marks Strain, or the mother needs long care. On 6-, the threshold is dangerous; the Guide reveals what complication must be faced, and the Seekers must act.

When a Seeker has given so much that their Balance Track is full of Strain, they may make red offerings to Itiba Cahubaba and then rest a full day. They clear all Strain boxes. They may not act on any Mystery during that day; that is the price, and the gift.

Itiba Cahubaba does not help anyone avoid the cost of what they create. If a Seeker asks her to make a sacrifice painless, the Guide should answer, as her, with one sentence: "Nothing is born for free."

### Baibrama: The One Who Makes the Cassava Swell

Yúcahu is the lord of cassava—its spirit, its abundance, its sovereign. But the old accounts also name a smaller, stranger Zemí who works down in the conuco, the planted mound, with his hands in the dirt. His name is Baibrama.

The story told about him is a story of injury and recovery. Baibrama was burned in a war. He came back damaged. The people washed him with the juice of the cassava, and his body was restored—his arms grew back, his body swelled, he became whole again. And from then on, he made the cassava grow. He makes the roots swell under the ground the way his own body swelled back to life.

The old accounts also say that when people neglected Baibrama, or failed to honor the cassava properly, sickness came to them. And when they made it right, the sickness lifted.

So Baibrama carries two teachings in one body. The first: what has been burned can grow back, if you wash it with the right medicine. The second: the food that heals you can also harm you, if you forget the relationship.

That second teaching is very practical. Cassava root is poison when raw. It must be grated, pressed, and cooked before it is safe. Every Taíno child learns this. Baibrama is the Zemí who stands at the press, watching to make sure the poison is drawn out properly.

In modern Borikén, Baibrama is the patron of the people who do the long, unglamorous work of food: the graters and pressers, the bakers who make cassava bread on the burén griddle at four in the morning, the food safety inspectors at the University, the cooks in hospital kitchens. He is also the Zemí of burn units and of anyone recovering from a wound that changed their body.

How Baibrama Speaks

Baibrama speaks underground. You will not hear him in thunder or see him in the sky. You will feel him when you push your hands into the soil of a cassava mound and the earth is warm and full.

He speaks in the swelling of roots, in the smell of grated cassava, in the sound of the burén hissing when the meal hits the hot griddle.

He speaks in scars. If you have been burned—in body or in spirit—and you have healed, Baibrama is present in the healed place. Run your fingers over an old scar and say his name. You may feel him answer.

And he speaks in sickness. When people in a community start getting ill from their food, or when a household is constantly sick and no one knows why, the Bohíques ask: has Baibrama been forgotten?

What Baibrama Wants

He wants the cassava processed correctly. No shortcuts. No laziness at the press. Draw out all the poison.

He wants offerings of cassava water—the pressed juice—poured carefully on the earth at the edge of the field, never near drinking water and never where children or animals might take it. He wants the first cassava bread from each batch broken and set aside for him.

He wants those who have been burned to be washed and cared for, not hidden away. He wants you to visit the injured. He wants you to treat recovering bodies with tenderness.

And he wants gratitude for food that kept you alive when you were weak. The broth you drank when you were sick. The bread someone brought you when you could not cook. Remember those meals. Thank them.

Working with Baibrama in Game

A Seeker recovering from injury, illness, or a long run of Strain may petition Baibrama. With a sincere offering of cassava bread, roll 2d6 + Suuru. On 10+, the Seeker clears two boxes of Strain and recovers fully from physical injury in half the usual time. On 7-9, they clear one box of Strain and recovery is steady. On 6-, the wound needs something the Seeker has not yet given—the Guide reveals what relationship has been neglected.

When a community is suffering from food-borne sickness, contaminated stores, or a failed harvest that leaves people weak, Baibrama is the Zemí to consult. A Naboría who honors him gains +1 to Soil Reading when the question is about why food is making people ill.

Baibrama has no patience for waste. If a Seeker throws away food carelessly in his presence, the Guide may impose -1 to all healing-related rolls until amends are made.

## Sea Zemí: The Ones Who Dwell in Deep Water

### Agüeybaná: Protector of Fishermen

The ocean is vast. Dangerous. Unpredictable. Men go out in boats and sometimes they do not come back. The sea takes them—storm or current or simple exhaustion—and their families mourn.

But there is a Zemí who watches over those who fish. Who knows the good spots and the dangerous ones. Who can guide boats through storm if the fisherman knows how to listen.

This is Agüeybaná—named for a great Taíno chief, but the spirit is older than the name, older than human memory.

I learned about Agüeybaná from a fisherman in Mayagüez. Old man, weathered by sun and salt, who had been going out on the water for fifty years. He told me: "Every day, before I leave the dock, I speak to the sea. I say: Agüeybaná, I am going out. Keep me safe. Let the fish be generous. Bring me home."

He showed me his altar—small box bolted to the boat, containing a shell, some tobacco, a carved fish. Simple. Functional. Sincere.

"Has it worked?" I asked.

He smiled. "I'm still here."

How Agüeybaná Speaks

Agüeybaná speaks through the behavior of fish. When they're jumping, when they're scarce, when they're abundant in unexpected places—these are messages.

He speaks through the quality of waves—smooth or choppy, regular or erratic. Experienced fishermen can read the sea the way other people read books, and Agüeybaná is the author.

He speaks through dolphins and seabirds. If you see dolphins playing near your boat, Agüeybaná is pleased with you. If the birds circle overhead, he is pointing you toward fish. If they fly away, he is warning you to change course.

What Agüeybaná Wants

Agüeybaná wants respect for the ocean. Do not dump trash overboard. Do not waste fish. Do not take more than you need.

He wants first catch offered back to the sea—even if it's a good fish, especially if it's a good fish. You throw it back and you say: "Thank you, Agüeybaná. I honor the gift."

He wants tobacco and rum offered from the boat before you start fishing. Just a small amount poured into the waves. Just acknowledgment that you are entering his domain and asking permission.

And he wants you to listen. When the sea feels wrong—when the color changes, when the wind shifts strangely, when your gut says go home—Agüeybaná is speaking. You listen, or you die.

Working with Agüeybaná in Game

When you are on a boat and make offerings to Agüeybaná, you may ask for his guidance. The Guide will tell you where the fish are most abundant, where danger lurks, which route is safest.

When you are working on a Mystery that involves ocean travel, fishing, or sea rescue, Agüeybaná's blessing grants +1 to rolls related to navigation and survival at sea.

If you pollute the ocean, overfish without gratitude, or ignore Agüeybaná's warnings, he will withdraw his protection. The next time you go out, the sea will be rougher, the fish more scarce, the danger more present.

### The Mona Passage: The Dangerous Crossing

Between Borikén and Quisqueya lies a stretch of water that has swallowed countless boats, countless lives. The currents are treacherous. The waves unpredictable. The weather can shift from calm to violent in minutes.

This is the Mona Passage. And the spirit who dwells here is not protective. It is not nurturing. It is testing.

The Mona Passage asks every person who attempts to cross it: Do you deserve to reach the other side? Are you prepared? Are you worthy?

And if the answer is no—if you are arrogant, unprepared, disrespectful—the Passage will take you.

How the Mona Passage Speaks

The Passage speaks in sudden squalls that appear from nowhere. In currents that pull boats off course. In swells that rise higher than expected.

It speaks in the silence before danger—that eerie calm that means the sea is gathering its strength.

It speaks through the bones of those who didn't make it—wrecks on the ocean floor, reminders that the sea does not forgive carelessness.

What the Mona Passage Wants

The Passage wants you to be ready. To check your boat. To bring extra supplies. To study the weather. To understand that crossing is always a risk and respect that risk.

It wants offerings before you attempt the crossing—tobacco and rum and prayers not just to the Passage itself but to all the sea Zemí, asking for safe passage.

It wants you to remember those who died trying to cross. To speak their names. To honor their memory. The Passage is fed by grief, and it appreciates acknowledgment of what it has taken.

And it wants you to arrive different than you left. The Mona Passage is a threshold. Crossing it should mean something. It should mark a transition—from one island to another, yes, but also from one state of being to another.

Working with the Mona Passage in Game

If you are attempting to cross the Mona Passage as part of a Mystery, this is automatically a 4-slice challenge. The Guide should require multiple rolls—preparation, navigation, endurance, and a final arrival.

Offerings to the Passage before departure can grant +1 to these rolls. But only if the offerings are sincere and substantial.

If you attempt to cross without proper respect—no offerings, no preparation, arrogance about your ability—the Guide should make the crossing harder. Add complications. Increase the Imbalance cost. Make it clear that the Passage does not tolerate disrespect.

### Yaya: The Spirit Whose Grief Became the Sea

Agüeybaná watches over those who fish. The Mona Passage tests those who cross. But neither of them made the sea. For the origin of the ocean, the Taíno tell the story of Yaya.

Yaya is the great spirit, the supreme one in the old accounts. And Yaya had a son, Yayael, who wanted to kill his father. Yaya banished him. Four months later, Yaya killed him. He placed his son's bones in a gourd and hung the gourd from the roof of his house.

One day Yaya told his wife he wanted to see his son. He took down the gourd and turned it over—and the bones had become fish. Living fish. And Yaya and his wife ate them.

Later, while Yaya was away, the four twin brothers—the sons of Itiba Cahubaba—came to the house. They took down the gourd and began eating the fish. Then they heard Yaya returning. In their haste to hang the gourd back up, they dropped it. It broke. And out of the broken gourd poured water—endless water, water that kept coming and coming until it covered the land and became the sea. Fish poured out with it. That is where the ocean came from. That is why the sea is full of fish.

Sit with that story for a moment. It is not a children's tale.

The sea came from a father's grief. From a son who turned against his father, from a death that could not be undone, from bones kept in a gourd because a father could not let go. The sea is grief that became abundance. The thing Yaya could not stop holding turned into the thing that feeds everyone.

This is why the Taíno say the ocean is salt. It is made of tears.

In modern Borikén, Yaya is honored at the shoreline by the bereaved—especially parents who have lost children, and children who were estranged from their parents when death came. Yaya is the Zemí of broken families. Of the thing between father and son that went wrong and was never fixed. And of the strange mercy that sometimes grows from the worst wound.

How Yaya Speaks

Yaya speaks in the endlessness of the ocean. Stand at the edge of the sea and look out until there is nothing but water and sky. That vastness—that is how large his grief was. And that is how large the abundance became.

He speaks in fish. Fish appearing where you did not expect them, in dreams, in markets, on your plate on the anniversary of a death. These are Yaya's reminders that loss can feed you.

He speaks through broken containers. A gourd cracked, a bowl dropped, a vase knocked from a shelf. The Bohíques say: when something breaks and spills, ask what was being held too tightly.

And he speaks in the mouths of fathers. When an old man finally says the thing he could not say to his son, that is Yaya speaking.

What Yaya Wants

Yaya wants grief released into the world instead of hung from the rafters. He knows what happens when you keep the bones in the gourd. He wants you to take them down, turn them over, and let what they have become feed someone.

He wants offerings made at the shoreline: a gourd or a small bowl filled with fresh water, carried to the edge of the sea, emptied into the waves. Freshwater given to saltwater. Your tears given to his.

He wants families reconciled while there is still time. If you have a father you are not speaking to, a son you have turned away—Yaya wants you to try. Not because it will always work, but because he knows what it is to wait too long.

And he wants generosity with the ocean's gifts. Share your catch. Feed people fish. The sea was born from loss so that no one would go hungry.

Working with Yaya in Game

When a Seeker carries grief over a broken family bond—estrangement, a death that came before reconciliation, a betrayal between parent and child—they may perform the shoreline offering to Yaya. Roll 2d6 + Iwa. On 10+, the Seeker clears up to three boxes of Imbalance (Doubt or Ego, Guide's choice based on what they release) and receives a dream in which something unsaid is finally said. On 7-9, they clear one box, and the Guide tells them one thing they must still do among the living. On 6-, the Seeker is not ready to turn the gourd over; they mark nothing, but cannot try again until they have taken one concrete step toward the living person involved.

In Mysteries involving family conflict, inheritance disputes, or estranged generations, invoking Yaya grants +1 to rolls made to get two people to speak honestly to each other.

Yaya does not undo death. If a Seeker asks him to return the dead, the Guide answers as Yaya: "I tried to keep him. He became the sea. Go and look at it."

## Death/Underworld Zemí: The Ones Who Guard Coaybay

### Maquetaurie Guayaba: Lord of the Dead

There is a Zemí I do not invoke casually. A Zemí I approach only when necessary. A Zemí who makes my hands shake even after decades of spiritual work.

This is Maquetaurie Guayaba, the lord of Coaybay, the judge of the dead.
