When you die—when your breath stops and your heart stills—your spirit does not immediately rest. First, there is the three-day vigil. First, there is the journey through the threshold. And first, there is the meeting with Maquetaurie.

He asks you three questions:

How did you live?

What did you give?

What remains of you in the hearts of the living?

Your answers determine where you go. Deep rest for those who lived well. Lingering for those with unfinished business. Something else—something harder—for those who caused great harm.

I have only encountered Maquetaurie once, during a deep cohoba journey. I did not see him—the darkness was too complete. But I felt him. Cold. Patient. Utterly without mercy or compassion. Not cruel—simply neutral. Like a scale that weighs exactly what is placed on it, no more and no less.

How Maquetaurie Speaks

Maquetaurie speaks in the moment of death. In the final breath. In the last thought before darkness.

He speaks through the mouths of the dying—sometimes they say things in their final moments that are clearly not their own words. That is Maquetaurie, speaking through them, preparing them for what comes next.

He speaks in caves, especially deep caves where no light reaches. If you sit in that darkness long enough, you will hear his voice—not audible, but unmistakable. A presence that asks: Are you ready?

What Maquetaurie Wants

Maquetaurie wants you to live well. Not perfectly—he understands human imperfection. But well. With integrity. With care for others. With the understanding that you will one day stand before him and be weighed.

He wants the dead to be properly honored. Three-day vigils. Tobacco smoke to guide the way. Songs that help the spirit release from the body. When you do this work—when you help the dead cross—Maquetaurie notices. Appreciates.

He wants offerings in caves—tobacco, rum, sometimes food for the ancestors who dwell in Coaybay. These offerings are not for him directly. They are for the dead, to ease their rest.

And he wants you to remember that death is not the enemy. Death is transition. Death is rest. Death is the other side of life, equally necessary, equally sacred.

Working with Maquetaurie in Game

You do not petition Maquetaurie for favors. You petition him for permission—permission to speak with the dead, permission to enter Coaybay (spiritually), permission to guide a spirit across the threshold.

When performing the Death Guide practice (Bohíque Third Breakthrough), you are working directly with Maquetaurie. Offerings to him before beginning this work can make the crossing easier for the spirit you're guiding.

If you encounter Maquetaurie in a vision or dream, pay close attention to what he shows you. These are not random images. These are glimpses of your own death, of unfinished business, of what you need to address before you stand before him for real.

### Opiyel-Guobiran: The Dog Spirit Guardian

There is a dog that stands at the threshold between life and death. Not a physical dog—though sometimes it appears that way. A spirit dog. Ancient. Loyal. Fierce.

This is Opiyel-Guobiran, and its job is simple: guide the worthy across, keep the unworthy out.

When a person dies and begins the journey to Coaybay, Opiyel appears. If the person lived with integrity, if they are ready to cross, the dog guides them gently—walking ahead, looking back to make sure they're following, leading them through the darkness to the place of rest.

But if the person is not ready—if they have unfinished business, if they are clinging to life, if they died with rage or bitterness unresolved—Opiyel blocks the path. The spirit cannot cross until the issues are addressed, until someone from the living world helps them let go.

This is why death work is necessary. This is why Bohíques perform the three-day vigil. We are helping the dead satisfy Opiyel's requirements so the dog will let them pass.

How Opiyel Speaks

Opiyel speaks through actual dogs. If a dog appears during a funeral or vigil—especially if it's a dog no one recognizes, a stray that shows up and then vanishes—that is Opiyel making itself known.

The dog's behavior tells you things. If it sits calmly, the spirit is ready to cross. If it paces or whines, there is trouble. If it growls or barks, something is wrong—unfinished business, unresolved conflict, danger.

Opiyel also speaks in dreams. If you are grieving someone and you dream of a dog leading you somewhere, follow it. The dream is showing you what the dead person needs in order to rest.

What Opiyel Wants

Opiyel wants the dead to be honored. Vigils performed properly. Tobacco offered. Songs sung. The full ritual, done with care.

Opiyel wants offerings of food left for dogs—actual, physical dogs. Feed a stray. Leave scraps for the neighborhood pack. This honors Opiyel and earns its favor.

And Opiyel wants you to finish your business before you die. To resolve conflicts. To say what needs to be said. To make amends. So that when the time comes, when Opiyel appears before you, it can guide you across without hesitation.

Working with Opiyel in Game

When you are performing death work (guiding a spirit across the threshold), call on Opiyel first. Make offerings to dogs. Speak its name. Ask for help.

If you do this, the Guide should make the Mystery easier—fewer complications, clearer guidance, a stronger sense that you are supported in the work.

If you encounter Opiyel in a vision, pay attention to what it shows you. The dog is telling you about death—your own or someone else's—and what needs to be resolved.

### Corocote: The One Who Walks at Night

Maquetaurie Guayaba rules Coaybay. Opiyel-Guobiran guards the threshold. But there is a third Zemí of the night-side who belongs to neither the underworld nor the daylight. He belongs to the hours in between—to the dark when the living sleep and the dead go walking.

His name is Corocote.

The old accounts say Corocote was a Zemí kept in the house of a cacique named Guamaretos. When the house burned, Corocote escaped. He did not stay put in his altar the way a Zemí was expected to. He moved. At night, the stories say, he would leave his place and come down among the people, and in the morning he would be found somewhere else. He was restless. He went where he chose.

The accounts also say that Corocote marked people. Children born with two crowns on their heads—two whorls in the hair—were said to be touched by him, to carry his sign. Look at the back of a baby's head. Some have one spiral where the hair grows. A few have two. In Borikén, the grandmothers still say those children belong a little bit to Corocote.

That is who he is. The night visitor. The one who does not stay where he is put. The Zemí of what happens after dark—dreams, desire, secrets, the strange things people do when they think no one is watching.

He walks among the opía. The opía are the spirits of the dead who come out at night—the ones who walk the roads and the guava groves after sunset, who eat the guayaba fruit, who sometimes take human form and lie down beside the living. You can tell an opía, the old people say, because it has no navel. The dead were never born the way the living are born, not anymore. Reach for the belly in the dark. If there is no navel, you are lying with the dead.

Corocote is at home among them. He knows which opía are only lonely and which are dangerous. He knows which dreams are just dreams and which are visits.

How Corocote Speaks

Corocote speaks in dreams—especially the vivid ones that feel more real than waking life. Dreams of being visited. Dreams of walking roads you have never seen. Dreams that leave a scent in the room when you wake.

He speaks in the sounds of the house at night. The creak in the hallway. The door that was closed and is now open. The feeling of someone standing at the foot of the bed.

He speaks through things that move on their own. If you set a stone on your altar and in the morning it is somewhere else, Corocote has been walking.

And he speaks through desire. Corocote is not a gentle Zemí, and he is not a shy one. He knows what people want in the dark—what they will not admit in daylight. He does not shame it. He simply sees it.

What Corocote Wants

Corocote wants honesty about the night-side of your life. He wants you to stop pretending you have no secrets, no hungers, no dreams you would be embarrassed to repeat. He does not demand that you act on them. He wants you to know them.

He wants offerings left after dark: guava fruit, a cup of water, a lit candle or a low lamp near a window. Leave them out overnight and do not watch to see what happens to them.

He wants protection for those who sleep. Before bed, he wants the house tended—doors latched, a little tobacco smoke passed through the rooms, the names of the living spoken so the wandering dead know who lives here.

He wants children with two crowns treated kindly. They are his. They are often restless, often dreamers, often difficult to keep in one place. Let them wander a little.

And he wants respect for the opía. Do not mock the dead who walk at night. Do not invite them in carelessly. If one comes to you, speak to it gently and send it on its way.

Working with Corocote in Game

When a Seeker is plagued by night visitations—restless dead, disturbing dreams, objects moving in the house—Corocote is the Zemí to consult. Make offerings after dark and ask him one question about what is visiting. The Guide answers honestly, as Corocote: whether the visitor is an opía, a living person's wandering spirit, or something else, and what it wants.

A Seeker who keeps a nightly offering to Corocote for a full month may enter any dream deliberately once per session: name a person, sleep, and meet them in the dream. Roll 2d6 + Oye. On 10+, the meeting is clear and both remember it. On 7-9, the Seeker remembers it but the other person recalls only a feeling. On 6-, the Seeker wanders among the opía instead and marks one box of Doubt.

When a Seeker tries to unmask the dead in a Mystery—to tell whether a stranger is living or opía—Corocote's favor grants +1 to that roll.

Corocote will not help anyone enter another person's dreams to harm, frighten, or seduce them. If a Seeker tries, the Guide should turn the dream back on them: they spend the night lost in Corocote's roads and wake marked with two boxes of Ego.

## Animal/Nature Zemí: The Sacred Creatures

### The Coquí Collective: Voice of the Island

If you have ever been to Borikén, you know the sound. As soon as the sun sets, as darkness spreads across the land, you hear them:

Ko-kee. Ko-kee. Ko-kee.

Thousands of tiny frogs, each no bigger than your thumb, singing in unison. Not chaotic—coordinated. Like a chorus that never needs a conductor because every member knows the song by heart.

This is the Coquí Collective. And they are not just frogs. They are watchers. They are the island's alarm system, its early warning signal, its voice.

When the coquíes fall silent—when the sound that should be there is suddenly, horrifyingly absent—something is wrong. Danger is approaching. Listen to the silence.

How the Coquí Collective Speaks

Primarily, they speak through sound. When the chorus is strong and clear, the island is at peace. When it's sporadic or weak, something is stressing the ecosystem—pollution, drought, predators.

When it stops entirely, pay attention. Earthquake coming. Hurricane approaching. Fire nearby. The coquíes know before you do, and they warn.

They also speak through numbers. Abundance of frogs means the land is healthy. Scarcity means damage. If you're working on environmental healing, watch the coquí population. When they return, you know you're succeeding.

What the Coquí Collective Wants

Clean water. Healthy forest. Safe habitat. The coquíes are indicator species—they thrive when the ecosystem is balanced, suffer when it's not.

They want you to protect their homes. To fight against development that destroys forest. To keep streams clean. To maintain the wetlands where they breed.

They want you to listen. When they sing, appreciate it. When they fall silent, take it seriously.

And they want you to understand that they are not decoration. They are not background noise. They are sacred. They are Borikén's voice made audible.

Working with the Coquí Collective in Game

If you are in Borikén and you need to know if an area is safe or dangerous, listen to the coquíes. If they're singing normally, it's safe. If they're agitated or silent, danger is present.

When you are working on environmental Mysteries (healing land, addressing pollution, restoring habitat), the return of the coquí song is a sign of success. The Guide should use this as a marker—when you hear the frogs again, you know the Mystery is complete.

If you make offerings to protect the coquíes specifically—donating to conservation efforts, cleaning streams, planting native plants—you earn their favor. The next time you need a warning, the Guide should give it to you clearly.

### Iguana Elder: Teacher of Patience and Sun-Wisdom

On the rocks by the sea, you will see them. Old. Slow. Unmoving for hours. Basking in the sun, absorbing its heat, converting light into life.

These are iguanas. And the oldest ones—the ones whose skin is thick with age, whose eyes have seen decades pass—these are not just animals. These are teachers.

The Iguana Elder is not a single iguana but a collective consciousness of all the old ones, all the survivors, all those who have mastered the art of patience and presence.

How Iguana Elder Speaks

Through stillness. Through the simple act of being without needing to do.

If you sit near an old iguana and watch it, you will begin to understand. It does not rush. It does not fidget. It simply exists, fully present, fully alive, requiring nothing but sun and time.

This is the teaching: you do not always need to be productive. You do not always need to be moving toward a goal. Sometimes, you just need to be.

What Iguana Elder Wants

Respect for slowness. For rest. For the wisdom that comes not from action but from patience.

Iguana Elder wants you to leave space for stillness in your life. To not fill every moment with activity. To sit in the sun sometimes and do nothing.

Offerings are simple: leave fruit near where iguanas bask. Leave water during dry season. Protect their habitat from development.

Working with Iguana Elder in Game

When you are feeling rushed, pressured, overwhelmed—when Imbalance is building and you can't find a way to clear it—go sit with an iguana. Spend an hour doing nothing but watching it.

The Guide should allow you to clear one box of Imbalance (any Strain) after this practice. The stillness heals.

When you are working on a long-term Mystery (8+ slices), invoke Iguana Elder for patience. The Guide might grant you +1 to Suuru for all rolls related to this Mystery, recognizing that you have committed to the long path.

### Deminán Caracaracol and the Turtle Mother

Deminán Caracaracol was the first of the four twin sons of Itiba Cahubaba—the first one out of his mother's opened body, the leader of the four brothers. His name, Caracaracol, carries the sense of scabbed or rough-skinned. He was not beautiful. He was not polite. He was the kind of hero who gets things done by taking risks no one else will take.

It was Deminán and his brothers who broke Yaya's gourd and loosed the sea upon the world. And it was Deminán who went to the house of an old man named Bayamanaco to ask for cassava bread.

Bayamanaco had fire. Bayamanaco had cassava. Bayamanaco had cohoba. He had everything the brothers needed and did not yet know how to make. When Deminán came begging, the old man grew angry. He spat at Deminán—a wad of cohoba and tobacco spittle—and it struck him on the back.

Deminán's back began to swell. It swelled so large and so painful that he nearly died. His brothers tried everything. Finally they cut the swelling open with a stone axe.

And out came a turtle. A living female turtle.

The brothers built a house. They raised the turtle. And in the old accounts, from the turtle and the brothers came the next generation of the people. The Turtle Mother is the mother of us all.

Sit with how strange and how true that story is.

The hero goes to the elder asking for what he needs. The elder strikes him. The wound swells until it nearly kills him. And when the wound is finally opened, it contains new life. The thing that almost destroyed him becomes the mother of his people.

That is Deminán's teaching: sometimes the wound is pregnant.

In modern Borikén, Deminán is the Zemí of rough-edged people who do necessary things—activists, whistleblowers, the ones who go to the powerful and ask for what the people need and get spat on for it. The Turtle Mother is honored on the beaches when the sea turtles come ashore to lay their eggs. Turtle-watch volunteers on the north coast keep small shrines to her at their stations. Children are taught never to touch a nesting turtle: she is doing the holiest work in the world.

How Deminán and the Turtle Mother Speak

Deminán speaks in boldness. The sudden nerve to say what needs saying. The impulse to knock on the door you have been afraid of.

He speaks in swelling—in the body, in the community, in a situation. A conflict that keeps getting bigger. A pressure in your back between the shoulders. A problem no one will name. When something is swelling, Deminán asks: what is inside it?

The Turtle Mother speaks slowly. She speaks in sea turtles—seen from a boat, glimpsed while snorkeling, crawling up the sand at night. She speaks in nests, in hatchlings racing for the waves, in the long patience of a creature that returns to the beach where she was born to lay her eggs.

She speaks to anyone who carries something that has not yet been born. A project. A child. A new way of living. She says: you can carry it longer than you think.

What Deminán and the Turtle Mother Want

Deminán wants courage used for the people, not for ego. He went to Bayamanaco for cassava for everyone. He did not go to make himself famous. He wants you to ask hard things of the powerful—and to ask them on behalf of others.

He wants wounds opened carefully, by people who love you. His brothers cut him open; strangers did not. When something has been swelling in your life, find your brothers and sisters. Let them help.

The Turtle Mother wants the beaches protected. Lights off near the shore during nesting season. Trash picked up. Nests left alone. Offerings for her are made by doing the work: volunteering, guarding, cleaning.

She wants patience with what is gestating. Do not cut it open before its time. Do not dig up the nest to check on the eggs.

And both of them want you to remember that heroes are rough. Deminán was scabby, rude, and reckless. He was also the one who brought the people cassava and fire. Do not wait to become polished before you do the necessary thing.

Working with Deminán and the Turtle Mother in Game

When a Seeker must confront someone powerful—a cacique, a developer, a hostile elder—to ask for something their community needs, they may call on Deminán before going. Roll 2d6 + Foriti. On 10+, they speak with full courage and the powerful one must answer them seriously. On 7-9, they are heard, but there is a sting—the Seeker marks one box of Strain or Ego as the price of the encounter. On 6-, they are struck down in some way; the Guide reveals what the wound contains, and it should hold the seed of the next solution.

When a Mystery involves something that keeps growing worse and no one will look at it directly, invoking Deminán grants +1 to rolls to expose what is hidden inside.

The Turtle Mother blesses long work. When a Seeker commits to protecting something fragile across several sessions—a nesting beach, a pregnancy, a slow healing, a new community project—they may ask her blessing. For as long as they keep their commitment, they gain +1 Suuru to rolls tied to that work. If they abandon it, they mark two boxes of Doubt.

## Contemporary/Urban Zemí: The New Spirits

### San Juan Harbor: Where Waters Meet

The harbor is not natural. It was deepened, widened, shaped by human hands over centuries. Ships come and go. Goods are loaded and unloaded. People arrive from distant places and depart for new destinations.

And through all of this—through the commerce and the noise and the constant change—a spirit has emerged. Not ancient like the mountain or the river. New. Born from the meeting of land and sea and human activity.

This is the San Juan Harbor Zemí. And it is not what you might expect.

How San Juan Harbor Speaks

Through arrivals and departures. Pay attention to who shows up in your life and who leaves. The harbor teaches that everything is temporary, everything is in transit, nothing stays forever.

Through the mixing of cultures. The harbor is where Taíno meets African meets Spanish meets American meets everything else. The Zemí here understands hybridity, synthesis, the creation of something new from many sources.

Through opportunity and loss. Ships bring goods—wealth, possibility, new connections. But ships also take things away—resources, people, pieces of home. The harbor is both gift and theft, always.

What San Juan Harbor Wants

The harbor wants you to understand flow. To not cling. To let things come and go without trying to hold them forever.

It wants offerings thrown into the water—coins, flowers, tobacco. Gestures of gratitude for safe arrival or successful departure.

It wants you to welcome newcomers. The harbor is the first thing visitors see of Borikén. The Zemí here wants that first impression to be welcoming, generous, kind.

And it wants you to remember those who were taken. Enslaved people who passed through this harbor. Indigenous people displaced. The harbor holds grief alongside commerce, and it wants both acknowledged.

Working with San Juan Harbor in Game

When you are beginning a journey—literal travel or metaphorical transition—make offerings to the harbor Zemí. The Guide should grant safe passage, good connections, opportunities in the new place.

When you are working on Mysteries involving trade, cultural exchange, or bringing different groups together, the harbor Zemí can facilitate. It specializes in connection across difference.

If you are struggling with attachment—to a person, a place, a phase of life that needs to end—the harbor teaches letting go. Spend time there. Watch the ships leave. Learn.

### University Zemí: Spirit of Questions and Growth

The University of Borikén is relatively young—maybe three hundred years old, not ancient by Taíno standards. But in that time, so many people have studied there, taught there, discovered there, that a spirit has coalesced around the place.

This is the University Zemí. Spirit of inquiry. Spirit of growth. Spirit of the eternal student who understands that learning never ends.

How University Zemí Speaks

Through sudden insights. Through the "aha!" moment when everything clicks. Through the connection between seemingly unrelated ideas.

Through questions more than answers. The University Zemí doesn't give you solutions—it gives you better questions, deeper questions, questions that lead somewhere true.

Through the people you study with. When you meet a teacher who changes your life, when you encounter a book that shifts your understanding, when a conversation with a fellow student reveals something you didn't know you knew—that is the University Zemí at work.

What University Zemí Wants

The spirit wants you to remain curious. To never think you know everything. To always be willing to learn, to be corrected, to discover you were wrong about something.

It wants offerings of books—donate to libraries, leave books in public places, share knowledge freely.

It wants you to teach. Learning for its own sake is good, but learning that is shared, that multiplies through teaching—that is sacred.

And it wants you to honor mistakes. The University Zemí knows that you learn more from failure than success. When you mess up, when you get something wrong, when you have to start over—that is when real growth happens.

Working with University Zemí in Game

When you are researching something, studying a new skill, or trying to understand a complex problem, make offerings to the University Zemí. The Guide should give you a crucial insight, a useful connection, or access to information you wouldn't have found otherwise.

When you are working on Mysteries involving knowledge, research, or teaching, invoke this Zemí. It grants +1 to Oye for rolls related to understanding, analysis, and learning.

If you refuse to admit when you're wrong, if you cling to ignorance, if you dismiss teachers or learning opportunities—the University Zemí withdraws. Your research hits dead ends. Your studies don't stick. You learn the hard way that knowledge requires humility.

## The Final Teaching

You have now met twenty Zemí—sky and earth, sea and cave, animal and urban. Twenty spirits, twenty relationships, twenty paths to deeper understanding.

But remember what I told you at the beginning of this chapter: these are not fictional. These are not symbols.

These are real beings.

And real beings require real relationship. You cannot simply read about Yúcahu and expect his blessing. You must build an altar. Offer cassava. Speak to him regularly. Prove over months and years that you are sincere.

You cannot read about Guabancex and expect to survive hurricanes. You must respect her. Prepare. Make offerings. Understand that her lessons come whether you're ready or not, and the only question is whether you'll learn or be destroyed.

You cannot read about the Coquí Collective and expect to hear their warnings. You must protect their habitat. Listen to their song. Care about their survival as if your own life depends on it—because it does.

This chapter is an introduction. A series of doorways. But you still have to walk through them.

So here is my challenge to you:

Choose one Zemí from this chapter. Just one. The one that calls to you most strongly, that resonates most deeply, that feels most necessary for your path right now.

Build a small altar. It doesn't have to be elaborate—a shelf, a stone, some tobacco, a glass of water. That's enough to start.

Speak to that Zemí every day for one month. In the morning when you wake up. Or at night before bed. Just a few words: I am here. I am learning. I am listening.

And watch what happens.

Because the Zemí are not waiting for you to be perfect. They are not waiting for you to have it all figured out. They are waiting for you to show up. To be sincere. To commit.

Do that, and everything changes.

We return to the root.

Ase. Ase. Ase.

# CHAPTER 4: RITUALS OF BORIKÉN

## The Work Begins Here

You have learned the cosmology. You have met the Zemí. You understand the Archetypes and the Attunements. You know the shape of the Taíno world.

But knowledge without practice is like a seed that never touches soil. It cannot grow. It cannot transform. It remains potential, not reality.

This chapter is where theory becomes practice. Where understanding becomes experience. Where you learn to do the work of the Taíno path—not just think about it, not just talk about it, but actually perform the rituals that connect you to the Zemí, that honor the ancestors, that maintain the sacred balance between humans and spirits.

These are not symbolic gestures. These are not role-playing exercises. These are real spiritual practices that have been refined over generations, that work when performed with sincerity, that will change you if you let them.

I have organized them into what we call the Hymnal of the Zemí—fifteen rituals ranging from simple daily offerings to complex multi-day ceremonies. Each one serves a specific purpose. Each one requires specific preparation. Each one connects you to specific Zemí and teaches specific lessons.

After the rituals, I will teach you about Sacred Implements—physical objects consecrated for spiritual work. Tools that amplify your practice, that carry the blessing of the Zemí, that become partners in your journey.

But before we begin, I need you to understand something crucial:

Rituals work because of relationship, not because of technique.

You can perform every step perfectly—gather the right materials, speak the right words, make the right gestures—but if your heart is not present, if you are going through motions without genuine intention, if you are treating this as performance rather than prayer, the ritual will be empty.

The Zemí do not care about perfect execution. They care about sincere connection.

So as you read these instructions, as you prepare to practice them, keep asking yourself: Am I present? Am I sincere? Am I approaching this with respect?

If the answer is yes, then even if you make mistakes, even if you stumble through the words, even if you forget a step—the ritual will work. The Zemí will meet you. The transformation will happen.

Let's begin.

## THE HYMNAL OF THE ZEMÍ: Fifteen Sacred Practices

### 1. Tobacco Offering: The Fundamental Practice

Virtues Required: Iwa (integrity)

Complexity: 1-slice Mystery

Duration: 5-10 minutes

Best Performed: Daily, or before any other spiritual work

If you learn only one practice from this entire book, let it be this one.

Tobacco offering is the foundation of all Taíno spiritual work. It is the most basic way to say: I acknowledge you. I respect you. I am grateful.

You can offer tobacco to any Zemí—river, mountain, stone, tree, the great spirits like Yúcahu or Atabey. You can offer it before beginning a journey, before planting crops, before making a major decision. You can offer it in gratitude after receiving help or in petition when you need guidance.

Tobacco is the universal language. The Zemí understand it. They appreciate it. They respond to it.

**Preparation:**

Obtain real tobacco. Not cigarettes—those are full of chemicals and additives. Real tobacco leaf, dried and ready to use. You can buy it from tobacco shops, harvest it yourself if you grow it, or order it online from suppliers who work with Indigenous communities.

You will also need a method of offering:

- You can burn it (loose leaf in a bowl or wrapped as a small bundle)

- You can bury it at the base of a tree or in the earth

- You can place it in water (river, ocean, rain barrel)

- You can simply leave it on a stone or altar

Have matches or a lighter if you plan to burn it. Have a small bowl or cloth to hold it.

**The Practice:**

Stand or sit in a quiet place. If you are offering to a specific Zemí (a river, a stone, a particular spirit), be physically present at that location. If you are offering generally, you can do this anywhere—though outdoors is better than indoors.

Hold the tobacco in your hands. Feel its weight. Smell it. Acknowledge that this plant gave its life to become this offering.

Speak aloud. Say your name. Say who you are—your lineage if you know it, your path if you have one, simply "a human being walking the Taíno path" if that is all you can claim.

Then say what you are offering and why:

"I offer tobacco to \[name of Zemí or 'all the spirits of this place'\]. I come with respect. I come with gratitude. I ask for \[blessing/guidance/protection/whatever you need\]. I promise to \[walk this path with integrity/honor the gift/give back/whatever you are willing to commit to\]."

Then place the tobacco. Bury it, burn it, place it in water, set it on stone—whatever method you chose. As you do, breathe deeply. Feel the action complete. Know that the offering has been received.

If you are burning the tobacco, watch the smoke rise. This is your prayer made visible, your intention carried to Turey.

When the offering is complete, say: "Ase. Ase. Ase." (This is a Yoruba word meaning "so be it" or "may it be so"—we use it in Taíno practice because many of us are also initiated in African traditions, and the Zemí don't mind. But you can also say "Thank you" or simply bow in silence.)

Then walk away. The offering is done. Do not linger expecting immediate response. The Zemí will answer in their own time.

**What This Accomplishes:**

Tobacco offering establishes relationship. It says: I see you. I honor you. I am not separate from you.

Each time you offer tobacco, the relationship strengthens. The first offering is an introduction. The tenth is a conversation between acquaintances. The hundredth is communion between friends.

In game terms, offering tobacco before attempting any other ritual or Mystery grants you +1 to your first roll related to that work. This is not a mechanical bonus—this is recognition that you approached with proper respect, and the Zemí are more willing to help you.

**Common Mistakes:**

Offering cigarettes instead of real tobacco. The Zemí find this insulting. Would you offer a guest instant coffee when they asked for water from the spring?

Offering without speaking. Silence is sometimes appropriate, but when you are first building relationship, the Zemí need to hear your voice, your intention, your commitment.

Offering and then immediately asking for more. Tobacco offering is not transaction—I give you this, you give me that. It is relationship. Sometimes you offer and ask for nothing. Sometimes you offer and pour out your gratitude. Sometimes you offer and make a request. But if every offering is a request, the relationship becomes exploitative.
Forgetting to say who you are. The Zemí need to know who is making the offering. They need to hear your name, your voice, your presence. Do not hide behind anonymity.

### 2. River Blessing: Cleansing in Living Water

Virtues Required: Suuru (patience)

Complexity: 2-slice Mystery

Duration: 30 minutes to 1 hour

Best Performed: When you are carrying heaviness, when Imbalance is building, when you need emotional/spiritual cleansing

Rivers carry things away. This is their nature. Water flows from mountain to sea, and everything in its path moves with it—leaves and stones and silt and, if you let them, your burdens.

The River Blessing is a cleansing ceremony. You enter the water carrying grief, anger, fear, shame—whatever emotion is weighing you down. You speak to the river Zemí. You ask for help. And you let the water take what you no longer need to carry.

**Preparation:**

Find a river. Not just any moving water, but actual river—preferably one that flows year-round, one that has Zemí presence (you will feel this when you arrive). If there are no rivers near you, a stream will work. Even a waterfall, if you are brave.

Bring tobacco. Bring a small bundle of herbs if you have them (basil, rue, rosemary—cleansing herbs). Bring white flowers if possible. Bring a change of clothes because you will get wet.

Go to the river when you have time—this is not a rushed practice. Early morning is traditional, but any time when you will not be disturbed works.

**The Practice:**

Stand at the river's edge. Make tobacco offering first—introduce yourself to the river Zemí, say why you have come, ask permission to enter the water for cleansing.

Wait. Feel the response. If the river feels welcoming (the water seems to shimmer, the sound becomes inviting, you feel warmth in your chest), proceed. If the river feels closed (the water seems dark or angry, the sound harsh, you feel pushed away), do not enter. Come back another day.

If permission is granted, remove your shoes and outer layers. You can enter fully clothed or in minimal clothing—whatever feels right. But you must be wet. The water must touch your skin.

Wade into the river until you are at least knee-deep, waist-deep if possible. Face upstream (toward the source of the water, the mountains where it begins).

Speak to the river. Tell it what you are carrying. Be specific:

"River spirit, I am carrying grief for my friend who died. I am carrying anger at my boss who disrespected me. I am carrying fear that I am not enough."

Name every burden. Say them out loud. Let the river hear.

Then say: "I ask you to take these from me. I do not need to carry them anymore. I release them to you. Take them to the ocean. Transform them. Let them become something else."

Bend down. Cup water in your hands. Pour it over your head. Do this three times—once for body, once for mind, once for spirit.

Each time you pour, feel something lift. Feel the weight lessen. Feel the water carrying away what you released.

If you brought herbs, crush them in your hands and rub them on your skin—arms, chest, neck, face. The scent helps the release. Then wash the herbs into the river, letting them flow downstream.

If you brought flowers, place them in the water. Watch them float away. These are offerings of gratitude—the river is doing work for you, and you thank it with beauty.

Stand in the water for as long as you need. Some people stay five minutes. Some stay an hour. The river will tell you when it's done. You will feel lighter. You will feel clear. You will know.

When you are ready, wade back to shore. Do not towel off immediately—let the water dry on your skin naturally. Let the blessing soak in.

Before you leave, make one more tobacco offering. Say thank you. Promise to return.

**What This Accomplishes:**

River Blessing clears Imbalance. After completing this ritual, you may clear up to three boxes of Imbalance from your Balance Track. The Guide determines which Strain is cleared based on what you released (grief is usually Doubt, anger is usually Ego, fear is usually Doubt again).

More importantly, River Blessing teaches you that you do not have to carry everything forever. That there are forces in the world—rivers, Zemí, community—willing to help you bear the weight. That release is sacred. That letting go is strength, not weakness.

**Common Mistakes:**

Entering water that is unsafe. If the river is in flood, if the current is too strong, if you cannot see the bottom—do not enter. The ritual requires living water, not risking your life. Find a gentler place.

Rushing through the release. You must actually feel the emotions you are releasing. If you just name them intellectually without feeling them, the river cannot take them. Cry if you need to. Scream if you need to. Let the emotion move through you and into the water.

Forgetting gratitude. The river is doing you a service. The river Zemí is helping you. You must say thank you. You must make offering. Do not take this gift for granted.

### 3. Cassava Thanksgiving: Honoring the Harvest

Virtues Required: Iwa (integrity)

Complexity: 2-slice Mystery

Duration: 1-2 hours

Best Performed: After any harvest, or quarterly as general gratitude practice

Food is sacred. This should not be controversial, but modern industrial society has taught us to treat food as commodity—something purchased, consumed, discarded without thought.

The Taíno know better. Food is gift. Food is relationship. Food is the body of the earth offering itself so you can continue to live.

Cassava Thanksgiving is the practice of honoring this gift. You take one meal—ideally cassava bread, but any food you have grown or harvested yourself works—and you consecrate it. You transform it from mere fuel into sacrament.

**Preparation:**

Obtain cassava bread. If you can make it yourself from cassava root, even better. If not, buy it from a market—many Caribbean groceries carry it.

If cassava is truly unavailable, use bread made from any grain you have connection to. Cornbread works well. Rice cakes. Even wheat bread if that is what you have. The point is not the specific plant but the relationship to it.

Set a table or altar space. This should be beautiful—clean cloth, flowers if you have them, candles. You are making space for the Zemí to join you in this meal.

Invite people if you want—Cassava Thanksgiving can be done alone or in community. But everyone present must understand this is ceremony, not just dinner.

Have tobacco ready for offering. Have rum or water for libation.

**The Practice:**

Begin with tobacco offering to Yúcahu, the lord of cassava and abundance. Say:

"Yúcahu, master of the growing things, source of all that feeds us—I thank you for this food. I thank you for the sun that grew it, the soil that held it, the rain that watered it, the hands that harvested it. I recognize this as gift. I promise not to waste it. I promise to share it. I promise to give back."

Pour rum or water on the ground or into a bowl as libation. This is for the earth, for the plant spirits, for all the beings who collaborated to create this food.

Then hold the cassava bread (or whatever food you are blessing). Really look at it. Smell it. Feel its texture. Think about everything that had to happen for this food to exist—the planting, the tending, the harvesting, the processing, the cooking. Think about all the beings, human and more-than-human, who contributed.

Say aloud:

"This is not just food. This is gift. This is relationship. This is the earth offering herself so I can live another day. I receive this with gratitude. I receive this with humility. I receive this with joy."

Break the bread. Take a piece for yourself. Place a piece on the altar as offering to the Zemí. Give pieces to everyone present.

Eat slowly. Taste every bite. Do not rush. Do not talk excessively. Let the eating be meditation.

When the meal is complete, speak gratitude again:

"Thank you, Yúcahu. Thank you, cassava spirit. Thank you, earth. Thank you, sun and rain. Thank you to all who made this possible. I am fed. I am grateful. I am blessed."

Leave the offering piece on the altar overnight. In the morning, return it to the earth—bury it, or leave it for animals.

**What This Accomplishes:**

Cassava Thanksgiving transforms your relationship to food. It reminds you that eating is not just biological necessity but spiritual practice.

In game terms, anyone who participates in this ceremony can clear one box of Imbalance (any Strain). The gratitude, the slowness, the recognition of gift—these heal.

Additionally, if you are a Naboría working with agriculture, performing Cassava Thanksgiving at the end of a growing season grants +1 to your next planting roll. The plant spirits remember. They appreciate. They give back.

**Common Mistakes:**

Rushing through the meal. This is not fast food. This is ceremony. If you cannot slow down, if you cannot give the food proper attention, wait until you can.

Forgetting to share. Even if you are alone, you must offer a portion to the Zemí and the earth. Food is meant to circulate, not be hoarded.

Performing this ritual while angry or bitter. Gratitude must be genuine. If you are in a state where you cannot feel real thankfulness, do a different practice first (River Blessing to clear the bitterness, then return to this).

### 4. Calling the Cohoba: Vision Journey

Virtues Required: Oye (insight)

Complexity: 4-slice Mystery

Duration: 3-6 hours

Best Performed: When you need guidance on a major decision, when you are stuck and cannot see the path forward

Cohoba is not recreational. Cohoba is not "getting high" in the casual modern sense. Cohoba is serious medicine—powerful, dangerous, transformative medicine that opens the boundary between worlds and allows you to see what is normally hidden.

When you take cohoba properly, your spirit rises to Turey. You speak with the great Zemí. You receive visions that guide your path. You return changed.

But cohoba does not give pleasant experiences. It gives true experiences. And truth is often harsh.

This ritual should not be attempted by beginners. You need training. You need a guide—ideally a Bohíque who knows this work, but at minimum someone experienced who can hold space for you and keep you grounded.

I am including it here because it is fundamental to Taíno practice. But I am also warning you: do not do this casually. Do not do this alone the first time. Do not do this without proper preparation.

**Preparation:**

First, obtain cohoba. This is made from the seeds of Anadenanthera peregrina or Anadenanthera colubrina trees. The seeds must be harvested, roasted, ground into powder, mixed with lime (from crushed shells) to activate the alkaloids.

If you do not know how to do this, find a Bohíque who does. If there are no Bohíques near you, find someone in the Ayahuasca or plant medicine community who understands sacred snuff preparations. Do not guess. Done wrong, cohoba is at best ineffective and at worst dangerous.

Second, prepare yourself. Fast for at least 12 hours before the ceremony. No food, only water. Your body needs to be clean. Your mind needs to be clear.

Third, prepare the space. This should be indoors, quiet, safe. Have a cohoba stool if possible (a low carved wooden seat—traditional, but a cushion on the floor works). Have tobacco for offering. Have a bucket nearby because nausea is common. Have water for after. Have someone to watch over you.

Set intention. Why are you taking this journey? What question do you need answered? What guidance do you seek? Write this down. Speak it aloud. Be clear.

**The Practice:**

Begin with tobacco offering. Call to the Zemí you wish to speak with—Yúcahu if seeking abundance, Guabancex if seeking transformation, Maquetaurie if seeking death wisdom, your personal Zemí if you have one.

Say:

"I am taking cohoba to receive vision. I ask for your guidance. I ask for your protection. Show me what I need to see. Teach me what I need to learn. Bring me back safely when the work is done."

Sit on the stool or cushion. Have your guide prepare the cohoba powder. Traditionally, they blow it into your nostrils using a special tube. Modern practitioners sometimes use a different method, but insufflation (snorting) is most effective.

The powder goes in. The reaction is immediate.

First comes the physical—burning in the nose, bitter taste dripping down the throat, nausea rising. Do not fight this. Breathe through it. If you need to purge, purge. There is a bucket. This is normal.

Then comes the shift. The room begins to change. Colors intensify. Sounds become profound. Your body feels distant, like you are wearing it loosely rather than inhabiting it fully.

And then—if you have approached with sincerity, if the Zemí accept your petition, if you are ready—the visions come.

What you see will be personal. Unique. Impossible to predict. Some people see geometric patterns unfolding into infinite complexity. Some see ancestor faces appearing in the smoke. Some experience full narrative visions—stories playing out, teachings delivered in image and symbol.

Some people hear voices. The Zemí speak. Sometimes in words. Sometimes in feelings that land in your chest with the weight of absolute knowing.

Do not try to control the experience. Do not try to direct it. Surrender. Trust. Let the medicine take you where you need to go.

Your guide will stay with you the entire time. They will remind you to breathe if you forget. They will reassure you if fear comes. They will hold space.

The peak lasts maybe 20-30 minutes. Then the intensity begins to fade. You return to your body. The room becomes solid again. You feel heavy, exhausted, sometimes confused.

Rest. Drink water. Let your guide help you to a comfortable position. Sleep if you can.

When you wake—hours later, sometimes the next day—begin to integrate. What did you see? What did you learn? Write it down immediately, before the memory fades.

**What This Accomplishes:**

Cohoba grants vision. It answers questions that cannot be answered through logic or research. It shows you paths you could not see on your own. It connects you directly to the Zemí with a clarity that is rare and precious.

In game terms, successfully completing a cohoba journey allows you to ask the Guide three questions about your current Mystery, your character's path, or the nature of the Zemí. The Guide must answer honestly. These are not riddles or cryptic prophecies—these are direct guidance from the spirits.

Additionally, if you receive specific instruction during cohoba (plant this crop, build this altar, make amends with this person), following that instruction grants +1 to all related rolls until the task is complete.

**Common Mistakes:**

Taking cohoba alone. Do not do this. Ever. Even experienced Bohíques have a second person present for safety.

Taking cohoba recreationally. If you are approaching this as drug experience rather than sacred medicine, the Zemí will know. The experience will be harsh. You will learn, but painfully.

Ignoring the purge. If your body wants to vomit or defecate during cohoba, let it happen. This is cleansing. Fighting it makes everything worse.

Failing to integrate. The visions are not the end—they are the beginning. You must take what you learned and apply it to your life. Otherwise the journey was wasted.

### 5. Planting the Trigonolito: Anchoring Zemí Presence

Virtues Required: Iwa (integrity) + Foriti (perseverance)

Complexity: 6-slice Mystery

Duration: Multiple sessions over 1-3 weeks

Best Performed: When establishing a new home, when committing to long-term relationship with a specific Zemí, when creating sacred space

The trigonolito—three-pointed stone—is the physical anchor for Zemí presence. When you consecrate a trigonolito properly, you are not just creating a religious object. You are creating a portal. A doorway. A place where a specific Zemí can dwell in the material world and be accessible to you.

This is serious work. This is permanent work. Once you plant a trigonolito and invite a Zemí to inhabit it, that relationship is established. You cannot simply discard it when you move or change your mind. You have made a commitment.

So before you begin this practice, ask yourself: Am I ready? Am I willing to maintain this altar, make regular offerings, honor this relationship for years or decades?

If the answer is not an unqualified yes, wait. There is no shame in not being ready.

**Preparation:**

First, obtain or create the trigonolito itself. Traditionally, these are carved from stone—smooth river stones or volcanic rock. You can carve it yourself if you have the skill, commission a carver, or find pre-made trigonolitos from Taíno artisans.

The stone should fit comfortably in your hands. It should have three points representing the three realms (Turey, Atabey, Coaybay). It can be simple or elaborately carved with petroglyphs and faces—both are valid.

Second, identify which Zemí you are inviting. This should be a Zemí you already have relationship with—a river you have visited many times, a mountain you have climbed, a specific manifestation of Yúcahu or Atabey you have worked with. Do not invite a Zemí you have never spoken to.

Third, prepare the altar space. This will be the trigonolito's permanent home. It should be elevated (shelf, table, altar cabinet), clean, and in a place where you can visit it daily. Gather tobacco, rum, cassava bread, flowers, a white cloth.

**The Practice:**

This is a 6-slice Mystery because it unfolds over time. You cannot rush consecration.

**Day 1 - Introduction:**

Place the trigonolito on your altar. Sit before it. Make tobacco offering. Say:

"I have brought this stone to be a dwelling place for \[name of Zemí\]. I ask this Zemí to inhabit this stone, to make it a doorway between your realm and mine, to be present here so I can speak with you, make offerings to you, build deeper relationship with you. I promise to care for this stone, to make regular offerings, to honor our relationship. If you accept this invitation, show me a sign."

Then wait. Sit in silence for at least 30 minutes. Pay attention to what you feel, what you see, what you experience. Does the stone warm under your hands? Does the air shift? Do you feel presence gathering?

If you feel clear acceptance, proceed. If you feel resistance or nothing at all, wait. Try again another day. The Zemí might not be ready, or you might not be ready.

**Days 2-7 - Daily Offerings:**

For the next week, make offerings to the trigonolito every day. Tobacco, water, flowers, prayers. Tell the stone about your day. Share your thoughts. Begin the conversation.

Each night, sleep with the stone near you (on a bedside table, under your pillow if it's small enough). Let your dreams inform the relationship.

**Day 8 - Naming:**

On the eighth day, the stone receives its name. This is not a name you choose—this is a name the Zemí gives.

Sit with the stone. Make tobacco offering. Ask: "What shall I call you?"

Then listen. A word will come. A phrase. An image that translates to language. Trust the first thing that arrives. That is the name.

Speak the name aloud three times. Write it on paper and place the paper under the stone.

**Days 9-14 - Integration:**

Continue daily offerings. Begin asking the stone simple yes-or-no questions using the Spirit of the Zemí ability from your Lineage. Build the communication pathway.

**Day 15 - Sealing:**

On the fifteenth day (or whenever feels right—some relationships move faster, some slower), perform the final sealing.

Anoint the stone with rum. Wrap it in white cloth. Place it in a bowl of salt overnight (salt purifies and protects). In the morning, unwrap it. The stone is now consecrated. The Zemí is in residence.

Make a substantial offering—tobacco, cassava, rum, honey, flowers. Feast if you can. This is a celebration. A new relationship has been formalized.

**What This Accomplishes:**

Once you have planted a trigonolito, you have a permanent portal to that Zemí. You can speak with them any time. You can make offerings easily. You have anchored their presence in your life.

In game terms, having a consecrated trigonolito for a specific Zemí grants you +1 to all rolls when petitioning that Zemí for help. The relationship is established. The communication is clear.

Additionally, you can use the trigonolito as a focus for other rituals. Any practice involving that Zemí becomes easier when performed at the altar where their stone resides.

**Common Mistakes:**

Rushing the process. Consecration takes time. If you try to compress this into a weekend, the Zemí will not fully inhabit the stone. Be patient.

Neglecting the altar. Once you plant the trigonolito, you must tend it. Weekly offerings at minimum. Daily is better. If you let the altar gather dust, if you ignore it for months, the Zemí will leave.

Moving the stone carelessly. If you move to a new home, the trigonolito comes with you. But you must inform the Zemí beforehand, make offerings during the move, and re-establish the altar with care. Do not just throw it in a box.

### 6. Petroglyph Reading: Consulting the Ancestor Stones

Virtues Required: Oye (insight)

Complexity: 4-slice Mystery

Duration: 2-4 hours

Best Performed: When seeking guidance on path, when needing to understand history, when ancestors are calling

The ancestors left messages. Carved into stone, etched into cave walls, marked on cliff faces across the Caribbean. These petroglyphs are not decoration. They are language. They are maps, warnings, blessings, teachings.

Most people look at petroglyphs and see interesting patterns. Spirals, faces, geometric designs. Pretty, but meaningless.

Those who have developed the Petroglyph Reader Attunement understand. The meanings land fully formed, undeniable, clear.

But even without that Attunement, you can learn to read the stones. It requires patience, study, and this ritual of formal consultation.

**Preparation:**

Find petroglyphs. If you are in Borikén (Puerto Rico) or elsewhere in the Caribbean, there are known sites—caves, rock outcroppings, ancient batey courts with marked stones. Go to one.

If you cannot physically visit petroglyphs, find high-quality photographs. This is not ideal—direct contact with the carved stone is better—but it works.

Bring tobacco. Bring rum. Bring a notebook and pen to record what you learn.

Research the site beforehand if you can. Who carved these? When? What is known about their meaning? This context helps.

**The Practice:**

Approach the petroglyphs with reverence. You are entering sacred space, consulting with ancestors. Behave accordingly.

Make tobacco offering first. Say:

"Ancestors who carved these stones, I come seeking your wisdom. I come with respect. I ask permission to read your messages. Guide my understanding. Help me see what you intended to teach."

Pour rum on the ground or at the base of the stone. This is libation for the dead.

Then sit before the petroglyphs. Really look at them. Do not just glance—study them. Notice every line, every curve, every detail.

Choose one symbol to focus on. Maybe it calls to you. Maybe it's the most prominent. Maybe it's the most mysterious. Trust your instinct.

Place your hand on the carving if possible (if the site allows touch—some preservation rules prohibit this, and you must respect those boundaries). Feel the grooves. Feel the age. Feel the hands that carved it centuries or millennia ago.

Close your eyes. Breathe. Ask: "What does this symbol mean? What were you trying to communicate?"

Then wait. Do not force interpretation. Do not let your analytical mind jump to conclusions. Just wait with open attention.

Images may come. Feelings. Sudden knowing. A voice in your mind speaking a language you don't consciously know but somehow understand.

When meaning arrives, write it down immediately. Do not edit. Do not second-guess. Just record what came.

Move to another symbol. Repeat the process.

Spend as long as you need. Some people read one or two symbols and feel complete. Some spend hours working through an entire panel.

When you are finished, make final offerings. Thank the ancestors. Promise to carry their wisdom forward.

**What This Accomplishes:**

Petroglyph Reading connects you to the ancestors in a direct, personal way. It demonstrates that the past is not dead—it is alive, speaking, offering guidance to those who listen.

In game terms, successfully reading petroglyphs grants you specific knowledge about:

- Sacred sites (where they are, how to approach them)

- Historical events (what happened here, what this place witnessed)

- Spiritual practices (how the ancestors worked with the Zemí)

- Warnings (dangers to avoid, mistakes not to repeat)

The Guide should provide concrete, useful information based on what symbols you focused on.

Additionally, if you are working on a Mystery that involves understanding the past or honoring ancestors, performing Petroglyph Reading grants +1 to Oye for rolls related to that work.

**Common Mistakes:**

Trusting the first interpretation that comes. Sometimes your conscious mind will offer explanations that are logical but wrong. Sit with the symbols longer. Let deeper meaning emerge.

Forgetting that context matters. A spiral carved in one location may mean something different than the same spiral carved elsewhere. Consider the site, the other symbols nearby, what is known about the people who carved them.

Treating this as academic exercise. This is not archaeology. This is communion with the dead. Approach with reverence, not detachment.

### 7. Caracol Summoning: Calling the Community

Virtues Required: Foriti (perseverance)

Complexity: 4-slice Mystery

Duration: Varies—preparation takes hours, the calling itself is moments

Best Performed: When the community needs to gather, when warning must be given, when important announcements must be made

The caracol—the conch shell—is the voice of the Taíno people. Its sound carries for miles. Its tone is unmistakable. When you hear it, you know: something important is happening. Pay attention. Respond.

Caracol Summoning is not just blowing a shell. It is consecration of the shell, learning its voice, understanding when and how to call, and bearing the responsibility of community attention.

**Preparation:**

Obtain a conch shell. Ideally, you find it yourself while diving or walking beaches. If that's not possible, you can purchase one—but choose carefully. Hold different shells. Listen to their voices (even without formal consecration, each shell has a particular tone). Choose the one that resonates with you.

The shell must be prepared. The tip needs to be cut or drilled to create a mouthpiece. This requires skill—too much and the shell won't sound properly, too little and you can't blow it. If you don't know how to do this, find someone who does. Many coastal communities have craftspeople who work with conch shells.

Once the shell has a mouthpiece, it must be consecrated. This is a 4-slice Mystery in itself.

**Day 1 - Purification:**

Wash the shell in ocean water if possible, river water if not. As you wash, pray:

"I cleanse this shell of all that is not sacred. I prepare it to be a voice for the Zemí, a call for the community, a sound that commands attention and respect."

Let it dry in the sun for a full day.

**Day 2 - Blessing:**

Take the shell to the ocean (or to a river if ocean is not accessible). Make tobacco offering to the water Zemí. Hold the shell and say:

"I ask the water spirits to bless this caracol. Fill it with your voice. Make it a bridge between the human world and the spirit world. Let its sound carry my intentions clearly."

Immerse the shell three times—once for body, once for mind, once for spirit.

**Day 3 - First Sounding:**

This is when you learn to play the caracol. It is not like blowing a whistle—it requires technique. You press your lips to the mouthpiece, tighten your lips as if saying "oooo," and blow steadily while buzzing your lips slightly.

It will sound terrible at first. Keep practicing. The shell will teach you its voice.

When you can produce a clear, resonant note, blow it three times. Listen to how the sound travels. Notice how it feels in your chest, in your bones.

**Day 4 - Naming:**

Like the trigonolito, the consecrated caracol receives a name. Sit with the shell. Ask: "What shall I call you?"

The name will come. Speak it aloud. Write it inside the shell or on a tag attached to it.

The shell is now ready.

**The Practice of Calling:**

When you need to summon the community—whether for ceremony, for warning, for celebration, for urgent meeting—take the caracol to a high place or open area where sound will carry.

Make tobacco offering first. Say:

"I call the community to gather. I sound the caracol for \[state the reason\]. Let all who hear respond."

Then blow the shell. Three times is traditional—three long, clear notes with pauses between.

The sound should carry your intention. If you are calling for celebration, the notes should feel joyful. If you are warning of danger, they should feel urgent. Put your full body, your full energy into the blowing.

Then wait. People will hear. People will come. Your job is to be ready to tell them why you called.

**What This Accomplishes:**

A consecrated caracol, properly sounded, compels attention. It is not rude to ignore it—it is difficult. The Zemí amplify the call. The sound carries not just physically but spiritually.

In game terms, when you blow the caracol to summon people, roll 2d6 + Foriti. On 10+, everyone who should respond comes immediately. On 7-9, most come, but a few are delayed or resistant. On 6-, something interferes with the call—the sound doesn't carry properly, or people are prevented from responding by circumstances beyond their control.

Additionally, if you are a Caracol Keeper with the Caracol Voice Attunement, the sound carries even further and cannot be ignored without active effort.

**Common Mistakes:**

Calling trivially. If you summon the community for something unimportant—to announce your new haircut, to ask if anyone wants lunch—you lose credibility. The next time you call, people will not respond. The caracol is for matters that genuinely require community attention.

Blowing without focus. The caracol amplifies your intention. If your mind is scattered, if you don't know why you're calling, the sound will be muddy and unclear.

Failing to follow through. If you call people and they come, you must deliver. You must have something worth saying. Do not waste their time.

### 8. Guatiao Oath: Binding Sacred Kinship

Virtues Required: Iwa (integrity) + Foriti (perseverance)

Complexity: 6-slice Mystery

Duration: One formal ceremony (2-3 hours) preceded by weeks of preparation

Best Performed: When formalizing deep friendship, when creating alliance between groups, when committing to mutual support

Guatiao is not friendship in the casual modern sense. Guatiao is sacred kinship—a bond as strong as blood family, chosen deliberately, formalized through ceremony, enforced by the Zemí.

When you enter Guatiao with someone, you are swearing to support them, defend them, prioritize their wellbeing as if they were your sibling. This is not a promise you make lightly.

**Preparation:**

Both parties must agree to Guatiao. This cannot be one-sided. Both must understand what they are committing to. Sit down and discuss:

- What does this bond mean to each of you?

- What are you promising?

- How will you support each other?

- What happens if one of you breaks the oath?

These conversations should happen over weeks. You are not deciding to get coffee together. You are binding your lives.

Once agreement is reached, set a date for the ceremony. Invite witnesses—Guatiao is public, not private. The community needs to know this bond exists so they can hold you accountable.

Prepare gifts for exchange. These should be meaningful—objects that represent your commitment. Tobacco, cassava bread, carved items, woven cloth, whatever has significance. The monetary value doesn't matter. The meaning matters.

Prepare the space. Ideally outdoors, at a sacred site (batey, river, cave entrance). Create an altar—white cloth, flowers, candles, tobacco.

**The Practice:**

The ceremony has seven parts:

1.  **Opening - Calling the Zemí:**

The ceremony leader (usually a Bohíque or respected elder, but can be anyone trusted by both parties) makes tobacco offering and calls the Zemí to witness:

"We gather today to create Guatiao bond between \[Name\] and \[Name\]. We ask the Zemí to witness this oath, to bless this relationship, to hold these two accountable to what they promise here."

2.  **Introduction - Declaring Intent:**

Each person stands before the community and declares their intent:

"I, \[Name\], choose \[Name\] as Guatiao. I swear to treat them as blood family, to support them in joy and hardship, to defend them when they are threatened, to speak truth to them even when it is difficult, to honor this bond for as long as we both live."

Both speak. The words can vary, but the commitment must be clear.

3.  **Exchange - Gift Giving:**

Each person presents their gift to the other. As they give, they explain the symbolism:

"I give you this \[object\] because \[meaning\]. It represents my commitment to you, my promise to walk this path alongside you."

Receive the gift with gratitude. Place both gifts on the altar.

4.  **Binding - The Physical Tie:**

Both people's wrists are tied together with cord or cloth (traditionally white, but any color works). This represents the binding of their fates. They stand tied for several minutes while the community sings or chants or sits in witnessing silence.

5.  **Offering - Shared Ebo:**

Still tied together, both people make joint offering—pouring rum, placing tobacco, speaking prayers together. This is the first act they perform as Guatiao.

6.  **Releasing - Untying:**

The cord is untied and divided. Each person keeps half as reminder of the bond. These pieces should be kept somewhere visible—on an altar, tied around the wrist, in a pocket.

7.  **Celebration - Feast:**

The ceremony concludes with food and drink. Everyone eats together. Everyone celebrates. This is not solemn obligation—this is joy. A new kinship has been created. That deserves celebration.

**What This Accomplishes:**

Guatiao creates accountability, support, and spiritual enforcement. When you have made this oath, you cannot easily dismiss the other person's needs. The bond is real. The Zemí are watching.

In game terms, when two characters are Guatiao:

- Either can call on the other for help, and the other must respond if physically possible (refusing marks Imbalance)

- When working together on a Mystery, they grant each other +1 to rolls (the bond strengthens cooperation)

- If one betrays the other without grave cause, the betrayer marks three boxes of Imbalance immediately and loses access to all Zemí blessings until amends are made

This is serious. This is binding. This is not a game mechanic to min-max—this is spiritual commitment.

**Common Mistakes:**

Rushing into Guatiao. Do not make this oath after knowing someone for a week. Take time. Build relationship. Make sure this bond is what both of you truly want.

Failing to maintain the relationship. Guatiao is not a one-time ceremony. It is ongoing commitment. You must stay in contact, check in regularly, show up when needed. If you ghost your Guatiao, the bond breaks and there are consequences.

Breaking the oath casually. If you absolutely must dissolve a Guatiao bond (because one person has fundamentally changed, because the relationship has become toxic), there is a formal unbinding ceremony. Do not just walk away.

### 9. Hurricane Petition: Asking Guabancex for Mercy

Virtues Required: Suuru (patience) + Oye (insight)

Complexity: 8-slice Mystery

Duration: Multi-day preparation, ceremony lasts 4-6 hours

Best Performed: Only in extreme need, never casually

This is the most dangerous ritual in this book. I include it because it is part of Taíno tradition, because the knowledge should not be lost. But I include it with a warning written in my own blood:

Do not petition Guabancex unless you are truly prepared for her answer.

She is the hurricane mother. She teaches through destruction. She transforms through chaos. When you petition her—when you ask her to spare your community, to redirect her storm, to show mercy—you are asking a force of nature to change its course.

She may listen. She may not. And either way, there is a price.

**Preparation:**

This ritual requires a Bohíque or advanced practitioner. Do not attempt it alone. Do not attempt it without training.

Fast for three days before the ceremony. Water only, no food. Your body must be empty, clean, ready to receive whatever comes.

Gather massive offerings—enough tobacco to fill a basket, rum by the bottle, cassava bread, honey, flowers. Guabancex is not impressed by small gestures. If you are asking her to change the course of a hurricane, your offerings must reflect the magnitude of the request.

Prepare a sacred space. Outdoors if possible, somewhere high where you can see the storm approaching. Create an altar that can withstand wind and rain—stone platform, weighted down, sturdy.

Inform your community what you are doing. They need to know. They need to prepare. And they need to support you through what comes next.

**The Practice:**

**Night 1 - The Opening:**

Begin at sunset the night before you plan to petition. Make offerings to Guabancex's two servants first—Guataubá (the herald and the wind) and Coatrisquie (the flood)—and then to Guabancex herself.

Light three fires: one for each servant, and a third, larger fire for their mother. Speak to each:

"Guataubá, herald who rides the wind, I ask you to carry my petition to your mother. Tell her I come with respect, with offerings, with genuine need."

"Coatrisquie, lady of the flood, I ask you to temper the waters. We cannot survive drowning. Show us mercy."

"Guabancex, mother of the storm, I ask your servants to gentle the wind and temper the water. We are fragile. We are human. We need your lesson but we need to survive it."

Feed tobacco to each fire. Pour rum. Leave the fires burning through the night (safely contained, watched).

**Dawn - The Fasting Vigil:**

As the sun rises, take your position at the altar. You will stay here until the petition is complete. No food. Water only. No leaving except for necessary functions.

Spend the day in prayer and meditation. Speak to Guabancex constantly—tell her about your community, about what you will lose if the storm comes full force, about why you are asking her to change course.

Do not beg. Do not grovel. State facts. Make your case with dignity.

**Afternoon - The Cohoba Journey:**

If you are trained in cohoba work, this is when you take it. The vision medicine allows you to speak with Guabancex directly, face to face, spirit to spirit.

In the cohoba space, you will see her. She may appear as a woman, as a storm, as something incomprehensible. However she manifests, speak your petition clearly:

"Guabancex, I ask you to spare us the full force of your teaching. We understand you bring transformation. We understand we need to change. But we cannot change if we are dead. Show us mercy. Give us time."

Listen to her response. She will either grant the petition, refuse it, or offer conditions. If there are conditions, you must accept them or the petition fails.

**Evening - The Offering:**

As the sun sets, make the final massive offering. Place everything on the altar—all the tobacco, all the rum, all the food. Pour it out. Burn it. Give it completely.

Speak the final words:

"Guabancex, I have fasted. I have prayed. I have offered everything I can offer. I place my community in your hands. Do as you will. We trust your wisdom even when we do not understand it."

Then wait.

**The Response:**

Within hours or days, you will know her answer. The storm will shift course, weaken, dissipate—or it will not. She will have spoken.

If she granted mercy, you owe her. The petition included a promise of change, and you must keep it. The community must address whatever the hurricane was coming to teach—environmental damage, social injustice, spiritual neglect, whatever it was.

If she refused, you must accept the lesson. The storm will come. Your job now is to help your community survive it and learn from it.

**What This Accomplishes:**

Hurricane Petition can, sometimes, redirect catastrophic storms. It can save lives, preserve communities, prevent massive destruction.

But it comes at cost. The price is not paid in money or objects—it is paid in commitment. The community must actually change. They must address the root causes that brought the hurricane. They must honor their promises.

In game terms, this is an 8-slice Mystery with high stakes. The Guide rolls secretly to determine Guabancex's response, modified by:

- Quality of offerings (+/- 2)

- Sincerity of the petition (+/- 2)

- Whether the community has honored Guabancex in the past (+/- 1)

- Whether the change they promise is genuine and achievable (+/- 1)

Even with perfect rolls, this is not guaranteed. Guabancex is a force of nature. She answers to no one.

**Common Mistakes:**

Petitioning selfishly. If you are asking Guabancex to spare your house specifically while letting everyone else suffer—she will refuse. Petitions must be for the community, not individual benefit.

Failing to keep promises. If you promise to change and then do not, the next hurricane will be worse. Guabancex does not forget.

Petitioning without authority. You cannot perform this ritual without community support. If you are acting alone, it will fail.

### 10. Cave Oracle: Listening to Coaybay

Virtues Required: Oye (insight)

Complexity: 4-slice Mystery

Duration: 3-5 hours, including preparation and cleansing afterward

Best Performed: When you need a message from the dead, from deep earth Zemí, or from the parts of yourself you have buried

The caves are the doorways to Coaybay. You learned this in Chapter 1, and you met Cueva Ventana in Chapter 3. Now I will teach you how to sit at the doorway and listen.

The Cave Oracle is not cohoba. You do not leave your body. You do not soar into Turey. You go down instead—into darkness, into stillness, into the cool breath of the earth—and you wait until something speaks. Sometimes it is an ancestor. Sometimes it is a cave Zemí. Sometimes it is your own buried knowing, finally loud enough to hear because there is nothing else competing with it.

This is a practice of courage more than technique. Most people cannot sit in complete darkness for long. The mind panics. Old fears surface. The body insists that something is wrong. The Cave Oracle asks you to stay anyway, and to discover what is on the other side of that fear.

**Preparation:**

Choose your cave carefully. In Borikén, there are caves set aside for oracle work, and the Bohíques know which ones are open to whom. In our world, the most important rule is safety: never enter a wild cave alone, never enter without someone above ground who knows where you are and when you will return, and never enter a cave system you do not know with someone who does. Many people die in caves every year. The Zemí do not want you to die. They want you to listen.

If you cannot safely reach a cave, you can create cave conditions: a closed room made completely dark, cool if possible, with a bowl of water and a stone on the floor beside you. This is not the same as a true cave, but the darkness is real, and the Zemí can work with it.

Bring tobacco, rum, a light that you will turn off, a notebook, and a warm layer of clothing. Bring a companion who will stay at the entrance or just outside the room.

Fast lightly for the day before—simple food, no alcohol, nothing heavy. Write down your question. One question. Keep it honest and simple: "What do my ancestors want me to know?" "What am I refusing to see?" "What does this cave want from me?"

**The Practice:**

At the cave mouth, make a full tobacco offering. Say your name and your lineage. Say why you have come. Say: "I am asking permission to sit in your darkness and listen. I will not take anything. I will not stay longer than I am welcome. Let me hear what I need to hear."

Pour a little rum on the ground at the entrance for the dead.

Wait for permission. If the air from the cave feels cold and pushing, or your body refuses to move forward, do not enter. Come back another day. If the air feels still and the way feels open, go in—slowly, with your light on—only as far as you can safely sit.

Find your place. Sit on the ground with your back against stone if possible. Place your offering beside you. Then turn off your light.

The first minutes are the hardest. Your eyes will search for anything. Your heart may race. You may feel watched. Breathe slowly. Count your breaths to forty. Let your body learn that it is safe.

Then speak your question once, aloud. Let the cave echo it back to you. Listen to how your own voice sounds when the stone returns it—deeper, older, as if someone else were asking.

Then be silent. Stay silent for at least thirty minutes. Do not try to make anything happen.

What comes will come in its own way. A voice inside your head that is not quite yours. A face appearing in the dark behind your eyes. A memory returning—something you forgot on purpose. A smell: tobacco, earth, a grandmother's perfume. A single word. Or nothing at all, followed hours later by a dream that answers the question.

When you feel the conversation end—and you will feel it, like a door quietly closing—say thank you. Turn your light back on. Leave the offering. Walk out the way you came, without turning your back on the darkness until you reach daylight.

Then cleanse. Before you go home, wash in running water—a river, the ocean, or at minimum your hands, face, and feet in clean water with a little salt. Cave energy clings. You need to give it back to the earth.

Write down everything you received before you sleep.

**What This Accomplishes:**
