The Cave Oracle gives you an answer from below—from the dead, from the earth, from the deep self. It is slower and quieter than cohoba, and often harder to interpret, but it is also gentler on the body and available to Seekers who are not yet trained for vision medicine.

In game terms, roll 2d6 + Oye after the vigil. On 10+, the Guide answers your question clearly, speaking as whoever answered—ancestor, cave Zemí, or buried self. On 7-9, the answer comes as an image or symbol that the Guide describes; you must interpret it. On 6-, the darkness gives you something you did not ask for—a fear, a memory, a truth about yourself—and you mark one box of Doubt, but the Guide must also tell you one true thing you did not know before.

If you have the Cave Walker Attunement, you may perform this practice in any cave without risk of becoming lost, and you add +1 to the roll.

**Common Mistakes:**

Entering unsafe caves. Spiritual courage is not the same as physical recklessness. A twisted ankle or a lost light in a wild cave can kill you. Go with experienced people and tell someone where you are.

Filling the silence. People bring music, chant constantly, or keep asking new questions because the silence frightens them. Ask once. Then wait.

Taking souvenirs. Do not break off formations. Do not pick up bones. Do not take stones from cave floors. What lives in the cave belongs to the cave.

Skipping the cleansing. Seekers who go straight home from the Cave Oracle often carry heaviness into their household—bad sleep, arguments, a lingering chill. Wash before you go home.

### 11. Zemí Awakening: Consecrating a Vessel
Virtues Required: Oye (insight) + Iwa (integrity)

Complexity: 6-slice Mystery

Duration: Three days and three nights, with a closing ceremony on the fourth morning

Best Performed: When a new carving, stone, tool, or object has come to you and asks to become the home of a Zemí

Planting the Trigonolito, which you learned earlier in this chapter, invites a Zemí you already know to dwell in a three-pointed stone on your altar. Zemí Awakening is broader and, in some ways, stranger. It is the practice of recognizing that an object—a carving, a found stone, a piece of driftwood, a drum, a tool you use every day—is already waking up, and helping that awakening complete.

Remember what you learned in Chapter 1: everything has spirit. But not everything is ready to be a vessel. Some objects simply are what they are. Others begin to call attention to themselves. A carving that keeps catching your eye. A stone that feels warm when the others are cool. A tool that seems to work better when you speak to it. These are signs that a Zemí is gathering in the thing and wants a name and a relationship.

The old accounts tell that zemí images were not invented by the carver alone. The Bohíque would discern, often in trance, what spirit was revealing itself in a tree or a stone. The carver helped it emerge. The making was a collaboration among the spirit, the specialist, and the hands. Zemí Awakening honors that same collaboration.

This includes modern objects. In Borikén, Bohíques have awakened Zemí in solar panel arrays, in the old radio transmitter on the mountain above Jayuya, in a fishing boat's engine, in the first server rack of the University's computing center. Technology is not excluded from spirit. It is simply newer to the conversation.

**Preparation:**

Spend at least a week with the object before beginning. Keep it near you. Notice how it feels at different times of day. Ask it, using Spirit of the Zemí: "Are you asking to be awakened?" If the answer is no, do not proceed. If the answer is yes, begin to prepare.

Gather: tobacco, fresh water, salt, a white cloth large enough to wrap the object, cassava bread, flowers, a candle or small lamp, and anything the object seems to want. Some objects ask for honey. Some ask for music. Some ask for sea water, or soil from a particular place. Listen.

Choose a place where the object can rest undisturbed for three days.

If the object was made by someone else—a carver, a builder, an engineer—contact them if you can. Tell them what you are doing. Their hands are part of this.

**The Practice:**

**Day 1 - Purification:**

At dawn, wash the object. Stone and wood can be washed in water with a little salt. Delicate objects and machines can be cleansed with tobacco smoke passed slowly around them. As you cleanse, speak: "I wash away what is not yours. I wash away the dust of the market, the hands of strangers, the noise of the world. Whatever you are becoming, become it clean."

Wrap the object in white cloth. Set it on the altar. Sit with it for a while in silence.

**Night 1 - The First Dream:**

Sleep near the object. Before sleeping, say: "If you want to show yourself to me, show me in my dreams." In the morning, write down every dream, even fragments.

**Day 2 - Feeding:**

Unwrap the object. Offer it tobacco, water, cassava bread, and flowers. Offer whatever it asked for during the week of preparation. Speak to it about your life—your family, your work, why you think it came to you. This is the beginning of relationship.

Then sit in stillness and ask: "What do you want? What do you protect? What do you help with?" Listen for the answer the way you listen when consulting stones. Write it down.

**Night 2 - The Second Dream:**

Sleep near the object again. Ask, before sleep: "What is your name?"

**Day 3 - Naming:**

In the morning, sit before the object with tobacco. Whatever name arrived in your dream—or whatever name rises in you now, first and unedited—speak it aloud three times. Write it on a slip of paper and place it beneath the object.

From this moment, address the Zemí by name.

If you are working with others, this is the day to gather them. Introduce the Zemí to your household or community: "This is \[name\]. It has awakened in this \[object\]. It helps with \[purpose\]. Greet it."

**Night 3 - The Vigil:**

Sit up with the Zemí through the night, or for as much of the night as you can manage. Keep a candle or lamp burning beside it. Sing to it, speak to it, or simply keep it company. A newly awakened Zemí, like a newborn, should not be left alone on its first full night with a name.

**Day 4 - Sealing:**

At dawn, pass the object through tobacco smoke one final time. Anoint it—rum for most Zemí, water for those who refuse alcohol, oil for wood. Say: "You are awake. You have a name. You have a home. I will feed you. I will speak to you. I will not abandon you. Let us walk together."

Feast. Share food with the people who witnessed the awakening. Leave a portion for the Zemí.

**What This Accomplishes:**

Zemí Awakening creates a new spirit-vessel with its own name, purpose, and relationship to you. It is the way a household, a workplace, or a community gathers its own spiritual allies over time.

In game terms, when the Mystery is complete, roll 2d6 + Iwa. On 10+, the awakened Zemí is strong and clear: you gain +1 to all rolls related to its purpose while it is with you or on your altar, and you may ask it questions using Spirit of the Zemí from anywhere in the same building. On 7-9, the Zemí is awake but young: the +1 applies only when you are touching it, and it will need extra offerings for the first month. On 6-, something else answered the call—the Guide reveals that the spirit in the object has its own agenda, and the relationship must be negotiated carefully.

**Common Mistakes:**

Awakening things for status. Do not consecrate objects to show off or to sell them as "activated" Zemí. A Zemí is a relationship, not a product.

Forcing an awakening. If the object said no, it means no. You cannot argue a spirit into a vessel.

Abandoning the Zemí after the ceremony. A newly awakened Zemí that is never fed again becomes resentful or simply goes back to sleep. If you cannot commit to ongoing care, do not begin.

Excluding machines out of prejudice. If your generator, your computer, or your truck is asking to be honored, do not dismiss it because it is not "natural." The Taíno have never drawn that line.

### 12. Storm Walking: Learning the Way Through Chaos

Virtues Required: Foriti (perseverance) + Suuru (patience)

Complexity: 8-slice Mystery

Duration: A full hurricane season of training (in game, typically several sessions), culminating in a single storm-night vigil

Best Performed: As advanced training for Caracol Keepers and anyone who serves their community during disasters

Read this whole section before you do anything.

Storm Walking is the training path behind the Caracol Keeper's First Breakthrough Practice and the Hurricane Heart Attunement. In the world of this book, master Caracol Keepers can move through hurricanes when their communities need them—carrying messages, reaching the stranded, guiding people to shelter.

In our world, I need to be absolutely clear: you do not walk into hurricanes. You do not go outside during a storm to prove your spiritual strength. Every hurricane season, people die doing exactly that—driving through floodwater, walking to check the ocean, going out during the eye and getting caught when the second wall arrives. Guabancex does not reward that. She takes it.

The real-life practice of Storm Walking is something else, and it is harder. It is learning to read storms so well that you are never caught off guard. It is training your body and your community so thoroughly that when the storm comes, you are calm. It is finding the still point inside yourself while the wind screams outside the shelter. And it is being the person who walks out first when the storm has passed and it is safe—who checks on the elders, who clears the road, who knows where everyone is.

That is storm walking. The in-game feat is the mythic version of it. Both are sacred.

**Preparation:**

This practice begins at the start of hurricane season and runs through its end. You need: a notebook for storm records, a caracol or other signal instrument, tobacco and rum for Guabancex and her servants, an emergency kit for yourself and at least one other household, and a teacher—ideally a Caracol Keeper, or in our world an experienced emergency responder, sailor, or weather-wise elder.

Learn your community's real emergency plan. Know the shelters, the evacuation routes, the flood zones, who is elderly or disabled or alone. This is not separate from the spiritual work. This is the spiritual work.

**The Practice:**

**Phase 1 - Learning the Sky (early season):**

Every day, morning and evening, go outside and read the weather. Record the wind direction, the shape and color of the clouds, the feel of the air, the behavior of birds and animals, what the sea looks like if you can see it. Compare what you notice with the official forecasts. Over weeks, your body will begin to recognize patterns the instruments confirm later.

Make a small offering to Guataubá, the herald, once a week: a pinch of tobacco given to the wind from a high place. Say: "Herald of the storm, teach me to hear you coming."

**Phase 2 - Learning the Body (mid-season):**

Train for endurance. Walk long distances. Carry weight. Learn to stay calm when you are tired, wet, and uncomfortable. Practice breathing that slows your heart. Practice standing still in heavy rain—real rain, in safe conditions—for long minutes, feeling it, not fleeing it.

Practice your signal. Learn to blow the caracol in wind, so the note carries. Learn the community's warning calls.

Make a small offering to Coatrisquie, the flood, once a week: fresh water poured into running water. Say: "Lady of the flood, teach me where the water goes."

**Phase 3 - Learning the Community (late season):**

Before the first major storm of the season, check on every household on your list. Help them secure their homes. Help them stock supplies. Make sure they know the plan. In Borikén, this is called the Great Securing, and the Caracol Keepers lead it.

**Phase 4 - The Storm-Night Vigil:**

When a real storm comes, you do not go out into it. You go to your shelter—your home, a community shelter, wherever you are safest—and you keep vigil.

Make a full offering to Guabancex before the wind rises: tobacco, rum, and a promise of what you will do for your community after she passes.

Then, while the storm rages, sit in the safest interior space you have and find the still point. Breathe. Listen to the wind without fearing it. Notice the moments when the pressure changes, when the sound shifts, when the rain changes rhythm. Name what you hear. Speak to the storm: "I hear you. I am not afraid of you. I am ready for what comes after."

If others are with you, be the calm one. Lead a song. Tell stories. Keep the children busy. Make sure everyone drinks water. This is the Caracol Keeper's work in the storm.

**Phase 5 - The Walk After:**

When the official all-clear comes—not before—walk out. Check your list. Go to the elders first. Clear what you can safely clear. Report what you cannot. Sound the caracol to gather people if the community needs to meet.

Then, when the immediate work is done, return to the high place where you made offerings to Guataubá. Thank Guabancex and her servants. Tell them what you learned.

**What This Accomplishes:**

Storm Walking transforms fear of chaos into readiness for it. It makes you the person your community can rely on when everything is falling apart.

In game terms, completing Storm Walking teaches a Caracol Keeper the Storm Walking practice described in Chapter 2 (if they have not learned it yet) or grants a permanent +1 to Foriti rolls made during storms and disasters (if they have). Seekers of any calling who complete this training may choose the Hurricane Heart Attunement as their reward, even if it is not normally available to their path.

Additionally, roll 2d6 + Suuru at the end of the storm-night vigil. On 10+, the Guide tells you one thing about the next storm before it arrives—when it will come, where it will hit hardest, or what it is coming to teach. On 7-9, you receive a warning but not the details. On 6-, you mark one box of Strain; the storm got inside you, and you will need a River Blessing before you can rest.

**Common Mistakes:**

Treating this as an extreme sport. The point is service. If you are doing this to feel powerful, you have misunderstood Guabancex completely.

Going out during the eye of the storm. The calm in the eye is not the end. The second wall comes fast and from the other direction. Stay in shelter until the storm has fully passed.

Neglecting the practical training. Spiritual readiness without a flashlight, water, and an evacuation plan is not readiness. It is wishful thinking.

Forgetting the walk after. The most important part of Storm Walking happens when the wind stops. The people who need you are waiting.

### 13. Ancestor Canoe: The Three-Day Vigil

Virtues Required: Suuru (patience) + Iwa (integrity)

Complexity: 6-slice Mystery

Duration: Three days and three nights, beginning as soon as possible after death

Best Performed: When someone has died and their spirit must be guided to Coaybay

You have heard about this ceremony throughout this book. In Chapter 1, you learned that the dead travel to Coaybay after three days of vigil, guided by Opiyel-Guobiran, met by Maquetaurie Guayaba. In Chapter 2, you learned that the Bohíque's Third Breakthrough Practice is Death Guide—the full Ceremony of the Canoe. Now I will teach you the shape of it.

The canoe is the image at the center. The Taíno have always been people of canoes—carving them from single trees, paddling between islands, trusting the vessel to carry them across dangerous water to the other shore. Death is the final crossing. The vigil builds a canoe for the spirit out of song, tobacco, memory, and love, and launches it toward Coaybay.

This is not a ritual you perform for strangers casually. It is the work of a family, a community, and a Bohíque or experienced death worker together. But every Seeker should understand it, because every Seeker will lose someone, and every Seeker will someday be the one in the canoe.

**Preparation:**

When death comes, first do what the law and the living require. Call the people who must be called. Care for the body according to the family's wishes and local regulations. Do not let spiritual work interfere with practical care, medical certainty, or the family's grief.

Then prepare the vigil space—the room where the body rests, or, if the body is elsewhere, a room with the person's photograph and belongings. Gather: tobacco, cassava bread, water, rum, flowers, candles or lamps that can burn through the night, a small carved or paper canoe, and something that belonged to the dead—a garment, a tool, a favorite cup.

Ask the family for the person's full name, their nickname, and their story. You will need all three.

Feed a dog. Any dog—a neighbor's, a stray, your own. Leave food out and say: "Opiyel, we are beginning. Please come when it is time."

**The Practice:**

**Day 1 - The Gathering:**

Open the vigil at sunset. Light the first candle. Make a tobacco offering to Maquetaurie Guayaba—not asking anything, simply announcing: "Lord of Coaybay, \[full name\] has died. We are keeping vigil. We will send them to you in three days, prepared."

Place the canoe at the head of the body or before the photograph. Set the dead person's belonging inside it.

Then gather the living around and speak the person's name—full name first, then the name everyone called them. Tell their story. Let anyone who wants to speak, speak. This first night is for memory. Laughing is permitted. So is wailing.

Keep the candle burning all night. Someone must stay awake with the dead at all times. Take shifts.

**Day 2 - The Unfinished:**

The second day is for unfinished business. The dead often linger because something remains unresolved—an apology not made, a debt not paid, a secret not told, a fear for someone they left behind.

Sit with the dead and ask aloud: "What is holding you here? What do you need from us?" Listen. In the family, someone will usually know. Sometimes the answer comes in a dream that night or the night before. Sometimes a dog appears and paces or whines—Opiyel showing that something is wrong.

Whatever can be resolved, resolve it. Make the phone call. Speak the apology on their behalf. Promise to care for the person they worried about. Pay the debt. Forgive what can be forgiven, and say aloud what cannot yet be forgiven, so that at least it has been named.

Offer cassava bread and water to the dead. They are traveling soon. Feed them for the journey.

**Day 3 - The Launching:**

The third day is for release. In the evening, gather everyone. Make a full offering—tobacco, rum, cassava, flowers. Sing. In Borikén, there are specific death songs; in our world, sing what the dead loved, or sing the simplest song you know over and over until everyone is singing together.

The Bohíque or ceremony leader speaks to Opiyel-Guobiran: "Guardian of the threshold, \[name\] is ready. Their business is finished. Their people have let them go. Lead them across."

Then speak to the dead directly, one final time: "Go now. You are loved. You are remembered. You do not need to stay. The canoe is ready. Go home to Coaybay. Rest."

Lift the canoe. Carry it out of the house—to a river, to the sea, to a fire, or to the earth. Release it: set it on moving water, burn it, or bury it. As it goes, everyone says the person's name together one last time.

Then turn away and walk back without looking behind you.

**Closing:**

Before anyone goes to sleep, everyone who kept vigil must wash—hands, face, and feet at minimum, ideally in running water. Sweep the vigil room. Open the windows. Death work clings, and the living must return to the living.

**What This Accomplishes:**

The Ancestor Canoe guides the dead to rest and helps the living let them go. It turns raw grief into a shared act of love. It prevents the restless lingering that the Taíno have always feared—spirits who do not know they are dead, or who cannot leave because no one helped them.

In game terms, when a Bohíque completes the Ancestor Canoe, roll 2d6 + Suuru. On 10+, the spirit crosses cleanly, the family is comforted, and everyone who kept vigil clears one box of Imbalance. On 7-9, the spirit crosses, but something unfinished remains among the living—the Guide reveals what, and it can become a future Mystery. On 6-, Opiyel blocks the path: the spirit lingers, and the Seekers must discover what was missed before trying again.

As described in Chapter 2, the Bohíque leading the ceremony marks Imbalance when it is done, and gains +1 to Iwa permanently at their next Breakthrough.

**Common Mistakes:**

Rushing the vigil. Three days is not arbitrary. Grief needs time. The dead need time. If you compress the vigil into one evening for convenience, the spirit may not be ready.

Ignoring the family's tradition. If the dead person or their family practiced another religion, honor that first. The Ancestor Canoe can sit alongside a funeral Mass, a Jewish shiva, or a secular memorial. It should never compete with them.

Leaving the dead alone. Someone must keep vigil through each night. A dead spirit left alone in the first days may wander.

Skipping the washing. Seekers who carry death home unwashed often suffer for it—illness, nightmares, a strange heaviness. Wash before you sleep.

### 14. Island Healing: Restoring Poisoned Land

Virtues Required: Suuru (patience) + Oye (insight) + Foriti (perseverance) + Iwa (integrity)

Complexity: 12-slice Mystery

Duration: Months, sometimes years, of coordinated community work, with ceremonies marking each stage

Best Performed: When a river, forest, coast, or field has been poisoned or destroyed, and the community commits to bringing it back

This is the largest ritual in this book. It requires all four Virtues because it requires everything you have.

Island Healing is not a single ceremony. It is a commitment by a whole community to heal a wounded place—a river poisoned by industry, a hillside stripped of forest, a beach choked with plastic, a mangrove cleared for hotels, farmland dead from chemicals. It weaves together the most practical work there is—soil testing, cleanup, planting, legal action, monitoring—with the most sacred work there is: apology, offering, and long relationship with a hurt Zemí.

In Chapter 2, you learned that the master Naboría's Fifth Breakthrough Practice is Land Healer. Island Healing is that practice expanded to the scale of a community, under the leadership of Naborías, Bohíques, and anyone else willing to do the work.

**Preparation:**

First, listen to the land. Before you plan anything, a Bohíque or experienced Seeker must sit with the wounded place and ask the Zemí directly: "What happened to you? What do you need? Will you accept our help?" Use Spirit of the Zemí, Soil Reading, River Memory, or simply long silence. Write down what you receive.

Second, learn the facts. In our world, this means real data: water tests, soil tests, the history of the site, who is responsible for the damage, what laws apply, what scientists and environmental groups already know. The Zemí's story and the scientist's report usually match. Where they do not, look harder.

Third, gather the community. Island Healing cannot be done by a few devoted people. It needs families, schools, elders, workers, officials—and, where possible, the people or companies who caused the harm. Hold a gathering. Sound the caracol if you can. Tell the land's story. Ask who will commit.

Fourth, set the stages. Divide the work into seasons: what must happen first (stop the poison), what comes next (clean what is there), and what comes last (replant, restock, rest).

**The Practice:**

**The First Ceremony - Apology:**

Gather at the wounded place. Make a full tobacco offering. Then the community apologizes—out loud, together, and specifically. "We let this happen. We dumped waste here. We looked away. We profited. We forgot you." If those who caused the harm are present and willing, they speak too.

Leave offerings of clean water and native seeds. Promise what you will do, and promise only what you can deliver.

**The Working Seasons:**

Then the work begins. Stop the source of harm—through negotiation, through law, through protest, through whatever honest means are available. Remove what can be removed. Plant native species. Restore the soil. Bring back the animals. Monitor the water.

At every new moon, someone from the community returns to the site with tobacco, speaks to the Zemí, and reports on progress. At every full moon, the community gathers for a small shared offering and a meal near the site. This keeps the relationship alive through the long, tedious months when progress is invisible.

Mark each milestone with ceremony: the first clean water test, the first native tree surviving a dry season, the first fish returning, the first nesting bird.

**The Closing Ceremony - Return:**

The Island Healing ends when the Zemí says it is finished—not when the grant money runs out, not when the volunteers get tired. Watch for the signs: the coquíes return and sing, the fish come back, the water clears, the place simply feels different when you stand in it.

When that day comes, gather everyone who worked. Hold an areyto at the site. Feast. Thank the Zemí, the volunteers, the scientists, the officials, the children who planted trees. Name the place again, aloud, as a living being.

Then appoint guardians. The healing is not over just because the ceremony is. Someone must keep watching.

**What This Accomplishes:**

Island Healing restores land that most people would have abandoned. It shows a community that it can undo harm. It turns despair into shared purpose.

In game terms, this is a 12-slice Mystery played over many sessions. At each stage, the Guide may call for rolls using the Virtue that stage demands: Oye to diagnose, Foriti to stop the harm, Suuru to endure the slow work, Iwa to keep the community honest. When the Mystery is complete, every Seeker who participated gains +1 permanently to the Virtue they relied on most, the healed Zemí becomes an ally to the community, and the Guide should describe the restored place in loving detail.

If the community abandons the work partway, the Zemí remembers. Every Seeker involved marks two boxes of Doubt, and the wounded place becomes harder to approach in future Mysteries.

**Common Mistakes:**

Doing only the ceremony. Prayers without cleanup are an insult to a poisoned river. The Zemí need the poison gone.

Doing only the cleanup. Cleanup without relationship heals the land but not the bond. The next time someone threatens the place, no one will remember why it matters.

Making promises you cannot keep. Promise the Zemí what your community can actually do. A small promise kept is better than a grand promise broken.

Declaring victory early. The first good water test is a milestone, not an ending. Let the Zemí tell you when it is done.

### 15. Areyto of Return: The Bridge Home

Virtues Required: Iwa (integrity) + Suuru (patience)

Complexity: 8-slice Mystery

Duration: One night of song and dance, preceded by weeks of preparation and followed by a year of keeping the promise

Best Performed: For those who were taken from the islands, who grew up far from the land of their ancestors, or who have lost connection to where they come from

This ritual sits close to the heart of why this book exists.

In the world of this book, colonization never happened. But even in that world, people leave. Some sail to the other islands for work. Some go to the mainland for study and stay. Some are sent away as children. Some marry far from home. Their children grow up speaking other languages, eating other food, not knowing the names of the Zemí of the river where their grandmother was born.

In our world, the separation is far deeper. Colonization did happen. Taíno descendants are scattered across the Caribbean diaspora and the world—in New York and Orlando and Chicago, in Spain and Hawaii and everywhere else. Many grew up being told that the Taíno were extinct, that their own ancestry was a myth, that there was nothing to return to.

The Areyto of Return is for every one of them. It is a ceremony of homecoming. It uses the areyto—the sacred circle of song, dance, and story—to build a bridge between the person who was separated and the land and ancestors who never stopped waiting for them.

It does not require that you be Taíno by blood. In the Introduction, I told you the Zemí check your heart, not your genealogy. But this ritual is especially for those whose blood remembers what their lives forgot.

**Preparation:**

The person returning—call them the one coming home—begins weeks before the ceremony. They learn what they can about their family's place of origin: the town, the river, the mountains, the names of grandparents and great-grandparents. If they do not know, they say so; not knowing is part of the wound, and it is honored too.

They gather a small bundle of things from home if they can: soil from a family town, water from a river, a stone from a beach, a photograph of an ancestor. If they cannot reach the islands, they ask a relative or friend to send something. If nothing can be gathered, they bring water from wherever they live now. The land will understand.

The community preparing the areyto gathers: drums, maracas, güiros, or whatever instruments they have; tobacco; cassava bread; fruit; and a circle of people willing to sing and dance through the night.

The one coming home writes or prepares to speak the story of the separation—how their family left, what was lost, what they long for.

**The Practice:**

**Opening - The Calling:**

At sunset, the circle gathers outdoors if possible, around a fire or a central altar. The caracol is sounded three times. The ceremony leader makes a tobacco offering and calls the ancestors: "Ancestors of \[names of family places\], one of your children is coming home tonight. Come and receive them."

**The Story:**

The one coming home stands at the center of the circle and tells the story of the separation. Their family's leaving. What was forgotten. What they were told. What they have searched for. What they hope to find. The circle listens without interrupting.

When they finish, the leader asks: "Do you want to come home?"

They answer: "I want to come home."

**The Bundle:**

The one coming home opens their bundle of things from home and places each item on the altar, naming it: "This is soil from Utuado, where my grandmother was born." "This is water from the river I have never seen." Each item is greeted by the circle with one beat of the drum.

**The Areyto:**

Then the music begins. The circle forms around the one coming home and begins to move—a slow walking step first, then faster as the drums build. The songs are call and response. The leader sings a line; the circle answers. In Borikén, there are traditional areyto songs of return. In our world, sing songs from the family's tradition, or let the leader improvise simple lines: "Where do you come from?" / "I come from the river." "Who are your people?" / "My people are here."

The one coming home dances in the center. At first they may feel awkward, self-conscious, foreign. That is expected. Keep dancing. As the night goes on, something shifts. The body remembers what the mind never learned.

The areyto continues for as long as the circle can sustain it—hours, ideally, until the deep middle of the night.

**The Receiving:**

When the music reaches its peak and then quiets, the circle stops. The leader takes water from the bundle—or plain water if there is none—and pours a little over the head of the one coming home, saying: "You are not lost. You were never lost. You were away. Now you are home."

Each person in the circle comes forward, embraces the one coming home, and says: "Welcome home."

**Closing - The Promise:**

The one coming home makes a promise to the ancestors—something they will do in the coming year to keep the bridge open. Learn ten words of their ancestral language. Visit the islands. Cook their grandmother's recipe. Support a living Taíno community. Teach their children the names of the Zemí. The promise is spoken aloud before everyone.

Then the circle feasts together until dawn, or as close to dawn as they can stay awake.

**What This Accomplishes:**

The Areyto of Return heals the wound of separation. It does not undo history, and it does not pretend that one night can replace a lifetime of belonging. But it builds a bridge—a living connection between a displaced person and their land, their ancestors, and a community that recognizes them.

In game terms, roll 2d6 + Iwa at the end of the night. On 10+, the one coming home is fully received: they may choose a Taíno Lineage expression (Borikén, Quisqueya, or Cuba) as if they had been born to it, and they clear all Doubt from their Balance Track. On 7-9, the bridge is built but fragile—they gain the Lineage expression, but must keep their promise within the year or lose it. On 6-, the ancestors are not yet ready, or the one coming home is not; the Guide reveals what still stands between them and home.

Everyone who danced in the circle clears one box of Imbalance.

**Common Mistakes:**

Treating it as costume. This is not a party where outsiders dress up as Indigenous people. If you are leading this ritual, lead it with seriousness and love.

Rushing the story. The story of separation is the heart of the ceremony. Let it be told fully, even if it is long, even if it is painful.

Breaking the promise. The ancestors remember what you promised in the circle. Keep it.

Thinking one night is enough. The Areyto of Return opens the door. Walking through it takes the rest of your life.

## SACRED IMPLEMENTS: Tools of the Path

Now I will teach you about the physical objects that support Taíno spiritual practice. These are not decorations. These are tools—consecrated, empowered, used in ritual.

Each implement listed here serves specific purposes. Some you will create yourself. Some you will receive as gifts. Some you will find or purchase. All require proper care and respect.

### 1. Consecrated Trigonolito

We have already discussed this in the Planting the Trigonolito ritual, but it bears repeating: the three-pointed stone is foundational.

How to Obtain: Carve yourself, commission a carver, purchase from Taíno artisans.

How to Use: Once consecrated, the trigonolito becomes the anchor for your altar. Place offerings before it. Speak your prayers to it. Let it be the physical presence of the Zemí in your home.

Care Requirements: Weekly offerings (tobacco minimum, fresh water, occasional cassava bread). Keep the stone clean but do not use harsh chemicals. Handle with respect—this is sacred, not decorative.

Game Effect: +1 to all rolls when petitioning the specific Zemí housed in this trigonolito.

### 2. Bohíque's Tobacco Pouch

A small leather or cloth bag, consecrated to hold tobacco for ceremonial use. Once blessed, the tobacco inside becomes more potent, more acceptable to the Zemí.

How to Obtain: Make from leather or fabric you've blessed through tobacco smoke and prayer. Or receive as gift from a teacher.

How to Use: Fill with real tobacco (not cigarettes). Before each use, hold the pouch and state your intention. Take what you need. Keep the rest sealed.

Care Requirements: Refresh the tobacco regularly (don't let it get moldy or stale). Re-consecrate the pouch quarterly by passing it through tobacco smoke while praying.

Game Effect: Tobacco offered from a blessed pouch grants +1 to the first roll of any ritual or Ebo.

### 3. Sacred Caracol

The conch shell, consecrated as we discussed in Caracol Summoning. Voice of the community. Voice of the Zemí.

How to Obtain: Find on beaches (legally—know your local regulations), purchase from ethical shell dealers, receive as gift.

How to Use: Blow to summon community, to call Zemí, to announce important events. Keep practicing—the shell's voice improves with use.

Care Requirements: Rinse with fresh water after use (salt corrodes over time). Store wrapped in cloth. Re-consecrate annually with ocean water.

Game Effect: When blown with intention, compels attention. People must actively choose to ignore the call rather than passively not hearing it.

### 4. Personal Zemí Stone

Different from the larger trigonolito altar stone, this is a small stone you carry with you—pocket-sized, smooth, chosen because it called to you.

How to Obtain: Find it yourself. Walk a riverbed, a beach, a mountain trail. When a stone calls to you, pick it up. Ask permission. If it feels right, keep it.

How to Use: Carry daily. Hold when you need grounding. Speak to it when you need counsel. Offer tobacco to it regularly.

Care Requirements: Cleanse quarterly in running water. Sleep with it under your pillow occasionally to deepen the relationship.

Game Effect: Once per session, you can consult your personal stone for guidance (using Spirit of the Zemí ability) without needing to be at an altar.

### 5. Cohoba Stool

Low wooden seat, traditionally carved from a single piece, used during vision medicine ceremonies.

How to Obtain: Commission from a wood carver who understands sacred objects. Or learn woodworking and make your own.

How to Use: Sit on it during cohoba ceremonies, during deep meditation, during any practice where you need to journey between worlds.

Care Requirements: Anoint yearly with sacred oils (coconut, olive, whatever you have blessed). Store in a clean, dry place.

Game Effect: When using cohoba while seated on a consecrated stool, visions come more clearly. Guide may grant additional detail or answer an extra question.

### 6. Cassava Grater (Guayo)

The grater is the oldest kitchen tool of the Taíno—a board studded with sharp stones or shell fragments, used to shred cassava root before the poison is pressed out. Without it, there is no bread. A consecrated grater honors both Yúcahu, who gives the cassava, and Baibrama, who watches over its safe preparation.

How to Obtain: Make one from a hardwood board and small sharp stones set in resin, or buy a traditional guayo from Caribbean artisans. A modern metal grater can be consecrated too, if it is the one you actually use.

How to Use: Use it to grate cassava for Cassava Thanksgiving and for any bread offered to the Zemí. Before each use, touch it to your forehead and say: "Let what I make here feed and not harm."

Care Requirements: Wash and dry it completely after every use; a damp grater rots. Once a year, at the Harvest Moon, set it on the altar with a piece of the first bread it made that season.

Game Effect: Cassava bread made with a consecrated grater counts as a sincere offering to Yúcahu and Baibrama without a roll. Once per session, a meal made with it allows everyone who eats to clear one box of Strain.

### 7. Hurricane Charm

A small protective amulet worn or hung in the home from the beginning of hurricane season until its end. Traditionally it is a carved image of Guabancex with her arms swept in a spiral—the shape of the storm—or a spiral cut into a disc of stone, shell, or wood.

How to Obtain: Carve or draw the spiral yourself, or receive one from a Caracol Keeper. They are often given as gifts to new households before their first storm season.

How to Use: Consecrate it at the start of the season with tobacco smoke and a promise to Guabancex: "I will prepare. I will help my neighbors. I will rebuild." Wear it or hang it above the main door.

Care Requirements: At the end of the season, thank it and take it down. If a storm struck and you survived, bury the old charm and make a new one next year. Its work is done.

Game Effect: While wearing a consecrated Hurricane Charm, a Seeker may reroll one failed roll during a storm-related scene per session. The charm does not prevent storms; it helps you survive them.

### 8. River Stone

A stone taken, with permission, from a river you have built relationship with. In Borikén, Bohíques often carry a stone from El Yunque's streams. In our world, removing stones, plants, or anything else from El Yunque National Forest and other protected lands is prohibited—so take your stone from a river you know personally, on land where it is allowed.

How to Obtain: Visit the river many times first. When you are ready, offer tobacco and ask the river to show you a stone. Pick up only the one that is offered. Leave tobacco in its place.

How to Use: Hold it during River Blessing, River Memory, or any work with water Zemí when you cannot be at the river itself. It carries the river's voice.

Care Requirements: Return it to running water once a season—any clean running water—for one night, then bring it home again. If you ever move far away, consider returning it to its river.

Game Effect: A Seeker holding their consecrated River Stone may use River Memory or perform a River Blessing at a distance from the river it came from, with -1 to any roll.

### 9. Darkness Stone

Some practitioners speak of cave crystals, but you must never break formations from a living cave—the stone took thousands of years to grow, and the cave Zemí do not forgive it. The Darkness Stone is the right way to carry cave power home: a stone of your own, usually quartz or dark river stone, that you bring into a cave and leave in the dark.

How to Obtain: Choose a stone that already belongs to you. During a Cave Oracle, ask the cave Zemí whether it may rest in their darkness. If they agree, leave it in a safe nook for one full moon (or, if returning is not possible, keep it in a dark, sealed box at home for a month).

How to Use: Hold it in your closed hand during oracle work, dream work, or death work. Its darkness helps you hear.

Care Requirements: Keep it wrapped in dark cloth. Never leave it in sunlight. Wash it in salt water after it has been used for death work.

Game Effect: When holding the Darkness Stone, a Seeker may perform the Cave Oracle without being in a cave, using a dark room instead, with no penalty.

### 10. Petroglyph Drawing

A copy of ancestor carvings, made by hand. Older books sometimes describe making rubbings by pressing paper against petroglyphs, but in our world rubbing damages ancient carvings and is not allowed at protected sites. Draw them instead—from sight, from photographs, or from approved reproductions.

How to Obtain: Visit a petroglyph site (or study good photographs). Make a tobacco offering. Ask permission. Then draw the symbols slowly, by hand, on paper or cloth, feeling each line as the ancestors must have felt it while carving.

How to Use: Hang it near your altar or keep it in your notebook. Use it as the focus for Petroglyph Reading when you cannot visit the site itself.

Care Requirements: Keep it clean and dry. Never sell it. If you give it away, give it to someone who will honor it.

Game Effect: A Seeker may perform Petroglyph Reading using their own drawing at -1 instead of needing to be at the site. A Seeker with the Petroglyph Reader Attunement suffers no penalty.

### 11. Batey Ball (Batú)

The rubber ball used in the sacred game. In the Taíno world it was made from the latex of local trees, solid and heavy, and it was struck only with hips, shoulders, and head. A ball used in many sacred games becomes a vessel in its own right.

How to Obtain: Make one from natural rubber if you have the skill, or buy a solid rubber ball of the right weight. The first time it is used, it should be in a game played with offerings at the batey.

How to Use: Bring it to every sacred game. Before play, the captains hold it together and make a tobacco offering. After play, it rests on the batey altar overnight.

Care Requirements: Keep it out of direct sun when not in use. Clean it with water only. Retire it with honor when it splits, burying it at the edge of the batey.

Game Effect: When a batu game is played with a consecrated ball to resolve a dispute, both sides must accept the outcome, and the Guide treats the result as the true will of the Zemí.

### 12. Areyto Drum

The drum that carries the areyto. In the Taíno world, ceremonial drums—including the mayohuacán, a hollow log slit drum—set the rhythm of the sacred dance. Any drum can become an areyto drum through consecration and use.

How to Obtain: Make one or commission one from a drum maker. A slit drum carved from a fallen log is traditional. A hand drum works.

How to Use: Consecrate it by playing it at its first areyto. Before each ceremony, offer it tobacco and a sip of water, and tell it what the night is for.

Care Requirements: Keep it away from extreme heat and damp. Play it regularly—an unplayed drum goes to sleep. Never use it for anything you would be ashamed of.

Game Effect: When an Areyto Leader plays a consecrated drum, participants clear an additional box of Imbalance beyond the Attunement's usual effect.

### 13. Guatiao Token

The matching objects exchanged during the Guatiao Oath—the cord halves, or a pair of carved beads, stones, or shells chosen by the oath-siblings. In the old accounts, those who entered guatiao exchanged names. In modern Borikén, they exchange tokens.

How to Obtain: Made or chosen during the Guatiao Oath ceremony. They cannot be bought ready-made.

How to Use: Carry it, wear it, or keep it on your altar. When you need to reach your Guatiao across distance, hold the token and speak their name.

Care Requirements: Offer it tobacco on the anniversary of the oath. If the bond is ever formally unbound, both tokens are buried together.

Game Effect: A Seeker holding their Guatiao Token always knows if their Guatiao is in danger. Once per session, they may send a single feeling—warning, comfort, urgency—to their Guatiao, who receives it clearly.

### 14. Navigator's Star Map

A chart of the night sky and the sea between the islands, made by a navigator for their own use. It shows the rising and setting points of guide stars, the swell patterns, the paths of birds at dusk, and the currents that carry canoes between islands.

How to Obtain: You must make it yourself, over many nights of watching the sky and many days on the water. A teacher can help you start, but the map must be in your own hand.

How to Use: Study it before every crossing. Carry it in a waterproof wrap. Before departure, make a tobacco offering over it to Agüeybaná and the sea Zemí.

Care Requirements: Update it whenever you learn something new. Keep it dry. When you retire from the sea, give it to an apprentice.

Game Effect: A navigator with their own Star Map gains +1 to all navigation rolls and may cross the Mona Passage without the Guide adding complications for unfamiliarity.

### 15. Fishing Blessing Net

A net consecrated to Agüeybaná, used by fishing families to ensure a good catch and a safe return. It is not necessarily the net they fish with every day—sometimes it is a small ceremonial net kept on the boat altar.

How to Obtain: Weave it yourself, or have an elder of a fishing family make it for you. Consecrate it at the shore at dawn with tobacco, rum poured into the waves, and the first fish of the next catch returned to the sea.

How to Use: Keep it on the boat. Touch it before you leave the dock and again when you return. Speak to Agüeybaná each time.

Care Requirements: Rinse it in fresh water after each trip. Mend it immediately if it tears. Never use it to take more than you need.

Game Effect: A boat carrying a consecrated Fishing Blessing Net always returns home if it is humanly possible. The Guide may still impose hardship, but not loss of the boat or crew, unless the fishers have broken faith with the sea.

## Closing Teaching

These rituals and implements are not museum pieces. They are living practices.

You are meant to DO them. To try them. To fail, learn, improve, try again.

Some will speak to you immediately. Some will feel awkward or strange until you've practiced them many times. Some will never quite fit, and that's fine—follow the practices that resonate, respect the rest.

The key is consistency. One tobacco offering is nice. One hundred tobacco offerings build relationship. One River Blessing feels good. Ten River Blessings teach you how water actually works.

Do the practices. Trust the process. Let the Zemí guide you.

We return to the root.

Ase. Ase. Ase.

# CHAPTER 5: BORIKÉN AS LIVING LAND

## The Island Speaks

Before we talk about specific mountains or rivers or caves, I need you to understand something fundamental:

Borikén is not just a place. Borikén is a person.

The island is alive. Conscious. Aware of every footstep, every prayer, every offering made on her soil. She has moods—generous some days, fierce others. She has preferences—she welcomes some people immediately and makes others work to earn her trust. She has memory—holding stories from before humans arrived, from the first Taíno who paddled their canoes to her shores, from every generation since.

When you walk through Borikén, you are not walking through landscape. You are walking through a living body. The mountains are her bones. The rivers are her blood. The rainforest is her lungs, breathing oxygen into the world. The caves are her deep thoughts, her unconscious mind, the places where she dreams.

And she knows you. She feels you. She responds to how you treat her.

This chapter is a map of sacred sites—places where the boundary between worlds grows thin, where the Zemí are most accessible, where transformation happens more easily. But it is not a tourist guide. This is not "top ten places to visit in Puerto Rico."

This is an introduction to the living geography of a conscious island. This is me teaching you how to walk respectfully through her body, how to listen when she speaks, how to build relationship with the land itself.

Each site I describe has multiple dimensions: the physical reality (what you will see with your eyes), the spiritual reality (what Zemí dwell there, what energies are present), and the practical reality (how to approach, what offerings to make, what you can learn).

Pay attention to all three. Miss any one and you miss the fullness of the place.

Let's begin at the heart—the mountain that holds the island's greatest power.

## El Yunque: The Rainforest Cathedral

You have already met the Zemí of El Yunque in Chapter 3. Now I will teach you about the actual place—the physical mountain, the ecological reality, the experience of walking through the most sacred forest in all of Borikén.

### The Physical Reality

El Yunque is a mountain range in the northeast of the island—28,000 acres of rainforest, rising from sea level to peaks over 3,500 feet. It is the only tropical rainforest in the U.S. National Forest system, though in our timeline (without colonization) it is simply called El Yunque National Reserve, managed by the Taíno government with deep respect for its spiritual significance.

It rains here. Constantly. The mountain creates its own weather—warm air rises from the coast, hits the peaks, cools, condenses, falls as rain. Some parts of El Yunque receive 240 inches of rain per year. You will get wet. Accept this.

The forest is layered—Tabonuco forest at lower elevations (named for the massive tabonuco trees whose resin was used for torches), Palo Colorado forest in the middle (harder woods, cooler temperatures), Cloud forest at the peaks (perpetual mist, moss covering everything, trees stunted by constant wind).

The sound is constant—rain on leaves, water running over rocks, the coquíes singing their two-note chorus, the calls of the Puerto Rican parrot (cotorra), the rustle of movement as lizards and snakes navigate the understory.

There are waterfalls everywhere. La Mina Falls is the most accessible—a 35-foot cascade into a pool where people swim (with permission). But there are hundreds more, some without names, some known only to the Bohíques who train here.

The trails are well-maintained in the lower sections—stone paths, wooden stairs, interpretive signs (in Taíno and Spanish and English). But the deeper you go, the wilder it gets. Some areas have no trails at all. Some areas the Zemí have declared off-limits. Respect these boundaries.

### The Spiritual Reality

El Yunque is a temple. Not a building with walls and roof, but a living cathedral where every tree is a column, every waterfall an altar, every bird a prayer.

The Zemí here are ancient. They remember when the island first rose from the sea. They remember the first humans. They remember every ceremony, every offering, every sincere prayer spoken in their presence.

The forest spirit (which we call El Yunque Zemí, though it has other names in older languages) is vast, patient, and utterly without mercy for disrespect. It welcomes those who come with open hearts. It tests those who come with arrogance. And it expels—gently but firmly—those who come to take without giving, to photograph without honoring, to consume without gratitude.

Specific Zemí dwell in specific places:

The Waterfall Spirits live in the cascades—each major waterfall has its own personality. La Mina is playful, welcoming, good with children. La Coca (further up the mountain) is more serious, better for solitary vision work. The unnamed falls deep in the interior are fierce, dangerous, only for advanced practitioners.

The Tree Elders are the oldest tabonuco and palo colorado trees—some over 1,000 years old. These are not just plants. These are conscious beings who have watched empires rise and fall (metaphorically—remember, no colonization in this timeline, but the trees have still witnessed vast change). You can speak to them. They will answer, though slowly. Tree time is not human time.

The Parrot Collective is the spiritual presence of all the Puerto Rican parrots who live in the forest. These birds are sacred—intelligent, social, beautiful. When you see them (rare, but possible), it is a blessing. When you hear their calls echoing through the canopy, the Zemí are telling you something. Pay attention.

The River Mothers are the spirits of the streams that run down from the peaks. Each one carries messages from the mountain to the sea. You can ask them questions by floating offerings downstream and watching where they go, what they catch on, how the water moves around them.

### How to Approach

Do not enter El Yunque casually. This is not a day trip. This is pilgrimage.

**Preparation:**

- Make tobacco offering before you enter. At the main entrance, at the trailhead, wherever you begin—stop, take out tobacco, say who you are and why you've come. Ask permission.

- Bring water, but plan to drink from the streams (the water is clean, pure, blessed). Bring snacks, but plan to leave offerings—scatter cornmeal, leave cassava bread, pour rum at the base of a great tree.

- Wear appropriate clothing. You will get muddy. You will get soaked. Dress for it.

- Tell someone where you're going and when you'll return. The forest is generally safe, but people do get lost. The Zemí sometimes disorient those who need a lesson in humility.

**During the Visit:**

- Walk slowly. This is not a hike to burn calories or prove your fitness. This is meditation in motion. Feel each step. Notice everything.

- Speak to the forest. Out loud if you're comfortable, silently if not. Tell El Yunque what you're experiencing—"The rain is cold," "This tree is beautiful," "I'm scared of the height." The forest appreciates honesty.

- Make offerings along the way. Tobacco at stream crossings. Flowers at waterfalls. A strand of your hair left at the base of an ancient tree (DNA is powerful offering—you are literally giving part of yourself).

- If you encounter animals, speak to them. Thank them for allowing you to share their home. If they seem agitated or warning you away, listen. They know this place better than you do.

- Do not take anything without asking permission first. Want a leaf for medicine? Ask the plant. Want a stone from the stream? Ask the water. If you feel resistance, leave it. If you feel permission, take it and leave tobacco in exchange.

**Before You Leave:**

- Final offering at the exit. Thank El Yunque for allowing you to visit, for whatever you experienced, for keeping you safe.

- Promise to return. The forest values relationship. One visit is introduction. Many visits build friendship.

### What You Can Learn Here

El Yunque teaches:

- Patience. Everything in the rainforest moves at its own pace. Rushing accomplishes nothing.

- Adaptation. The plants here have learned to thrive in constant rain, poor soil, high winds. They show you how to work with conditions rather than fighting them.

- Interconnection. The rainforest is a web—every plant, every animal, every fungus depends on every other. You cannot separate one element without affecting everything else.

- Sacred Listening. The forest is always speaking—in bird calls, in water sounds, in the rustle of leaves. Learning to hear these voices is learning to hear the Zemí everywhere.

### Plot Hooks for Mysteries

El Yunque is perfect for:

- Vision quests (Seekers go into the forest to fast, pray, receive guidance)

- Plant medicine gathering (healing herbs grow here, but only with permission)

- Lost children (someone wanders off the trail and needs rescue—but the forest is hiding them for a reason)

- Sacred site desecration (developers want to cut trees, build roads—the community must decide how to respond)

- Parrot protection (poachers threatening the cotorra population)

- Water pollution (something upstream is poisoning the rivers—track the source and stop it)

## The Central Mountains: Backbone of the Island

If El Yunque is the heart, the Cordillera Central—the central mountain range—is the spine. These mountains run east to west across the length of Borikén, creating the island's distinctive shape, dividing north from south, holding the history of the people in their stone.

### The Physical Reality

The Cordillera peaks at Cerro de Punta (4,390 feet), the highest point in Borikén. But the range includes dozens of other peaks, each with its own name, its own character, its own stories.

The mountains are steep. Roads wind through them in hairpin curves. Small towns cling to hillsides—Jayuya, Utuado, Adjuntas, Orocovis. These are farming communities, primarily coffee and plantains and root vegetables grown on terraces carved into the slopes.

The climate is cooler here than on the coast. Mist rises in the mornings. Rain comes suddenly, fierce, then clears. Nights can actually be cold (relative to tropical standards—you might need a light jacket).

The rock is ancient—volcanic in some areas, limestone in others. Caves honeycomb the mountains. Some are accessible, mapped, used for ceremony. Others are unexplored, dangerous, sacred.

### The Spiritual Reality

The mountain Zemí here are different from El Yunque. El Yunque is rainforest spirit—lush, wet, alive with constant growth. The Cordillera spirits are rock and height and endurance. They teach different lessons.

Cerro de Punta (the highest peak) is where you go when you need perspective. When your problems feel overwhelming, when you cannot see the pattern, when you need to rise above the immediate—you climb Cerro de Punta. The Zemí there grants vision from height. You see farther, wider, clearer.

The Coffee Mountains (around Jayuya and Utuado) are where the Naborías train in agricultural wisdom. The Zemí here know soil, know shade, know how to coax food from steep slopes. They teach patience and attention to small things.

The Cave Systems (especially around Río Camuy—we'll discuss this in detail next) are gateways to Coaybay. The mountain Zemí guard these entrances. They decide who enters, who stays out, who returns.

### The Mountain Towns

These communities deserve special attention because they preserve Taíno culture most purely. San Juan is cosmopolitan, influenced by the world. But up in the mountains, change comes slowly. The old ways remain.

Jayuya is the center of Bohíque training. The House of Yúcahu (one of the three major training centers) is here. Young people come from across the island to apprentice, to learn agricultural spirituality, to study plant medicine. The town holds major festivals at planting and harvest times—everyone participates, even visitors. If you want to learn authentic Taíno ceremonial practice, spend time in Jayuya.
Utuado is known for its artisans. Carvers, weavers, potters—people who make sacred implements with their hands. The best trigonolitos come from Utuado. The most beautiful cohoba stools. If you need ritual objects, this is where you go.

Adjuntas is the meditation town. Quiet. Contemplative. Many retirees, many healers, many people who have finished the busy phase of life and now focus on depth. There are more altars per capita in Adjuntas than anywhere else in Borikén. The town itself feels like a temple.

### How to Approach

Visiting the mountains requires different preparation than visiting El Yunque:

- Bring warmer clothes. Even in summer, mountain nights are cool.

- Drive carefully. The roads are steep and winding. Don't rush. Arrive alive.

- Respect the communities. These are people's homes, not tourist attractions. Ask permission before photographing. Offer to buy something if you visit a workshop. Contribute to the economy.

- Make offerings at the peaks. If you climb Cerro de Punta or any significant mountain, leave tobacco at the summit. Thank the Zemí for the view, for the lesson, for the safety.

### What You Can Learn Here

The mountains teach:

- Endurance. Climbing teaches you that you can do more than you think, that pain is temporary, that the view from the top is worth the effort.

- Humility. Mountains make humans feel small. This is good. We need to remember our scale.

- Community. Mountain towns survive through cooperation. When roads wash out, neighbors help. When crops fail, everyone shares. This is the Taíno way made visible.

### Plot Hooks for Mysteries

The mountains are perfect for:

- Coming-of-age rituals (young people proving themselves through endurance challenges)

- Lost hikers (search and rescue, but complicated by Zemí interference)

- Land disputes (who owns which terraces, who has rights to which water)

- Coffee blight (spiritual or biological problem destroying crops)

- Cave exploration (discovering new passages, finding ancestor artifacts)

- Weather working (petitioning the mountain Zemí to bring rain during drought)

## Río Camuy: The Underworld River

Of all the sacred sites in Borikén, Río Camuy might be the most powerful and the most dangerous. This is a river that runs mostly underground, carving massive caves through the limestone, creating a literal pathway to Coaybay.

### The Physical Reality

Río Camuy is the third-largest underground river in the world. It runs for miles beneath the surface, creating the Camuy River Cave Park—one of the most extensive cave systems in the hemisphere.

The caves are enormous. Cathedral-sized chambers. Passages you could drive a truck through. Sinkholes that drop hundreds of feet. Stalactites and stalagmites that have grown for millennia.

The river itself is sometimes visible—flowing through huge caverns where you can stand on ledges and watch it pass. Sometimes it disappears completely, running through passages too small or too deep for humans to follow.

The temperature in the caves is constant—cool (around 70°F), humid, dark. The silence is profound when the river is distant. The roar is deafening when it's close.

There are bats. Thousands of them. Hanging from the ceilings, flying through the chambers at dusk and dawn. They are sacred here. Do not disturb them.

### The Spiritual Reality

Río Camuy is a threshold. One of the primary gateways between Atabey (the realm of the living) and Coaybay (the realm of the dead).

The river itself is a Zemí—ancient, powerful, utterly indifferent to human concerns. It flows. It carves. It takes things down into darkness and sometimes brings them back changed.

The Cave Entrance Spirits guard access. Not all caves are open to all people. Some are for Bohíques only. Some are for those in mourning. Some are forbidden entirely. When you approach, you will feel whether you are welcome. Trust that feeling.

Maquetaurie Guayaba (the lord of the dead) is accessible here. Not present in the way he is in deep Coaybay, but close. If you need to speak with him—if you are doing death work, guiding spirits, consulting with the ancestors—Río Camuy is where you come.

The Bat Collective is the spiritual presence of all the bats who live in the caves. They are messengers between worlds. They navigate darkness perfectly. They teach echolocation—not physical, but spiritual. How to sense what you cannot see.

The Stone Singers are the formations themselves—stalactites, stalagmites, flowstone, all of it. When water drips, when wind moves through the chambers, the caves sing. Low frequencies, barely audible, but you feel them in your chest. This is the stone remembering, the stone speaking, the stone teaching.

### How to Approach

Do not enter Río Camuy caves alone unless you are trained. Seriously. People die in caves. The darkness is absolute. The passages are confusing. The drops are deadly.

If you are going as part of an organized tour (which the Taíno government offers), you are relatively safe. Guides know the routes, carry lights, understand the protocols.

If you are going for spiritual work (not tourism), you need a Bohíque guide or explicit permission from the cave Zemí:

**Before entering:**

- Substantial tobacco offering. Not just a pinch—a handful, burned at the cave mouth.

- Rum poured into the earth. The dead appreciate rum.

- Statement of purpose. Why are you entering? What do you seek? Speak clearly so the Zemí can decide whether to allow you.

**During the visit:**

- Carry light, but also practice darkness. Sit in the cave with your light off for at least a few minutes. Let your eyes adjust. Let the darkness teach you.

- Speak softly. The caves amplify sound, and the Zemí appreciate quiet respect.

- Do not touch the formations unnecessarily. Oils from human skin damage the stone. Look, but don't handle.

- If you see bones, do not disturb them. The caves have been used for burial for centuries. The dead rest here. Let them.

**When leaving:**

- Final offering. Thank the caves for allowing you to enter, for whatever you experienced, for keeping you safe.

- Cleanse yourself. Before you return to normal life, wash in a river or the ocean. Cave energy clings. You need to release it.

### What You Can Learn Here

Río Camuy teaches:

- Comfort with darkness. Not all knowing comes from light. Some wisdom requires sitting in the dark and waiting.

- Acceptance of death. The caves remind you that death is real, inevitable, and not the enemy. The dead rest peacefully here. You will too, someday.

- Deep time. The formations took millions of years to grow. The river has been carving for longer than humans have existed. Your problems are temporary. The stone endures.

### Plot Hooks for Mysteries

Río Camuy is perfect for:

- Death vigils (bringing the newly dead to the cave for final goodbye)

- Ancestor consultation (speaking to the dead in the place where they dwell most accessibly)

- Lost spelunkers (rescue mission complicated by spiritual issues)

- Cave desecration (someone stealing formations or disturbing burial sites)

- Oracle work (the caves reveal the future to those who sit in darkness long enough)

- Bat sickness (pollution or disease affecting the bat population, which disrupts the ecosystem and the spiritual balance)

## The Seven Sacred Waterfalls

Scattered across Borikén are seven waterfalls that the Bohíques have designated as particularly sacred. Each one serves different purposes. Each one has different Zemí. Each one offers different teachings.

I will not give you their exact locations—some knowledge must be earned through relationship, not handed over in books. But I will tell you their names and their natures so you know what to seek.

### 1. La Mina (The Mine) - Waterfall of Joy

Located in El Yunque, easily accessible, this is the beginner's waterfall. The pool at its base is safe for swimming. The energy is playful, welcoming, good for children and those new to water work.

What it teaches: Simple happiness. Sometimes spiritual work doesn't have to be serious. Sometimes you just need to jump in the water and laugh.

Best for: Cleansing depression, reconnecting with childlike wonder, community celebration.

### 2. La Coca (The Coconut) - Waterfall of Vision

Higher up in El Yunque, harder to reach, this waterfall is powerful and focused. The mist creates rainbows. The sound is constant, hypnotic.

What it teaches: Clarity of sight. The ability to see through illusion, to perceive what is real beneath what appears.

Best for: Decision-making, oracle work, seeing hidden truths.

### 3. Salto Collazo (Collazo Falls) - Waterfall of Healing

In the central mountains, this waterfall has specific healing properties. The water is rich in minerals. The Zemí here specializes in physical healing—broken bones, chronic pain, illness.

What it teaches: The body's wisdom. How to listen to physical pain and understand its message.

Best for: Illness recovery, pain management, learning herbalism.

### 4. Gozalandia (Joylandia) - Twin Waterfalls of Balance

These are actually two falls feeding the same pool—one masculine, one feminine. Together they teach balance, complementarity, the dance of opposites.

What it teaches: Integration. How to hold seeming contradictions in tension without forcing resolution.

Best for: Working through dualities (logic and intuition, strength and gentleness, etc.), relationship healing.

### 5. Cascada La Plata (Silver Cascade) - Waterfall of Abundance

This waterfall is associated with Yúcahu. The water runs over rocks that sometimes glitter in the sun (hence "silver"). The Zemí here grants material blessings to those who approach with right intention.

What it teaches: Reciprocity. Abundance flows when you give as much as you receive.

Best for: Financial difficulties, career challenges, learning generosity.

### 6. Salto Curet (Curet Falls) - Waterfall of Courage

A powerful, dangerous waterfall. The drop is significant. The current is strong. This is where warriors come to face their fears.

What it teaches: Bravery. Not the absence of fear, but action despite fear.

Best for: Confronting trauma, preparing for difficult challenges, warrior initiation.

### 7. Charco Azul (Blue Pool) - Waterfall of Dreams

The water here is an impossible blue—turquoise, crystalline, almost unreal. The Zemí here speaks through dreams. Many people fall asleep on the rocks and receive visions.

What it teaches: Trust in the unconscious. Dreams know things the waking mind cannot access.

Best for: Dream work, vision quests, connecting with ancestors who speak through dreams.

## The Beaches: Where Land Meets Ocean

Borikén is an island. Every direction eventually leads to coast. And the beaches—those liminal spaces where earth meets water—are profoundly sacred.

### The North Coast Beaches

The Atlantic side. Rougher water, bigger waves, stronger currents. These are the beaches for serious swimmers, for surfers, for those who need the ocean's fierce teaching.

Playa Luquillo is the most accessible, the most family-friendly. Protected by a reef, the water is calmer here. Good for children, for those new to ocean work.

Ocean Park (San Juan) is urban beach, city beach, where young people gather to play volleyball, to surf, to socialize. The Zemí here appreciate community, laughter, connection.

Playa Jobos (Isabela) is surf beach. The waves are powerful. The surfers who ride them are engaged in moving meditation, in reading water, in conversation with ocean Zemí.

### The South Coast Beaches

The Caribbean side. Gentler water, warmer temperatures, different energy entirely.

Playa Flamenco (on Culebra island) is consistently rated one of the most beautiful beaches in the world. White sand, turquoise water, sea turtles swimming close to shore. The energy here is rest. Pure rest. The beach says: you do not need to do anything. You can simply be.

La Parguera (southwestern coast) is famous for bioluminescent bay. At night, when the water is disturbed, it glows with blue-green light—microscopic organisms (dinoflagellates) producing bioluminescence. This is sacred. This is the ocean revealing its magic. Swimming in glowing water is baptism, is reminder that the world is more mysterious than we usually acknowledge.

### The East Coast - Border Waters

The eastern coast faces the passage between Borikén and the smaller islands (Vieques, Culebra) and beyond them, the open Atlantic. This is border territory. This is where Taíno and Carib sometimes meet.

The energy here is more tense, more complex. The ocean is beautiful but also dangerous. Friendly some days, treacherous others.

Playa Seven Seas is departure point for boats heading to Vieques and Culebra. The Zemí here understand transition, journey, leaving and returning. Make offerings here before any significant travel.

### How to Approach the Ocean

**Before entering the water:**

- Offer tobacco. Stand at the water's edge and speak to the ocean Zemí—introduce yourself, state your intention, ask permission.

- Wade in slowly. Do not rush. Let the water greet you. Let it touch your feet, your legs, your waist. Feel how it responds.

**While in the water:**

- Respect the currents. If the ocean is pulling you somewhere, there is a reason. Either go with it or get out—do not fight.

- Dive beneath the surface at least once. Immerse completely. This is baptism, is cleansing, is acknowledgment that the ocean is bigger than you.

- Speak to the water. Tell it what you need. Ask it to take what you no longer want to carry. The ocean, like rivers, can cleanse—but on a vaster scale.

**When leaving:**

- Thank the ocean. Out loud. Simple gratitude.

- Leave something behind—a flower, a shell, tobacco buried in the sand.

### What the Ocean Teaches

The ocean teaches:

- Vastness. Your problems, your life, your entire civilization—all of it is a grain of sand compared to the ocean's depth and age.

- Movement. The ocean is never still. Waves come and go. Tides rise and fall. This is the rhythm of existence.

- Surrender. You cannot control the ocean. You can only learn to move with it.

### Plot Hooks for Mysteries

Beaches and ocean work are perfect for:

- Rescue missions (swimmer in trouble, boat capsized)

- Pollution crises (oil spill, plastic waste, chemical contamination)

- Border conflicts (Carib boats appearing on the horizon)

- Sea turtle protection (poachers, habitat destruction)

- Storm preparation (hurricane approaching, community must secure the coast)

- Bioluminescent bay healing (the glow is fading, something is wrong)

## Vieques and Culebra: The Sister Islands

Off the eastern coast of Borikén lie two smaller islands—Vieques and Culebra. These are autonomous in their own right but deeply connected to the main island. They have their own character, their own Zemí, their own teachings.

### Vieques: The Wild Sister

Vieques is larger, more populated. It has farms, towns, beautiful beaches on both coasts.

In our timeline (without colonization), Vieques was never used for military bombing practice. Instead, it developed as agricultural center—farming, fishing, small-scale tourism focused on natural beauty and spiritual retreat.

The Zemí of Vieques are wild. Less touched by human development, less tamed by civilization. The horses that run free on the beaches (yes, wild horses—descendants of Spanish horses that were never brought here in this timeline, but rather horses traded with mainland Americas over centuries). The iguanas that sun themselves on rocks. The frigatebirds circling overhead.

Vieques teaches wildness. Teaches that not everything needs to be controlled or understood. Teaches that sometimes you need to run free, to follow instinct, to remember you are an animal.

### Culebra: The Gentle Sister

Culebra is smaller, quieter, more focused on sea turtle conservation and coral reef protection.

The beaches here are among the most beautiful in the Caribbean. The water is so clear you can see the bottom thirty feet down. The snorkeling is extraordinary—fish in every color, coral gardens, sea turtles gliding past.

Culebra teaches gentleness. Teaches that beauty does not need to be dramatic or overwhelming. Teaches the power of softness, of simplicity, of being small and perfect rather than large and impressive.

### How to Visit the Sister Islands

You take the ferry from Fajardo (or fly in small planes if you have money). The journey itself is sacred—crossing water, leaving mainland, entering different space.

Make offerings before you board. Ask the sea Zemí—Agüeybaná above all, whom we discussed in Chapter 3—for safe crossing.

Once on the islands, follow the same protocols as the main island—tobacco, respect, listening. But expect different responses. These Zemí are not the same as mainland Zemí.

### What the Islands Teach

Vieques and Culebra teach:

- Independence. Small does not mean weak. Isolated does not mean lesser.

- Uniqueness. Every place has its own character. You cannot impose mainland expectations on island reality.

- Protection. Island ecosystems are fragile. They require active care, constant vigilance.

## The Urban Sacred: San Juan as Spiritual Center

I have talked mostly about natural sites—mountains, rivers, caves, beaches. But sacred space is not only wilderness. Sacred space can also be urban, human-made, built with intention and blessed with centuries of prayer.

San Juan, the capital, has its own sacred geography.

### The Grand Batey

In the center of Viejo San Juan (old city) sits the Grand Batey—the ceremonial ball court where the sacred games are played, where the Council of Elders meets, where the Cacica Supreme addresses the people.

This batey is ancient—originally built 800 years ago, rebuilt and expanded many times, but always on the same ground. The stones themselves hold memory.

The Zemí here is the Batey Spirit—the collective consciousness of every game played, every oath sworn, every decision made on this ground.

When you stand in the Grand Batey, you stand in the center of Taíno governance, Taíno sport, Taíno ceremony. All of it happens here.

### The University of Borikén

Just east of the old city, the University sprawls across hundreds of acres—libraries, labs, classrooms, dormitories, gardens.

This is where traditional knowledge and modern research meet. Where Bohíques teach alongside biologists. Where students learn to honor the Zemí while also mastering engineering and medicine and law.

The University Zemí (which we discussed in Chapter 3) dwells in the main library—a massive building holding millions of books, countless research papers, the accumulated knowledge of Taíno civilization.

### The Harbor

San Juan Harbor is where the island meets the world. Ships come and go. Goods are loaded and unloaded. People arrive from distant places, carrying stories, bringing change.

The Harbor Zemí appreciates this flow, this constant movement, this mixing of cultures and ideas.

### San Cristóbal Fort

On the northern edge of the old city sits a massive stone fort—built not for colonial defense (because no colonization), but rather as protection against potential pirate raids and as ceremonial watchtower.
