The fort has never seen battle (pirates learned quickly that raiding Taíno communities was more trouble than it was worth). Instead, it serves as meditation space, as place where people go to think about protection, boundaries, defense without aggression.

### How to Navigate Urban Sacred Space

Cities are different from forests, different from mountains, different from beaches. The protocols shift:

- Respect human space. In wilderness, you are guest in the Zemís' home. In cities, you share space with other humans. Both matter.

- Quiet offerings. You cannot burn tobacco in the middle of a busy street. But you can carry tobacco in your pocket, can whisper prayers, can make internal offerings. The Zemí understand urban constraints.

- Find the pockets of peace. Even in San Juan, there are quiet corners—small plazas, hidden gardens, early morning streets before crowds arrive. These are where urban Zemí are most accessible.

### What Cities Teach

Urban sacred sites teach:

- Adaptation. Spirituality does not require wilderness. It requires intention.

- Community. Cities are where people gather, collaborate, create together. This is sacred work.

- Complexity. Modern life is complicated. The Zemí understand this. They do not demand simplicity. They demand integrity within complexity.

## Closing Teaching: How to Walk the Land

You now have a map. You know where the sacred sites are, what Zemí dwell in each, how to approach with respect.

But here is what I need you to understand:

Every place is sacred if you approach it correctly.

Yes, El Yunque is powerful. Yes, Río Camuy is a threshold. Yes, the beaches and the mountains and the caves hold concentrated spiritual energy.

But the tree in your backyard also has a Zemí. The stream behind your house also speaks. The rock in your garden also holds memory.

The sites I have listed in this chapter are training grounds. Places where the energy is strong enough that even beginners can feel it, where the Zemí are patient enough to teach those who are just learning.

But the goal is not to visit sacred sites forever. The goal is to become someone who recognizes the sacred everywhere, who can build relationship with any Zemí, who can make offerings in a parking lot or a kitchen and know that the gesture matters just as much as offerings made on mountaintops.

Learn from the land. Let Borikén teach you. And then carry those lessons into whatever landscape you actually inhabit.

The island is alive. But so is every place.

Remember this, and you will never walk alone.

We return to the root.

Ase. Ase. Ase.

# CHAPTER 6: MYSTERIES OF THE ISLAND

## How to Use These Mysteries

You hold in your hands ten complete adventures—ten Mysteries, in the language of the Temple of Gu game system. Each one is designed to be playable in one to three sessions, depending on how deeply your group explores the situation, how much they engage with the NPCs, how many complications arise.

These are not railroad adventures. There is no single "correct" solution. Each Mystery presents a problem, introduces the people involved, suggests complications that might arise, and offers multiple paths to resolution.

Your job as Guide is not to force the Seekers down a predetermined path. Your job is to present the situation honestly, embody the NPCs with full humanity, let the Seekers make choices, and then respond to those choices with realistic consequences.

Some of these Mysteries are gentle—opportunities for learning, relationship building, simple service. Some are dangerous—life-or-death situations requiring courage and quick thinking. Some are ethically complex—no clear right answer, only hard choices and consequences that must be lived with.

All of them are rooted in authentic Taíno spiritual practice. All of them teach real lessons about walking this path. And all of them can be adapted to your specific group, your specific setting, your specific needs.

Use them as written, or use them as inspiration. Change the details. Combine elements from multiple Mysteries. Make them your own.

The Zemí don't care about perfect adherence to my words. They care about sincere practice.

So let's begin.

## Mystery One: The Silent Stone

Complexity: 4-6 slices

Best For: Beginning Seekers learning about Zemí relationships

Themes: Communication, patience, humility, making amends

### The Hook

The Seekers are approached by an elderly woman named Marisol who maintains a small shrine in her home—a consecrated trigonolito that houses the Zemí of a local stream. For fifteen years, this stone has spoken to her daily, offering guidance, comfort, companionship. But three weeks ago, the stone went silent. No matter how much tobacco she offers, no matter how sincerely she prays, the Zemí will not respond.

Marisol is not just concerned—she is bereft. This relationship has sustained her through the death of her husband, through her children moving away, through the loneliness of old age. The silence feels like abandonment.

She asks the Seekers: "Please, find out why the Zemí has stopped speaking to me. Have I offended? Is something wrong with the stream? I cannot bear this silence."

### The Situation

The stream Zemí has not abandoned Marisol. But it is deeply upset, and its silence is intentional—not punishment exactly, but withdrawal until a problem is addressed.

Here's what happened:

Two months ago, a small development company began construction upstream—nothing huge, just three new houses being built on land that was sold by a farmer who needed money for medical bills. The construction crew, not from the area and not spiritually trained, dumped construction waste directly into the stream—concrete runoff, paint, chemical sealants.

The stream Zemí suffered immediate harm. The water became cloudy. Small fish died. Plants along the bank withered. The Zemí tried to communicate this to Marisol through the trigonolito, but Marisol didn't understand—she thought the stone was just having "off days" and didn't investigate.

Frustrated by being ignored, the stream Zemí withdrew completely. The silence is its way of saying: Pay attention. Something is wrong. Fix it.

### Key NPCs

**Marisol Rivera (67, retired teacher, Naboría)**

- Sincere, devout, a bit naive about environmental issues

- Deeply lonely since her husband died five years ago

- The trigonolito relationship is her primary source of spiritual comfort

- She wants the Seekers to "make the Zemí forgive her" without understanding that she's not the problem

**The Stream Zemí**

- Old, patient, but at the end of its patience

- Has been poisoned and ignored, and will not resume communication until the stream is cleaned and proper apologies are made

- Not cruel, but firm—this is teaching moment

**Tomás Vega (42, construction foreman)**

- Not malicious, just ignorant of spiritual protocols

- Under pressure to finish the houses on time and under budget

- Will be defensive if accused, but genuinely doesn't want to cause harm

- Can become an ally if approached correctly

**Don Esteban (81, the farmer who sold the land)**

- Desperate—he sold land his family held for generations because his wife needed expensive medical treatment

- Feels guilty about the sale but didn't know what else to do

- Knows the land was sacred but couldn't afford to keep it

### The Investigation

The Seekers need to discover:

1.  That the stream is polluted - This requires either:

    - Following the stream upstream from Marisol's shrine

    - Using River Memory Attunement to learn what the water remembers

    - Consulting other water Zemí in the area who might explain

2.  What caused the pollution - This requires:

    - Finding the construction site

    - Examining the waste being dumped

    - Talking to workers or neighbors

3.  Why it hasn't been addressed - This requires:

    - Understanding that Tomás doesn't know about Zemí

    - Learning that Don Esteban sold the land out of desperation

    - Recognizing that this is systemic problem, not individual villainy

### Complications

The Workers Are Skeptical

Tomás and his crew are not Taíno—they're from San Juan, city people, not spiritually trained. When the Seekers try to explain about the stream Zemí being harmed, they might laugh it off as superstition. This requires the Seekers to either:

- Demonstrate the spiritual reality in ways the workers can't dismiss

- Find secular arguments (environmental law, community relations, property values)

- Bring in a Bohíque or respected elder who can speak with authority

The Houses Are Almost Finished

Stopping construction now would be expensive. The company has contracts, deadlines, buyers waiting. Tomás might agree the pollution was wrong but argue that stopping won't undo the damage—why not finish the houses and clean the stream after?

The Seekers must decide: Is this acceptable? Or does the stream Zemí require that construction stop immediately as demonstration of respect?

Don Esteban's Guilt

When the Seekers track down who sold the land, they find an old man wracked with guilt, sitting vigil at his wife's bedside (she's in hospice, dying slowly). He weeps when he learns what's happened to the stream. He offers to buy the land back—but he has no money. All he can offer is his labor, his service, his public apology.

How do the Seekers balance justice with compassion?

Marisol's Impatience

While the Seekers are investigating upstream, Marisol keeps asking: "Can't you just make it speak to me again? Can't you perform some ritual?" She doesn't understand that this isn't about her relationship with the trigonolito—it's about the stream's health. Teaching her this without dismissing her pain is delicate work.

### Resolution Paths

Path One: Full Restoration

The Seekers convince Tomás to stop construction, hire environmental cleanup crew, and remove all pollutants from the stream. Don Esteban performs public apology ceremony at the stream's edge. Marisol makes massive offering to the stream Zemí—not just tobacco, but commitment to become guardian of this waterway, to monitor it, to ensure this never happens again.

The stream Zemí is satisfied. The water clears. Fish return. And the trigonolito speaks again—but the first thing it says is: "Now you are listening. Good."

Reward: Each Seeker clears 2 Imbalance. Marisol becomes ally who can be called upon for future water-related Mysteries. The stream Zemí grants +1 to any future River Blessing rituals performed in this area.

Path Two: Compromise

The Seekers negotiate middle ground: construction continues, but Tomás agrees to implement better waste management, to make offerings to the stream, to bring in a Bohíque to bless the land. The pollution is cleaned up (mostly), though not perfectly. Don Esteban cannot buy back the land, but he volunteers at the local environmental center as penance.

The stream Zemí accepts this—grudgingly. The trigonolito speaks again, but the Zemí is cool, reserved. The relationship is restored but not to its previous warmth. Marisol must work to rebuild trust.

Reward: Each Seeker clears 1 Imbalance. The stream Zemí is neutral toward them—not hostile, but not helpful either.

Path Three: Failure to Act

If the Seekers cannot or will not address the root problem—if they try to cajole the Zemí into speaking without fixing the pollution, if they give up when complications arise, if they side with the construction company purely for economic reasons—the stream Zemí remains silent.

Marisol is heartbroken. The stream continues to sicken. And the Seekers learn a hard lesson: you cannot maintain relationship with Zemí while ignoring their suffering.

Consequence: Each Seeker marks 1 Imbalance (Doubt—they failed to serve). Marisol loses faith in them. The stream Zemí will not work with any of them until significant amends are made.

### What This Mystery Teaches

**For Seekers:**

- Zemí relationships require more than personal devotion—they require environmental stewardship

- Silence is communication, and you must learn to hear what is not being said

- Many problems have no villains, only people making difficult choices under pressure

- Compassion and justice must be balanced, not chosen between

**For Guides:**

- Not every NPC is evil or good—most are complex, doing their best with limited options

- Environmental issues are spiritual issues in Taíno practice

- Let the Seekers struggle with the ethical complexity rather than presenting clear solutions

---

## Mystery Two: Hurricane's Test

Complexity: 6-8 slices

Best For: Intermediate Seekers ready for high stakes

Themes: Humility, community, preparation, interpreting divine will

### The Hook

Hurricane season. The Seekers are in a coastal town (your choice which—Luquillo, Isabela, anywhere vulnerable) when the weather reports start coming in: a massive storm is forming in the Atlantic. Category 4, possibly 5. Projected path: directly toward this town.

The community begins preparation—securing homes, stockpiling supplies, opening shelters. But there is disagreement about spiritual response:

Elder Benita (Bohíque, 73) believes this hurricane is Guabancex teaching a lesson. The community has become complacent, focused on tourism and money, neglecting the old practices. She wants to perform Hurricane Petition to ask for mercy, but only after the community acknowledges its failures and commits to change.

Mayor Delgado (45, practical administrator) thinks spiritual response is fine but secondary to physical preparation. He wants everyone focused on evacuation plans, shelter readiness, emergency supplies. He thinks spending time on lengthy ceremonies is dangerous distraction.

Young activist Carmen (28, environmental organizer) believes the hurricane is punishment for specific environmental damage—the mangrove swamps that used to protect the coast have been cleared for beachfront development. She wants to halt all development and commit to mangrove restoration, but the developers are powerful and the economy depends on tourism.

All three turn to the Seekers: "What should we do? What is Guabancex trying to tell us?"

### The Situation

The hurricane is real and dangerous. It will hit in 72 hours. People will die if preparation is inadequate. But the spiritual dimension is also real—Guabancex is speaking, and whether the community listens will affect both the storm's severity and the community's ability to survive it.

The truth is: all three NPCs are partially right.

- Benita is right that complacency is problem and spiritual response matters

- Delgado is right that physical preparation is essential and cannot be sacrificed

- Carmen is right that environmental damage is inviting disaster

The Seekers must find a way to honor all three perspectives while also leading the community through imminent danger.

### Key NPCs

**Elder Benita Reyes**

- Traditional Bohíque, trained in the old ways, respected but sometimes seen as out of touch

- She has survived seven major hurricanes in her lifetime

- She knows Hurricane Petition ceremony but has only performed it once before (successfully)

- She is willing to sacrifice her life if necessary—at 73, she has lived long enough, and if dying during the ceremony saves the town, so be it

**Mayor Rafael Delgado**

- Elected official, not particularly spiritual, but not hostile to tradition

- He lost family members in a hurricane twenty years ago (when preparation was inadequate), and he is terrified of repeating that failure

- He genuinely wants to do right by his people but thinks ceremonies are luxury they cannot afford right now

- He will support spiritual work if Seekers can prove it won't interfere with evacuation

**Carmen Ortiz**

- Young, passionate, sometimes self-righteous

- She grew up watching the mangroves get cleared, watching the coast become vulnerable

- She has been organizing protests for years with limited success

- She sees this hurricane as vindication of everything she's warned about, and part of her wants to say "I told you so" rather than help

- But she's also terrified for her younger siblings who still live here

**Mateo Cruz (55, developer, antagonist but not villain)**

- He owns most of the beachfront hotels

- He hired the crews that cleared the mangroves (legally—he had permits)

- He employs hundreds of people from this town

- He is genuinely torn: his business will be destroyed if the development stops, but he also lives here and doesn't want people to die

- He will fight to protect his interests but can be persuaded if approached with respect rather than condemnation

### The Challenge

The Seekers must:

1.  Lead immediate preparation - Regardless of spiritual work, people need to secure homes, stock shelters, evacuate vulnerable areas. This is 2-3 slice Mystery in itself.

2.  Decide whether to perform Hurricane Petition - This is 8-slice Mystery (as detailed in Chapter 4), requires days of preparation, and pulls key people (Bohíques, community leaders) away from physical prep. Is it worth it?

3.  Address the mangrove issue - Restoring mangroves takes years, but the community can commit to it now, can halt further clearing, can make the promise that might satisfy Guabancex even if full restoration isn't immediate.

4.  Unite the community - Benita, Delgado, and Carmen are all pulling in different directions. If the Seekers cannot bring them together, efforts will be scattered and less effective.

### Complications

Time Pressure

72 hours is not much time. Every hour spent on one task is an hour not spent on others. The Seekers must prioritize ruthlessly.

Guabancex's Answer Is Not Guaranteed

Even if Hurricane Petition is performed perfectly, Guabancex might refuse. She might decide the lesson needs to be harsh. The Seekers are not in control here.

Some People Will Refuse to Evacuate

There are always people who say "I've lived through storms before, I'm staying." The Seekers must decide: do they respect this choice or force evacuation? What if those who stay endanger rescuers who will try to save them?

The Developers Fight Back

If Carmen's environmental demands gain traction, Mateo will push back hard. He has lawyers, political connections, money. He can make things difficult. But he also has a point—shutting down development overnight puts hundreds of people out of work.

Elder Benita's Health

Midway through preparation, Benita collapses—exhaustion, stress, age. She's still alive but weakened. Can the Hurricane Petition continue without her? Should someone less experienced take over? What if this is Guabancex taking her as sacrifice?

### Resolution Paths

Path One: Perfect Storm (literal and metaphorical)

The Seekers manage everything brilliantly:

- Physical prep completed (shelters stocked, vulnerable evacuated, homes secured)

- Hurricane Petition performed (with Seekers supporting weakened Benita)

- Developers agree to halt clearing and commit to restoration (Mateo becomes ally)

- Community united under shared purpose

Guabancex acknowledges the effort. The hurricane shifts course—not dramatically, not away entirely, but enough. Category 4 becomes Category 2. The town suffers damage but survives. No deaths.

Reward: Each Seeker clears 3 Imbalance and gains +1 to Foriti permanently. The community becomes ally—these people will help the Seekers in future Mysteries. Guabancex grants blessing: once, when the Seekers face destruction, the storm will spare them.

Path Two: Survived But Scarred

The Seekers do well but not perfectly:

- Physical prep adequate but some gaps

- Hurricane Petition attempted but compromised (Benita too sick, ceremony incomplete)

- Environmental promises made but developers only partially convinced

- Community somewhat divided

The hurricane hits hard. Category 4, full force. Buildings destroyed. Some injuries, maybe one death (NPC, Guide's choice). But because of preparation, it could have been much worse.

Reward: Each Seeker clears 1 Imbalance. They mark 1 Imbalance (Doubt—wondering if they could have done more). The community is grateful but traumatized. Rebuilding becomes next Mystery.

Path Three: Catastrophe

The Seekers fail to unite the community, fail to complete adequate preparation, fail to address spiritual dimension:

- Evacuation chaotic, many people staying behind

- Hurricane Petition either not performed or botched

- Developers fighting until the last minute

- Benita dies angry and disappointed

The hurricane is devastating. Multiple deaths. Infrastructure destroyed. The community is broken.

Consequence: Each Seeker marks 2 Imbalance (Ego—they thought they could handle this, or Doubt—they realize they couldn't). Guabancex's lesson is harsh. The town may not recover. The Seekers must live with the weight of failure.

### What This Mystery Teaches
**For Seekers:**

- You cannot control forces of nature, only prepare for them and respond with wisdom

- Spiritual work and practical work are not opposed—both are necessary

- Leadership means making hard choices under pressure with incomplete information

- Sometimes your best effort still results in tragedy, and you must find a way to continue

**For Guides:**

- This Mystery should feel genuinely dangerous—the stakes are real

- Guabancex's response should be based on the quality of the Seekers' efforts AND on what lesson the community needs

- Don't pull punches, but also don't punish Seekers for making reasonable choices that happen not to work

---

## Mystery Three: The Lost Batey

Complexity: 4-5 slices

Best For: All levels, but especially good for exploring Taíno history

Themes: Belonging, history, community claims, cultural preservation

### The Hook

Deep in the forest, two young hikers stumble upon something extraordinary: an ancient batey—ball court—completely overgrown but still recognizable. Stone markers at the ends. Carved trigonolitos partially buried. Petroglyphs on surrounding rocks.

They report their find to the local Cacique, who sends the Seekers to investigate. If this is a genuine ancient batey, it is sacred site that must be protected, studied, possibly restored. But several questions must be answered first:

- How old is it?

- Which community does it belong to?

- Should it be excavated or left undisturbed?

- Can it be protected from looters and tourists?

Three groups immediately claim interest:

The Mountain Town (nearby community of 500 people) says the batey is on their ancestral land and they should control it.

The University wants to study it, excavate carefully, learn everything possible about the people who built it.

The Bohíques' Council wants to consecrate it immediately and restrict access to spiritual practitioners only.

All three turn to the Seekers: "Help us determine the right path forward."

### The Situation

The batey is approximately 800 years old—built by Taíno ancestors, used for ceremonial games, abandoned when the community relocated (reasons lost to time—possibly drought, possibly conflict, possibly just gradual movement).

The site is sacred, but it is also fragile. Exposure to elements after centuries of being protected by forest canopy will accelerate decay. Any excavation risks damage. But leaving it untouched means missing opportunity to learn.

There is no perfect solution. Every choice involves tradeoffs.

### Key NPCs

**Cacique Luz María Torres (Mountain Town leader, 52)**

- Proud of her community's history

- Wants the batey to become centerpiece of local identity—restore it, hold games there, attract respectful visitors

- Worried that University or Bohíques will take control away from her people

- Can be defensive and territorial, but genuinely wants what's best for ancestors

**Dr. Alejandro Vega (University archaeologist, 38)**

- Brilliant, passionate about Taíno history

- Sees this as chance to answer questions about ancient Taíno life

- Can be tone-deaf about spiritual concerns—he thinks of batey as historical artifact, not living sacred site

- Not malicious, just operating from different worldview

**Bohíque Isabela (Council representative, 61)**

- Deeply spiritual, protective of sacred sites

- Believes that academic study violates the sanctity of ancestor spaces

- Wants the batey consecrated and sealed—only initiated practitioners allowed

- Suspicious of "scholars who treat our ancestors as research subjects"

### The Investigation

The Seekers must:

1.  Verify the site's authenticity and age - This requires:

    - Examining the stonework, petroglyphs, layout

    - Consulting with elders who know architectural history

    - Possibly using Petroglyph Reading to hear directly from the stones

2.  Understand the competing claims - This requires:

    - Listening to each group's perspective

    - Researching land ownership and historical boundaries

    - Determining what legal/spiritual rights each group has

3.  Assess the risks - This requires:

    - Evaluating how exposed site will affect preservation

    - Understanding what excavation would entail

    - Considering security concerns (looters, vandals, tourists)

4.  Propose a solution that serves everyone - This requires:

    - Creativity, diplomacy, and willingness to facilitate difficult conversations

### Complications

The Petroglyphs Speak

If a Seeker uses Petroglyph Reading, the stones reveal something unexpected: this batey was built jointly by Taíno and Carib peoples during a brief period of peace. The games here were not just sport but diplomacy—a way for two peoples to compete without warfare.

This complicates everything. Now the nearby Carib community also has claim. Do the Seekers reveal this? Hide it? How does this change the discussion?

Looters Arrive

Word has gotten out. Artifact dealers hear about the find and send thieves to steal the trigonolitos before site can be secured. The Seekers must either:

- Guard the site personally (dangerous, exhausting)

- Convince the three groups to cooperate on security (hard given their conflict)

- Move the artifacts to safety temporarily (but where, and who controls them?)

The University Pushes Ahead

Dr. Vega, frustrated by delays, begins excavation without permission. He argues that time is wasting, that academic freedom means he doesn't need approval from "local politics." The Seekers must decide how to respond—diplomacy, confrontation, or finding third path.

A Vision in the Batey

One night, while the Seekers are keeping watch, a spirit appears—an ancestor who played on this court centuries ago. The vision offers guidance, but the message is cryptic and can be interpreted multiple ways. Different groups will claim the ancestor supports their position.

### Resolution Paths

Path One: Collaborative Stewardship

The Seekers facilitate agreement:

- Mountain Town maintains the site and controls access

- University conducts limited excavation with Bohíque oversight and spiritual protocols

- Bohíques consecrate the space but allow respectful visitation

- All three groups form committee for ongoing stewardship

- If Carib claim is revealed, they are included in the agreement

The batey is restored carefully, with both academic rigor and spiritual respect. It becomes model for how sacred sites can be preserved while still being accessible.

Reward: Each Seeker clears 2 Imbalance and gains +1 to Iwa. All three groups become allies. The batey becomes accessible for future Mysteries—Seekers can come here for vision quests, sacred games, or consultation with ancestors.

Path Two: Partial Success

The Seekers broker compromise but it's imperfect:

- One group gets more control than others (Guide decides based on Seekers' choices)

- Excavation happens but with tension and resentment

- Site is protected but relationships are strained

The batey is preserved, but the community is divided.

Reward: Each Seeker clears 1 Imbalance. One group is friendly, one neutral, one resentful. The batey is accessible but visiting it always involves navigating political complications.

Path Three: Conflict Escalates

The Seekers cannot unite the groups:

- Legal battle erupts over control

- Dr. Vega's unauthorized excavation damages the site

- Looters steal major artifacts

- The Bohíques curse everyone involved

The batey is lost—not physically destroyed, but so tangled in conflict that no one can use it for its intended purpose.

Consequence: Each Seeker marks 1 Imbalance (Ego or Doubt, their choice). The ancestor spirits are disappointed. The community is fractured. This failure will haunt future Mysteries in this region.

### What This Mystery Teaches

**For Seekers:**

- Sacred sites belong to everyone and no one—stewardship requires humility

- Academic knowledge and spiritual wisdom are both valuable but operate on different frequencies

- Sometimes the best solution requires all parties to compromise, giving up some of what they want for collective good

- History is never simple, and claims of "this is mine" are always more complicated than they appear

**For Guides:**

- Let the NPCs all be right in different ways—no one is villain

- The Carib revelation is optional but powerful if your group is ready for that complexity

- The ancestor vision should be genuinely helpful but not prescriptive—the living must make their own choices

---

## Mystery Four: River in Pain

Complexity: 6-8 slices

Best For: Intermediate Seekers, especially Naborías and anyone with River Memory

Themes: Environmental justice, economic survival, healing land and people together

### The Hook

The Seekers are visiting one of the Seven Sacred Waterfalls—Salto Collazo, the Waterfall of Healing, in the central mountains—when they realize something is terribly wrong. The pool at the base of the falls, normally clear and green, is brown and foamy. Dead fish float at the edges. The ferns along the banks are yellowing. The air smells faintly of metal.

Medicine Woman Inés Rivera, who gathers herbs at this waterfall every week, is kneeling at the water's edge in tears. "She is screaming," Inés says. "Can't you hear her? The waterfall is screaming."

Sick people come to Salto Collazo for healing. Three of them are now in the clinic in town with rashes, vomiting, and headaches after bathing in the pool. The waterfall that heals is making people ill.

### The Situation

Six kilometers upstream, hidden in a steep side valley, an illegal mining operation is extracting gold from the riverbed. It began eight months ago. The miners use mercury and other chemicals to separate the gold, and the runoff drains straight into the river that feeds Salto Collazo.

The operation is run by an outsider—Ramiro Ortega, a trader from the coast who saw an opportunity. But the workers are local. Forty people from two struggling mountain villages work at the site. The coffee blight two years ago destroyed their harvests, and the mine is the only work that pays enough to feed their families.

The waterfall Zemí has been suffering for months. It tried to warn people through the coquíes (who fell silent along the upper river), through dreams (several villagers have dreamed of drowning), and through the failing health of the herbs Inés gathers. Now it is in open pain.

### Key NPCs

**Inés Rivera (58, medicine woman, Naboría)**

- Herbalist and healer who is not a Bohíque, but knows plants better than most

- Has gathered at Salto Collazo for thirty years and treats the waterfall as family

- Furious and grieving—she wants the mine destroyed and its owners punished

- Will refuse to cooperate with any solution that keeps the mine open even one more day

**The Salto Collazo Zemí**

- Old, gentle, a healer by nature—which makes its pain worse

- Will not speak in words while it is being poisoned; it speaks only in pain, sickness, and dying plants

- Wants the poison stopped first and everything else second

**Ramiro Ortega (49, mine boss)**

- Not a monster, but a man who stopped asking questions once the money started coming

- Knows the mining is illegal and harmful; tells himself that everyone benefits

- Will lie, bribe, and threaten to protect the operation, but will fold if faced with both legal consequences and community pressure

**Coffee Farmer Raúl Torres (62, mountain Naboría, songs keeper)**

- Lost most of his harvest to the coffee blight; his two sons now work at the mine

- Ashamed that his sons are poisoning the river, but more afraid of them going hungry

- Keeps the old work songs of the mountain farms—songs that may matter later

**Javi and Leo Torres (24 and 21, mine workers, Raúl's sons)**

- Javi is defensive and bitter: "Where were all you spiritual people when the coffee died?"

- Leo is sick—he has had tremors and headaches for weeks—and is starting to suspect the mine is killing him too

### The Investigation

The Seekers need to discover:

1.  What is poisoning the water - This requires:

    - Following the river upstream, reading its condition at each bend

    - Using River Memory or Soil Reading to sense where the sickness enters

    - Talking to the coquí-silent villages along the way

2.  Who is responsible - This requires:

    - Finding the hidden mine site (it is guarded)

    - Learning who owns the operation and who works there

    - Understanding how long it has been running and what chemicals are used

3.  Why the community has not stopped it - This requires:

    - Learning about the coffee blight and the economic collapse it caused

    - Hearing the workers' side of the story

    - Discovering that some local officials have been looking the other way

### Complications

The Workers Fight Back

When the Seekers first approach the mine, the workers—not Ortega—are the ones who block them. These are desperate people defending the only income they have. If the Seekers come in angry, there will be violence. If they come in listening, there is a chance.

Inés Will Not Compromise

Inés wants the mine shut down immediately and will publicly denounce anyone who negotiates. She may try to destroy equipment herself, which could get her hurt or arrested. Her grief is righteous, but her methods could make everything worse.

Leo Is Dying

Leo Torres has mercury poisoning. If the Seekers notice his symptoms (Spirit Diagnosis can reveal it), they realize the mine is poisoning its own workers as well as the river. Leo needs medical treatment immediately. This could turn the workers against Ortega—or it could make Javi even more desperate to keep the money coming for his brother's care.

The Official Is Paid

The regional land inspector, who should have caught this months ago, has been taking payments from Ortega. Exposing him will help shut down the mine, but it also brings scandal on the Cacique of the district, who appointed him and is now defensive.

### Resolution Paths

Path One: Heal the River and the People

The Seekers stop the poisoning and address the hunger that caused it. They expose Ortega and the corrupt inspector through the Cacique's council. They get Leo and other sick workers to the clinic. And they organize an alternative: the workers are hired for the Island Healing of the river (a 12-slice Mystery that follows), paid through the regional cooperative, while Naborías help replant the blighted coffee terraces with resistant varieties from the University.

Raúl Torres teaches the old work songs to the cleanup crews. On the day the first clean water test comes back, the workers sing them at the waterfall.

The Salto Collazo Zemí speaks at last—to Inés first, then to everyone. Its first words are: "You heard me."

Reward: Each Seeker clears 3 Imbalance and gains +1 to Iwa permanently. Inés and Raúl become allies. Salto Collazo grants each Seeker one free healing per session for the rest of the campaign (clear one box of Strain when bathing there).

Path Two: Stop the Poison, Leave the Hunger

The Seekers shut the mine down—through law, force, or public pressure—but do not address what drove the workers there. Ortega is fined and leaves. The river begins slowly to recover. But forty families lose their income overnight. Within months, some workers have started a smaller illegal mine in another valley.

The waterfall Zemí is grateful but still uneasy. It speaks again, briefly, and then grows quiet.

Reward: Each Seeker clears 1 Imbalance. They mark 1 Imbalance (Doubt—they know the problem has only moved). A future Mystery waits in the next valley.

Path Three: The River Keeps Dying

If the Seekers are driven off by the workers, bought off by Ortega, or paralyzed by the competing demands, the mine keeps running. Inés does something desperate. Leo gets sicker. The waterfall of healing becomes a waterfall of sickness.

Consequence: Each Seeker marks 2 Imbalance (Doubt). Salto Collazo becomes a place of danger rather than healing for the rest of the campaign, until an Island Healing is completed there.

### What This Mystery Teaches

**For Seekers:**

- Environmental justice is never simple; healing land requires healing economic systems

- People who harm the land are often also being harmed

- Anger is a valid response to destruction, but anger alone rarely heals anything

- The Zemí's pain and the workers' pain are both real

**For Guides:**

- Play the workers with dignity—they are not villains, they are trapped

- Let Inés be right about the urgency even when she is wrong about the method

- The mercury poisoning is a turning point; make sure it lands with weight

---

## Mystery Five: The Carib Raid

Complexity: 8-10 slices

Best For: Experienced Seekers ready for moral complexity and danger

Themes: Retaliation, responsibility, peace-making, seeing the enemy as human

### The Hook

The caracol sounds at dawn from the east coast—three short blasts, over and over. The alarm. When the Seekers reach the border village of Punta Guayanés, half the fishing houses are burning. Six people are injured. And four people have been taken: two teenagers, a fisherman, and a grandmother who refused to leave her grandson's side.

The raiders came in fast canoes before sunrise and were gone within an hour. They were Carib. Everyone is certain of that.

The village is in uproar. Young men are already preparing their own canoes to go after them. Caracol Keeper Javier Cruz is shouting for calm and being ignored. The local Cacique has sent a runner to San Juan asking for the Cacica Supreme to authorize a war party.

The Seekers are asked to do something—anything—before the situation turns into a war.

### The Situation

This raid was not random. It was retaliation.

Three weeks ago, a group of Taíno hunters from Punta Guayanés crossed into Carib waters around a small offshore cay that both peoples claim. On that cay, they killed a sea turtle that was nesting—an animal the local Carib community regards as sacred to their own spirits. Worse, they killed it on the beach where the Carib bury their navigators.

The Carib community sent a message demanding restitution. The message was carried by a Taíno trader, who never delivered it. He was afraid of how the village would react and decided it was not his problem.

So, in the eyes of Cacique Kairú, the Taíno ignored a demand for justice. The raid is his answer. The four captives are not being harmed. They are being held as leverage for an apology and a payment. But if the Taíno respond with a war party, the captives may not survive the chaos, and the conflict will spread along the whole coast.

### Key NPCs

**Cacique Kairú (Carib warrior-leader, see Chapter 7)**

- Ordered the raid; believes he had no choice after his demand was ignored

- Tired of fighting, but will not appear weak

- Holds the captives in his own village and has ordered that they be treated well

**Raiding Captain Daguao (Carib, see Chapter 7)**

- Led the raid itself; professional, honorable, and ruthless

- Personally ensured no one was killed in the raid—and he is proud of that

- Will fight to the death if the Taíno attack

**Peace Advocate Anani (Carib, see Chapter 7)**

- Argued against the raid in council and lost

- Wants to end this before it escalates; may be the Seekers' way in

**Caracol Keeper Javier Cruz (Taíno, see Chapter 7)**

- Sounded the alarm and is now trying to stop the village from launching a war party

- Believes, correctly, that something in this story is missing

**Benicio Maraguey (31, Taíno hunter)**

- One of the hunters who killed the turtle on the cay

- Knows exactly why the raid happened and has told no one

- Terrified of what his neighbors will do if they learn the truth

**Teodoro Quiñones (55, Taíno trader)**

- Carried the undelivered message; hid it out of fear

- Will confess only if he believes the Seekers can protect him

### The Investigation

The Seekers need to discover:

1.  Why the raid happened - This requires:

    - Questioning villagers about any recent contact with Carib

    - Noticing Benicio's guilt (an Oath Reading or simple observation reveals he is hiding something)

    - Finding the trader and the undelivered message

2.  Where the captives are and how they are being treated - This requires:

    - Tracking the canoes, reading the sea, or consulting sea Zemí

    - Making contact with Carib through traders, fishers, or the Peace Advocate Anani

    - Using Spirit of the Zemí or a Guatiao Token, if any captive carries one

3.  What it would take to end this - This requires:

    - Opening communication with Kairú

    - Understanding what restitution the Carib actually want

    - Convincing both their own village and the Cacica Supreme's council to wait

### Complications

The War Party Leaves Without Permission

Twenty young men from the village launch their canoes at night. If the Seekers do not stop them, they will reach the Carib village by dawn—and Daguao's warriors are ready.

The Truth Turns the Village

When it comes out that the Taíno hunters started this, part of the village turns on Benicio and his friends. Some want to hand them over to the Carib. Others insist the raid was still unjustified. The village fractures.

The Captives Are Not All Victims

The grandmother, Doña Celestina, has been treating the Carib children's fevers during her captivity, and she has quietly decided she likes them. She may refuse to be rescued until the Carib and Taíno elders talk face to face.

San Juan Wants Blood

The Cacica Supreme's war council, hearing only that the Carib burned a village and took hostages, is preparing to authorize an armed response. The Seekers have limited time to bring the true story to the Council of Elders.

### Resolution Paths

Path One: Restitution and Peace

The Seekers bring out the truth, stop the war party, and arrange a meeting on the disputed cay between Cacique Kairú, Anani, the Punta Guayanés Cacique, and a Guatiao mediator (Rosa Marrero is ideal). The Taíno village admits fault for the killing of the turtle and the desecration of the navigators' graves. Benicio, with great fear, apologizes in person. The Carib release the captives. The Taíno make restitution with goods and a ceremony on the cay to honor the Carib dead. The two communities agree on clear boundaries around the cay and a way to send messages that does not depend on one frightened trader.

If the Seekers are especially skilled, this becomes the beginning of a Guatiao bond between the two villages (the Guatiao's Fifth Breakthrough Practice).

Reward: Each Seeker clears 3 Imbalance and gains +1 to Iwa. Anani and Javier become allies. The Carib community will receive the Seekers peacefully in future Mysteries.

Path Two: Captives Returned, Wounds Open

The Seekers negotiate the release of the captives, but the deeper conflict is not resolved. The Taíno pay restitution grudgingly without a real apology, or the Carib release the captives only after a brief, bloody skirmish. Everyone comes home alive, but both sides are angrier than before.

Reward: Each Seeker clears 1 Imbalance. They mark 1 Imbalance (Ego or Doubt). Carib Encounters in this region are rolled with -1 for the rest of the campaign.

Path Three: War

The war party reaches the Carib village, or San Juan authorizes an attack. People die on both sides. Perhaps the captives die in the fighting. The conflict spreads along the coast.

Consequence: Each Seeker marks 3 Imbalance (Ego—they could not stop the violence, or chose it). The coast becomes a war zone in future Mysteries. Healing it will take years.

### What This Mystery Teaches

**For Seekers:**

- There are no pure victims or villains in most conflicts

- Understanding why the other side acted is not the same as excusing it—but it is necessary for peace

- Admitting your own people's fault is one of the hardest and most sacred acts there is

- A single unsent message can start a war

**For Guides:**

- Play Kairú and Daguao with full dignity; they are acting from their own sense of justice

- Do not reveal the turtle killing too early—let the Seekers earn it

- If the Seekers choose war, let them; then let them live with it

---

## Mystery Six: Cave of Voices

Complexity: 6 slices

Best For: Bohíques and death workers; Seekers with Cave Walker or Cohoba Vision

Themes: The restless dead, discernment, compassion and force in balance

### The Hook

A small earthquake shakes the northern karst country. Nobody is hurt. But two days later, a goat herder named Yamil Collado comes down from the hills pale and shaking. The earthquake opened a new passage in a hillside cave above his pasture. When he looked in, he heard voices—many voices, whispering and weeping and calling out names.

Since then, strange things have been happening in the nearby village. Children wake screaming. Dogs howl at night and refuse to go near the hill. A young mother claims a woman with no navel lay down in her bed and asked for her baby. Bohíque Isabela del Río, who would normally handle this, is gravely ill (see her secret in Chapter 7) and cannot climb the hill.

She sends for the Seekers. "Something old has woken up," she tells them. "Go and listen. Find out who they are. Help the ones who need help. And be careful with the ones who don't."

### The Situation

The new passage leads into an ancient burial cave that was sealed by a rockfall centuries ago. Dozens of people were laid to rest there—some properly, with vigils and offerings; some hastily during a time of famine and sickness, without any vigil at all.

The earthquake broke the seal. Now the dead are stirring.

Most of them are peaceful ancestors who were properly sent to Coaybay long ago; only their bones remain, and they are merely disturbed. A smaller group never received the Ancestor Canoe. They are opía—restless, confused, wandering at night, looking for what they lost. Most are lonely rather than dangerous.

But one is dangerous. Long ago, a man named Guaybana was buried in the cave after he murdered his brother. He was buried without honor, deliberately left unsent, as punishment. His rage has festered for centuries. He is the one who visited the young mother. He wants a living body, and children are easiest.

### Key NPCs

**Bohíque Isabela del Río (see Chapter 7)**

- Too ill to climb the hill, but can guide the Seekers from her bed

- Knows the old death songs and can teach them

- Will ask the Seekers to promise they will not use force unless there is no other way

**Yamil Collado (44, goat herder)**

- Found the passage; has not slept well since

- Knows the hill better than anyone and can guide the Seekers to the cave

- Superstitious, frightened, but brave when it matters

**Young Bohíque Apprentice Sofía Ruiz (see Chapter 7)**

- Isabela's student, sent along to help

- Wants desperately to prove herself; may rush into the cave alone

- Her fear of cohoba becomes a problem if the Seekers need vision work here

**Ana Alicea (26, young mother)**

- Was visited by Guaybana's spirit; terrified for her baby

- Has a two-crowned son—a child of Corocote, in the grandmothers' words—which is part of why the dead are drawn to her

**The Peaceful Dead**

- A chorus of ancestors who want only to be honored and resealed in peace

- Can tell the Seekers who the restless ones are, if asked respectfully

**Guaybana (the dangerous dead)**

- Centuries of rage; cunning; pretends at first to be one of the lonely dead

- Can be bound, but also, possibly, finally sent—if someone is willing to face what he did

### The Investigation

The Seekers need to discover:

1.  Who is in the cave - This requires:

    - Performing the Cave Oracle or a cohoba journey in or near the cave

    - Speaking to the peaceful dead and learning their names

    - Using Petroglyph Reading on carvings near the old entrance

2.  Which spirits are restless and why - This requires:

    - Learning from the village elders about the famine burials long ago

    - Asking Corocote or Opiyel for guidance

    - Telling opía from peaceful ancestors (Corocote's favor helps)

3.  What Guaybana is and what he wants - This requires:

    - Discovering the old story of the fratricide from an elder or from the petroglyphs

    - Recognizing his deception before he reaches a child

### Complications

Sofía Goes In Alone

On the second night, Sofía enters the cave alone to prove herself. The Seekers must find her before Guaybana does.

Not All Opía Are Willing to Go

Some of the restless dead do not want to be sent to Coaybay. They want to stay near the living, near their descendants in the village. Forcing them across feels cruel. Leaving them feels dangerous.

Guaybana Possesses Someone

If the Seekers are slow, Guaybana enters a living person—Yamil, Sofía, or Ana. Now the Seekers must deal with a dangerous spirit wearing the face of someone they care about.

Isabela Is Dying

As the Mystery unfolds, Isabela grows weaker. She may die before it is resolved, adding another death vigil to the Seekers' work—and making them the only death workers left in the region.

### Resolution Paths

Path One: Every Spirit Rightly Placed

The Seekers perform Ancestor Canoe vigils for the unsent famine dead. They make offerings to the peaceful ancestors and reseal the passage with ceremony. And they face Guaybana: they tell his story aloud, name his crime, speak for the brother he killed, and offer him what he was denied—an honest accounting before Maquetaurie Guayaba. If they do this with courage and compassion, Opiyel appears at last, and Guaybana is led across.

Reward: Each Seeker clears 2 Imbalance and gains the Cave Walker Attunement if they do not already have it. The village dead become protective ancestors. If Isabela dies, she names one Seeker her successor with her last breath.

Path Two: Bound but Not Released

The Seekers send the unsent famine dead, but cannot bring themselves to send Guaybana—or fail in the attempt—and instead bind him in the cave with stones, tobacco, and a sealed passage. The village is safe for now.

Reward: Each Seeker clears 1 Imbalance. The binding must be renewed once a year, and if anyone neglects it, Guaybana returns in a future Mystery.

Path Three: The Dead Walk

If the Seekers use force against all the spirits, fail to tell the dangerous from the lonely, or abandon the work, the village suffers. Children fall ill. Dogs keep howling. Guaybana eventually takes a living body.

Consequence: Each Seeker marks 2 Imbalance (Doubt). The hill becomes a haunted place. Its dead will not trust the Seekers again.

### What This Mystery Teaches

**For Seekers:**

- Not all spirits are friendly, and not all restless spirits are dangerous

- Death work requires discernment—knowing when to comfort and when to bind

- Even the worst of the dead may be owed the honest accounting they were denied

- Force and compassion both have their place; wisdom is knowing which is needed

**For Guides:**

- Make the peaceful dead genuinely moving—names, stories, small requests

- Let Guaybana be truly frightening, but give him a human story underneath

- Do not make the "right" answer obvious; let the Seekers struggle with mercy

---

## Mystery Seven: The Broken Oath

Complexity: 4-6 slices

Best For: Guatiao Seekers and anyone learning mediation

Themes: Loyalty, impossible choices, forgiveness, the limits of sacred vows

### The Hook

The Seekers arrive in the hill town of Guayabal to find it divided down the middle. Neighbors have stopped speaking. Two families who have shared a cassava field for three generations have built a fence through it. At the weekly areyto, half the town now dances on one side of the plaza and half on the other.

At the center of it all are two women: Yarelis Ocasio and Milagros Bermúdez. They have been Guatiao for twenty years—oath-sisters bound in public ceremony, their tokens worn on their wrists. Or they were. A month ago, Yarelis revealed a sacred secret Milagros had entrusted to her. Milagros has publicly declared the bond broken. Yarelis has refused to accept it.

The town's Cacique asks the Seekers for help. "The Zemí are withdrawing from us," he says. "The springs are low. The children are getting sick. Everyone says it is the broken oath. Fix it, or tell us how to live with it."

### The Situation

Here is what happened.

Milagros is the keeper of a family healing secret: the location of a hidden spring in the hills whose water, prepared in a particular way, cures a rare and deadly childhood fever. Her family has guarded it for generations. They believe the spring's Zemí will abandon them if its location is ever shared outside the family. Milagros told only one person outside her bloodline: Yarelis, her Guatiao, as part of their oath.

A month ago, Yarelis's granddaughter fell ill with exactly that fever. The doctors in the town could not stop it. Milagros was away in San Juan, unreachable. The girl was dying.

Yarelis went to the spring herself, followed the preparation as best she remembered it, and gave her granddaughter the water. The girl lived. But Yarelis was seen going to the spring, and she had to explain to her family—and so the secret spread. Within a week, half the town knew the location. People began visiting the spring. The spring's Zemí, disturbed and angry, has begun to dry it up.

Both women are right. Yarelis saved a child's life. Milagros lost her family's sacred trust. The oath-break has spiritual consequences, as the Guatiao Bond Attunement describes: Imbalance flooding both women, the Zemí withdrawing from both families and from the whole town that took sides.

### Key NPCs

**Yarelis Ocasio (56, Guatiao, grandmother)**

- Would do it again without hesitation; refuses to apologize for saving a child

- Deeply grieved at losing her oath-sister

- Her Balance Track is nearly full; she is sleeping badly and getting sick

**Milagros Bermúdez (55, Guatiao, keeper of the spring)**

- Feels betrayed in the worst way possible—not only by her sister, but by the town that now tramples her family's sacred place

- Knows, deep down, that she would have shared the secret herself to save the child

- Cannot forgive yet, and is ashamed that she cannot

**Guatiao Mediator Rosa Marrero (see Chapter 7)**

- Performed the original oath ceremony twenty years ago
- Is the obvious person to mediate—but her own broken Guatiao bond (her secret) makes her unexpectedly harsh with Yarelis

**The Spring Zemí**

- Old, private, protective; it agreed long ago to heal only through one family's careful stewardship

- Is now overwhelmed by visitors and drying itself up in self-defense

- Can be calmed, but it will want new terms

**Dulce Ocasio (9, Yarelis's granddaughter)**

- Alive because of the spring water

- Has dreams in which a woman made of water thanks her and then cries

### The Investigation

The Seekers need to discover:

1.  What actually happened - This requires:

    - Hearing both women's accounts separately (Find Common Ground is ideal)

    - Learning about the fever and Dulce's recovery

    - Seeing the spring and its damage

2.  What the Zemí involved need - This requires:

    - Consulting the spring Zemí directly

    - Reading the town's Imbalance: which Zemí have withdrawn and why

    - Learning the terms of the original Guatiao oath from Rosa

3.  Whether this bond can or should be saved - This requires:

    - Understanding what each woman truly wants underneath her anger

    - Deciding whether reconciliation or formal unbinding is the right path

### Complications

The Town Wants a Verdict

Both factions want the Seekers to declare one woman right and the other wrong. Any hint of taking sides will make one half of the town furious.

Rosa Takes a Side

Rosa's own unhealed wound comes out. She tells Yarelis, in public, that oath-breakers deserve what they get. The Seekers may need to mediate with the mediator.

The Spring Is Dying

Every day the spring gives less water. If it dries up completely, the cure is gone forever—for everyone, including future children.

Dulce Gets Sick Again

Halfway through the Mystery, Dulce's fever returns, weaker. She needs the spring water again. Now the Seekers must either help Milagros heal her—or watch Milagros refuse.

### Resolution Paths

Path One: A New Oath

The Seekers help both women see each other's truth. Milagros goes to Dulce's bedside and prepares the water herself, with Yarelis assisting. In that act, something breaks open. The two women, with Rosa (who has finally faced her own wound), perform a renewal of their Guatiao bond, adding a new vow: that when a life is at stake, the secret serves the life. The town makes a collective apology to the spring Zemí and agrees to new terms: the spring is closed to visitors and will be tended by Milagros's family and Yarelis's together, with healing water given freely to any child who needs it.

Reward: Each Seeker clears 2 Imbalance. Yarelis and Milagros each clear their entire Balance Track. Rosa begins to heal her own broken bond (a future Mystery). The spring Zemí grants the Seekers one dose of healing water, which cures any single illness.

Path Two: Honorable Unbinding

The Seekers help the women see that the bond cannot be repaired right now, and guide them through a formal unbinding ceremony with dignity. Their tokens are buried together. The town stops taking sides because the oath is properly ended. The spring is closed again and slowly recovers.

Reward: Each Seeker clears 1 Imbalance. The Imbalance flooding the two women stops. The town heals, but carries a sadness.

Path Three: The Town Splits

If the Seekers take a side, fail to address the spring, or cannot get the women to speak, the town fractures for good. The spring dries. The fever cure is lost.

Consequence: Each Seeker marks 1 Imbalance (Doubt). In a future Mystery, a child somewhere in the region falls ill with the fever, and there is no cure.

### What This Mystery Teaches

**For Seekers:**

- Sacred oaths are serious, but they are not always absolute; context matters

- Both people in a broken bond can be right

- Forgiveness is harder and more necessary than judgment

- Sometimes the most loving thing is to end a bond with honor

**For Guides:**

- Do not let anyone off easily—Yarelis did break a sacred trust, and Milagros's pain is real

- Rosa's secret is a powerful twist; use it if the table is ready

- Dulce's relapse forces the issue; use it if the Mystery stalls

---

## Mystery Eight: Coquí's Warning

Complexity: 6-8 slices

Best For: Seekers of all levels; excellent for groups that mix scientists and spiritual practitioners

Themes: Science and spirit as partners, neglect, collective responsibility

### The Hook

It is a warm, clear evening in the mountain town of Adjuntas—the meditation town, the town with more altars per capita than anywhere in Borikén. The Seekers are sitting on a porch after a good meal when they notice it.

Silence.

No ko-kee. Not one frog. On a night like this the hills should be roaring with the Coquí Collective. Instead there is nothing but the wind.

By morning, the news is spreading: the coquíes have fallen silent across the whole central region. Fishers report the same along the north coast rivers. Children are frightened. Elders are muttering about earthquakes and hurricanes. Everyone knows what the Coquí Collective's silence means: something terrible is coming.

But what?

### The Situation

Two things are happening at once, and both are real.

The first is biological. A deadly fungal disease, chytrid, has reached the island's frog populations. It arrived on frogs brought in through the pet trade at the San Juan harbor and has spread through the mountain streams. Chytrid has wiped out frog species all over the world. If it is not contained, many of Borikén's coquíes could disappear forever.

The second is spiritual. Over the last decade, the towns of the central region have grown comfortable and busy. Fewer families keep their altars. Fewer people make offerings at the streams. The Planting Moon ceremonies have become shorter. The Coquí Collective has been singing a warning about this neglect for years, but no one listened. Now the Zemí who protect the frogs have withdrawn their care, and the disease is moving faster than it should.

The silence is the Collective's warning: the island is sick, in body and spirit, and the frogs are only the first to fall.

### Key NPCs

**Dr. Itzel Lugo (39, University herpetologist)**

- Studies amphibians at the University of Borikén; the first to identify the chytrid

- Brilliant, exhausted, overwhelmed—she has two assistants and a crisis across half the island

- Skeptical of the spiritual explanation but desperate for help of any kind

**University Librarian Ana Colón (see Chapter 7)**

- Guardian of the University archives; the University Zemí's favorite

- Can find records of an older frog die-off, centuries ago, and the ceremony that ended it

**Volunteer Coordinator Pablo Muñoz (see Chapter 7)**

- Organizes community crisis response

- Can mobilize hundreds of volunteers, but needs a clear plan and someone people trust

**Elder Benita Reyes (see Chapter 7)**

- Sees the silence as proof of everything she has warned about

- Wants a massive community ceremony immediately; dismissive of "the frog doctor"

**Teenage Rebel Luis Vega (see Chapter 7)**

- Thinks the whole thing is overblown; refuses to "pray for frogs"

- Is secretly the one who, with friends, released a box of imported pet frogs into a stream last year when they got bored of them

**The Coquí Collective**

- Silent, but not gone; it can still be heard by those with Zemí Sense or River Memory

- Wants two things at once: the disease stopped, and the people to remember their relationships

### The Investigation

The Seekers need to discover:

1.  What is killing the frogs - This requires:

    - Working with Dr. Lugo, collecting samples, finding dead and dying frogs

    - Using Soil Reading or River Memory along the streams

    - Tracing the spread back toward its source

2.  Where the disease came from - This requires:

    - Investigating the pet trade at the harbor (Harbor Master Elena Soto knows everything that comes in)

    - Discovering the illegal release of imported frogs

    - Getting Luis to confess

3.  Why the Zemí have withdrawn - This requires:

    - Consulting the Coquí Collective through stillness and listening

    - Speaking with elders about how practice has faded in the region

    - Finding Ana Colón's records of the old die-off ceremony

### Complications

Science and Spirit Will Not Talk

Dr. Lugo and Elder Benita dismiss each other. Lugo thinks ceremonies are a distraction from containment. Benita thinks lab work is pointless without making things right with the Zemí. Both are half right. The Seekers must get them to work together.

The Containment Plan Hurts People

Stopping chytrid requires closing some streams to all human use, disinfecting boots and gear, and banning the movement of any frogs or plants between valleys. This affects farmers, fishers, tourists, and the people of Adjuntas who bathe in those streams for prayer.

Luis Is Exposed

When the illegal release comes out, some people want to punish Luis harshly. He is seventeen. His shame turns to defiance.

The Collective Starts Singing Again in One Place

Midway through, the coquíes begin singing again in a single valley—the one where an old couple never stopped their nightly offerings. This is a clue, but it also makes some people believe the crisis is over.

### Resolution Paths

Path One: Both Medicines

The Seekers bring Dr. Lugo and Elder Benita together. The University's containment plan and the old ceremony from Ana Colón's archives become one coordinated effort. Pablo Muñoz mobilizes volunteers to disinfect, survey, and protect healthy populations. Every town in the region restores its stream offerings and holds a great Areyto for the frogs. Luis confesses publicly and becomes one of the most dedicated volunteers.

Months later, on a warm night, the hills begin to sing again.

Reward: Each Seeker clears 3 Imbalance. Dr. Lugo and Elder Benita both become allies—and, eventually, friends. The Coquí Collective grants the Seekers a permanent blessing: once per Mystery, the Guide must give them a clear warning of danger before it arrives.

Path Two: The Frogs Survive, the Relationship Doesn't

The Seekers support only the scientific response. Chytrid is contained. Many coquíes survive. But the deeper neglect is never addressed, and the Collective remains quieter than before.

Reward: Each Seeker clears 1 Imbalance. The Collective sings again, but more softly. The Coquí warnings in future Mysteries become harder to hear (-1 to rolls to interpret them).

Path Three: The Silence Spreads

If the Seekers choose only ceremony and ignore the disease, or let the conflict between science and spirit paralyze the response, chytrid spreads. Several coquí species are lost.

Consequence: Each Seeker marks 2 Imbalance (Doubt). For the rest of the campaign, the Guide should describe nights in the mountains as quieter than they should be. The island remembers.

### What This Mystery Teaches

**For Seekers:**

- Modern problems require modern and ancient solutions together

- Science and spirituality are not opposed; both describe the same living world

- Small acts of neglect, multiplied across many people, can become a crisis

- Mistakes made by the young deserve accountability, not exile

**For Guides:**

- Present Dr. Lugo and Benita as equally competent within their worlds

- The single singing valley is a gift—let the Seekers discover why

- Make the return of the coquí song a genuinely emotional moment

---

## Mystery Nine: The Challenger

Complexity: 4-6 slices

Best For: Bohíques and spiritually confident Seekers who might benefit from humility

Themes: Pride, curiosity, cultural exchange, respect between traditions

### The Hook

A Carib canoe lands openly on the beach at Fajardo in broad daylight, flying a white cloth. Stepping out is a woman in her fifties with a necklace of shells and bones and a gaze that misses nothing. She is Boyé Amaya, the shaman of Cacique Kairú's people.

She walks into the plaza, sits down beside the batey, and announces—loudly enough for the whole town to hear—that she has come to test the Bohíques of Borikén. "Your people say your Zemí are the strongest in the islands," she says. "My people say our spirits are. Let us find out. Bring me your best."

The town is electrified. Some are furious at her arrogance. Some are thrilled. Several young Bohíques want to accept the challenge immediately. The Circle of Vision sends word: the honor of Borikén's spiritual tradition is at stake. The Seekers are asked to represent the Taíno.

### The Situation

The challenge is real, but it is not what it seems.

Amaya is not here to humiliate anyone. She has been receiving disturbing visions for a year: a great sickness coming to all the islands, one that neither Carib boyés nor Taíno Bohíques can face alone. In her visions, the healing requires knowledge from both traditions.

But she cannot simply walk into Borikén and ask for help. Her people would see it as weakness, and Kairú would forbid it. So she issued a challenge instead. A contest is something her people understand. A contest lets her stand beside Taíno practitioners as an equal.

And there is one more layer. Decades ago, Amaya and a Taíno Bohíque were lovers (see her secret in Chapter 7). That Bohíque is Elder Benita Reyes. Amaya has not seen her in thirty years. Part of the reason she came to Fajardo, specifically, is that she heard Benita lives nearby.

### Key NPCs

**Boyé Amaya (see Chapter 7)**

- Proud, mysterious, speaks in riddles that make sense if you are patient

- Genuinely wants to learn—and genuinely wants to see if the Taíno are worth learning from

- Will reveal her real purpose only to someone who shows curiosity rather than pride

**Elder Benita Reyes (see Chapter 7)**

- Hears that Amaya has come and refuses at first to leave her house

- Thirty years of feelings she has never spoken about

- Can be the key to the whole Mystery, if the Seekers notice her reaction

**Batey Champion Diego Pérez (see Chapter 7)**

- Wants the contest held on the batey, with the sacred ball game as one of the trials

- Fiercely competitive; sees this as a chance for glory

**Young Bohíque Apprentice Sofía Ruiz (see Chapter 7)**

- Desperate to be chosen as one of Borikén's champions

- Will do something reckless if passed over

**Teenage Dreamer Itibi (Carib, see Chapter 7)**

- Amaya's apprentice, who came with her in the canoe

- Wants to become a storyteller, not a shaman; may tell the Seekers what Amaya will not

**The Crowd**

- Most of Fajardo, who want Borikén to win and will be loudly disappointed by anything else

### The Challenge

The contest Amaya proposes has three trials:

1.  The Trial of Seeing - Each side must divine something hidden: an object buried somewhere on the beach. (Spirit of the Zemí, Petroglyph Reading, cohoba, or Carib bone-and-smoke divination.)

2.  The Trial of Healing - Each side must treat a sick person from the town. (Spirit Diagnosis, herbs, River Blessing, Baibrama's favor.)

3.  The Trial of the Batey - A sacred ball game, Taíno against Carib, with the town watching.

How the Seekers approach each trial matters more than whether they win it.

### Complications

The Crowd Turns Ugly

If the Taíno lose a trial, the crowd turns hostile toward Amaya and Itibi. People shout. Someone throws a stone. The Seekers must protect the visitors or risk a diplomatic disaster with Kairú.

Sofía Cheats

Sofía, desperate to win, secretly tampers with the Trial of Seeing by asking a Zemí to hide the object from Amaya. The Zemí are offended. The Seekers must decide whether to expose her.

Kairú Hears of It

News reaches Cacique Kairú that his shaman has gone to Borikén. He sends Raiding Captain Daguao to bring her home—by force if necessary. Daguao's canoes appear offshore on the second day.

Benita Comes to the Batey

On the final day, Benita appears at the edge of the batey. Amaya sees her. Neither moves. The whole contest stops.

### Resolution Paths

Path One: Contest Becomes Collaboration

The Seekers approach Amaya with curiosity rather than pride. They learn her real purpose. They help reframe the contest: instead of trying to defeat each other, the Taíno and Carib practitioners demonstrate their traditions to each other. In the Trial of Healing, they work together on the same patient—and do better than either could alone. The batey game becomes a celebration rather than a battle. Benita and Amaya finally speak. Amaya shares her vision of the coming sickness, and the Circle of Vision agrees to meet with the Carib boyés.

Reward: Each Seeker clears 2 Imbalance and gains +1 to Oye. Amaya becomes an ally and teacher. Itibi becomes a friend. A future Mystery opens: the sickness from Amaya's vision, faced by both peoples together.

Path Two: Honorable Contest

The Seekers compete fairly and respectfully but never discover Amaya's deeper purpose. Whoever wins, both sides are honored. Amaya leaves impressed but without what she came for.

Reward: Each Seeker clears 1 Imbalance. Amaya will return someday, and next time she may ask directly.

Path Three: Humiliation

The Seekers compete with arrogance, cheat, or let the crowd turn violent. Amaya leaves insulted. Daguao takes her home. Kairú treats it as an act of hostility.

Consequence: Each Seeker marks 2 Imbalance (Ego). Carib Encounters roll at -1 for the rest of the campaign. When the sickness from Amaya's vision arrives, the Taíno face it alone.

### What This Mystery Teaches

**For Seekers:**

- Strength is not proving superiority

- Different spiritual paths can coexist and learn from each other

- Curiosity is more valuable than pride

- People sometimes wear challenge as armor over a request for help

**For Guides:**

- Play Amaya as genuinely formidable—she should win at least one trial if the Seekers are careless

- Let the crowd's pressure be real; the Seekers should feel the temptation to win at all costs

- The Benita-Amaya reunion is optional, but if your table is ready for it, it can be the heart of the Mystery

---

## Mystery Ten: Ancestor's Request

Complexity: 6 slices

Best For: Seekers who have done cohoba or ancestor work; groups interested in history and repatriation

Themes: The claims of the dead, repatriation, ownership, difficult compromise

### The Hook

During a cohoba ceremony (or a Cave Oracle, if no Seeker is trained in vision medicine), one of the Seekers is visited by an ancestor. She is an old woman, a Bohíque from long ago, wearing a necklace of carved shell. She is distressed.

"My stone," she says. "My stone is not where it belongs. It was buried with me. It was taken from me. It is sitting in a house of glass, among strangers, and it cannot hear the river. Bring it home."

She shows the Seeker an image: a three-pointed stone, larger than any trigonolito they have seen, carved with a face on one point and a frog on another. Then she shows them a house on a hill above the sea, with tall windows and a glass case in the main room.

When the Seeker returns from the vision, the image is burned into their memory.

### The Situation

The ancestor is Abuela Guaynía—Grandmother Guaynía—a Bohíque who served the river communities of the Río Grande de Loíza valley more than five centuries ago. She was buried in a cave with her personal trigonolito, which housed the Zemí of the river she served for her whole life.

Ninety years ago, during a road-building project, workers broke into the burial cave. The bones were reburied with ceremony. But the trigonolito was quietly taken by a foreman and sold. It passed through several hands. Today it sits in a glass case in the hilltop home of the Arzola family in the coastal town of Dorado.

The Arzolas bought it legally, thirty years ago, from a respected dealer. They treasure it. They have no idea it came from a grave. To them, it is the finest piece in their art collection.

And the Zemí inside the stone is suffering. Cut off from its river, separated from the Bohíque who kept it, displayed rather than fed, it has been slowly fading. The river communities of the Loíza valley have been having small, unexplained misfortunes for decades—floods arriving at the wrong time, fishing that never quite recovers.

### Key NPCs

**The Ancestor (Abuela Guaynía)**

- Patient, but has waited ninety years; she will keep visiting the Seeker until this is resolved

- Wants the stone returned to the river, not to a museum

- Will accept a compromise if it is offered with respect—but she will know if the Seekers are being dishonest

**Doña Victoria Arzola (68, art collector)**

- Educated, cultured, proud of her collection; inherited the house and the stone from her late husband

- Not malicious; genuinely loves Taíno art and believes she is preserving it

- Will be offended at first by any suggestion that she possesses stolen grave goods

- Has a granddaughter who has been having dreams about a river

**Dr. Alejandro Vega (see Chapter 7)**

- Wants the stone for the University's Museum of Memory, where it can be studied and displayed publicly

- Has documentation that could prove where the stone came from

**Bohíque Isabela del Río (see Chapter 7) or another death worker**

- Can confirm that the ancestor is who she says she is

- Insists that grave goods belong to the dead, not to museums or collectors

**Art Collective Leader Marisol Cruz (see Chapter 7)**

- Preserves petroglyphs and makes sacred implements

- Could carve a replica of the stone—an idea some will find helpful and others insulting

### The Investigation

The Seekers need to discover:

1.  Who the ancestor is - This requires:

    - Returning to vision or dream to ask her name and story

    - Researching burial caves and old records in the Loíza valley

    - Consulting death workers who can confirm her identity

2.  Where the stone is - This requires:

    - Tracking the image from the vision to the house in Dorado

    - Researching the art market (Dr. Vega and Ana Colón can help)

    - Gaining access to the Arzola home

3.  How the stone left the cave - This requires:

    - Finding records of the road project ninety years ago

    - Tracing the stone's sales through dealers' records

    - Proving, if possible, that it was taken from a burial

### Complications

Doña Victoria Says No

When the Seekers first approach, Doña Victoria refuses. The stone was bought legally. It is hers. She threatens legal action if they keep bothering her.

The University Wants It Too

Dr. Vega argues that the stone should go to the Museum of Memory, where everyone can see it. The ancestor does not want this. The Seekers must decide whose claim matters more.

Someone Suggests Theft

A young activist—or a desperate Seeker—proposes simply stealing the stone back. It would be quick. It would also be a crime, and it would turn Doña Victoria into an enemy of every repatriation effort to come.

The Granddaughter's Dreams

Doña Victoria's granddaughter, Isa, age twelve, has been dreaming of an old woman beside a river. If the Seekers learn this, it may open Doña Victoria's heart—or frighten her into refusing even harder.

### Resolution Paths

Path One: The Stone Goes Home

The Seekers approach Doña Victoria with patience and respect. They show her the documentation of the stone's origin. They tell her about the ancestor, and they listen to her granddaughter's dreams. Doña Victoria chooses, on her own, to return the stone. The Seekers organize a ceremony: the stone is carried in procession from Dorado to the Loíza valley and returned to the cave with a full Ancestor Canoe vigil and offerings to the river. Doña Victoria and Isa walk with the procession. Marisol Cruz carves a replica for Doña Victoria's home, consecrated as a memorial, with the family's blessing.

Reward: Each Seeker clears 2 Imbalance and gains +1 to Iwa. The ancestor stops visiting and blesses the Seeker who carried her request: once per campaign, they may call on her for guidance. The Loíza valley's misfortunes ease. Doña Victoria becomes a champion of repatriation.

Path Two: Halfway Home

The Seekers negotiate a compromise: the stone goes to the Museum of Memory, displayed with a full account of its origin and fed by Bohíques monthly, or Doña Victoria keeps it but agrees to allow ceremony and offerings to it at her home. The ancestor accepts, but she is not at peace.

Reward: Each Seeker clears 1 Imbalance. The ancestor visits less often but still comes. A future Mystery may revisit the stone.

Path Three: Taken or Lost
