If the Seekers steal the stone, they may succeed—but Doña Victoria presses charges, the repatriation movement is discredited, and the ancestor, though she has her stone, is shamed by how it came home. If the Seekers give up, the stone stays in its glass case.

Consequence: Each Seeker marks 2 Imbalance (Ego if they stole it, Doubt if they gave up). The river communities' misfortunes continue.

### What This Mystery Teaches

**For Seekers:**

- The dead have claims on the living

- Repatriation of sacred objects is complex, but it is necessary

- Legal ownership and rightful ownership are not always the same

- Sometimes you cannot fully satisfy every obligation, and you must choose with care

**For Guides:**

- Play Doña Victoria as a sympathetic person, not a villain; her change of heart should feel earned

- The ancestor should be both loving and stern

- Let the Seekers feel the temptation of the quick, wrong solution

---

## Using These Mysteries

Each of these ten adventures can stand alone or be woven into larger campaign. Mix and match. Adjust details to fit your group's interests and skill level.

The key is: present problems honestly, embody NPCs fully, let Seekers make real choices, and then follow through with realistic consequences.

The Zemí are watching. The ancestors are listening. And your Seekers are learning to walk the path with every decision they make.

We return to the root.

Ase. Ase. Ase.

# CHAPTER 7: FOR THE GUIDE

## You Hold Sacred Responsibility

If you are reading this chapter, you are preparing to be a Guide—the person who presents Mysteries, embodies the Zemí, breathes life into NPCs, and facilitates the spiritual journey of your Seekers.

This is not a small thing. This is not "just running a game."

You are creating space for real practice. You are teaching people how to walk the Taíno path. You are representing spirits who are genuinely present and genuinely powerful. You are holding stories that matter, facilitating choices that have weight, guiding transformation that will affect people's actual lives.

Take this seriously. But don't let the seriousness make you rigid. The Zemí appreciate sincerity, not perfection. Your job is not to execute flawlessly. Your job is to show up with open heart, to do your best, to learn from mistakes, and to keep trying.

This chapter gives you tools:

- Twenty Taíno NPCs ready to drop into your game

- Ten Carib NPCs with equal depth and humanity

- Random tables for when you need to improvise

- Advice on running Taíno-centered campaigns with cultural respect

- Wisdom about embodying Zemí and making them feel real

Use these tools. Adapt them. Make them your own. And remember: you are not alone in this work. The ancestors are with you. The Zemí are watching. And I am rooting for you.

Let's begin.

## Twenty Taíno NPCs

Each NPC includes: name, age, role, personality, motivation, hook (how Seekers might meet them), and game stats.

### 1. Cacica Luz María Torres (Mountain Town Leader)

Age: 52 \| Role: Cacique of mountain community

Virtues: Foriti 3, Iwa 2, Suuru 2, Oye 1

Personality: Proud, protective, sometimes defensive. She has worked her entire life to build respect for her small community, to prove that mountain people are not "backward" despite what city dwellers might think. She loves her people fiercely and will fight anyone who threatens them—but she's learning that fighting isn't always the answer.

Motivation: Keep her community safe, honor the ancestors, prove that small towns matter just as much as San Juan.

Hook: Any Mystery involving mountain communities, land disputes, rural-urban tensions. She's the first person Seekers meet when they arrive in her town.

Secret: Her adult son moved to San Juan and rarely visits. She feels abandoned, and some of her defensiveness about "city people" is personal pain disguised as political stance.

### 2. Bohíque Isabela del Río (Death Worker)

Age: 61 \| Role: Specialist in death vigils and ancestor communication

Virtues: Oye 3, Suuru 3, Iwa 2, Foriti 1

Personality: Calm, grave, impossibly patient. She has sat with hundreds of dying people, performed countless death vigils, guided more spirits to Coaybay than she can count. Death does not frighten her. What frightens her is people dying badly—unprepared, unreconciled, leaving chaos behind.

Motivation: Help people die well, ensure the dead are properly honored, teach younger Bohíques that death work is sacred service.

Hook: Any Mystery involving death, ghosts, ancestors, or Coaybay. She's the one you call when someone dies or when spirits are restless.

Secret: She is dying. Slowly, from illness she's chosen not to treat. She has maybe two years left. She's at peace with this, but she's racing to train her successor before her time comes.

### 3. Dr. Alejandro Vega (University Archaeologist)

Age: 38 \| Role: Academic researcher, sometimes tone-deaf about spiritual concerns

Virtues: Oye 3, Iwa 1, Foriti 2, Suuru 1

Personality: Brilliant, passionate, enthusiastic to the point of being exhausting. He talks fast, gestures wildly, gets so excited about discoveries that he forgets social niceties. He genuinely loves Taíno history—loves it so much that sometimes he treats it as intellectual puzzle rather than lived reality.

Motivation: Learn everything, publish important research, make discoveries that change how people understand the past.

Hook: Any Mystery involving ancient sites, artifacts, historical questions. He shows up wherever there's something to study.

Secret: He grew up poor, fought his way to education, and feels constant imposter syndrome in academic spaces. His pushiness is partly overcompensation—he's terrified people will realize he "doesn't belong."

### 4. Carmen Ortiz (Environmental Activist)

Age: 28 \| Role: Organizer, protester, voice for the land

Virtues: Foriti 3, Oye 2, Iwa 2, Suuru 0

Personality: Fierce, passionate, impatient, self-righteous, exhausting, inspiring. She has been fighting since she was sixteen—fighting developers, fighting pollution, fighting apathy. She's burned out but can't stop. She sees the world in stark terms: you're helping or you're complicit. No middle ground.

Motivation: Protect the environment, hold powerful people accountable, create a world where her younger siblings can thrive.

Hook: Any Mystery involving environmental damage, corporate wrongdoing, generational conflict. She's at every protest, every town meeting, every crisis.

Secret: She's terrified she's wrong—that her absolutism is driving away potential allies, that her tactics aren't working, that she's wasting her life. But admitting doubt feels like betrayal of everything she's fought for.

### 5. Elder Benita Reyes (Traditional Bohíque)

Age: 73 \| Role: Keeper of old ways, hurricane worker

Virtues: Suuru 3, Iwa 3, Oye 2, Foriti 2

Personality: Patient, wise, sometimes stern. She has lived through more than most people can imagine—hurricanes, droughts, deaths of loved ones, social changes that made her feel like a relic. But she endures. She knows that her knowledge matters, that the old ways must be preserved, that someone must hold the line.

Motivation: Pass on the teachings before she dies, ensure the next generation knows how to work with weather and death and deep power.

Hook: Any Mystery involving hurricanes, major ceremonies, traditional practices under threat. She's the elder everyone respects even if they don't always listen.

Secret: She's lonely. Her peers are mostly dead. Young people are interested in the teachings but not in her personally. She misses being seen as a whole person, not just a repository of wisdom.

### 6. Mayor Rafael Delgado (Practical Administrator)

Age: 45 \| Role: Elected official balancing tradition and modernity

Virtues: Iwa 2, Foriti 2, Oye 2, Suuru 2

Personality: Competent, harried, perpetually compromising. He wants to do right by everyone and knows that's impossible. He respects spiritual tradition but also needs roads fixed, schools funded, economy functioning. He's the man in the middle, catching criticism from all sides.

Motivation: Keep the community safe and functioning, get re-elected, prove government can actually help people.

Hook: Any Mystery involving community resources, political decisions, practical vs spiritual priorities. He's the one who has to approve things, allocate funds, make hard choices.

Secret: He wanted to be a teacher. Still does. But his father was mayor before him, and the family tradition pulled him in. Sometimes he fantasizes about resigning and going back to school to get his teaching certificate.

### 7. Naboría Miguel Santos (Master Farmer)

Age: 67 \| Role: Agricultural expert, patient teacher

Virtues: Suuru 3, Iwa 3, Oye 1, Foriti 1

Personality: Quiet, observant, deeply rooted. He speaks slowly, moves deliberately, and says nothing that doesn't need saying. He has farmed the same terraces for forty-five years. He knows every plant, every stone, every mood of the soil. His hands are gnarled and strong and always dirty.

Motivation: Grow good food, teach young people proper farming, honor Yúcahu through his labor.

Hook: Any Mystery involving agriculture, food security, rural life, or patient endurance. He's the one you go to when you need to learn about cassava, when you need help with planting, when you need someone who understands slow work.

Secret: He never had children. His wife died young. The land is his child, his legacy, his love. When he dies, he worries no one will tend these terraces the way they deserve.

### 8. Caracol Keeper Javier Cruz (Storm Caller)

Age: 34 \| Role: Master of the conch shell, loud truth-teller

Virtues: Foriti 3, Oye 2, Iwa 1, Suuru 1

Personality: Loud, blunt, physically imposing, startlingly honest. When Javier speaks, people listen—partly because his voice carries, partly because he says what everyone else is thinking but afraid to voice. He has no patience for bullshit, no tolerance for cowardice, no interest in making people comfortable.

Motivation: Speak truth, warn of danger, call the community to action when action is needed.

Hook: Any Mystery requiring someone to blow the caracol, to summon people, to deliver uncomfortable truths. He's the one who shows up when diplomacy has failed and someone needs to be direct.

Secret: He's deeply insecure about his intelligence. He's not book-smart, struggles with reading, and compensates by being physically capable and brutally honest. He fears being seen as stupid.

### 9. Guatiao Mediator Rosa Marrero (Peace-Weaver)

Age: 51 \| Role: Creates sacred bonds, resolves conflicts

Virtues: Iwa 3, Oye 3, Suuru 2, Foriti 0

Personality: Warm, diplomatic, endlessly patient, sometimes too accommodating. Rosa believes that almost any conflict can be resolved if people will just sit down and truly listen to each other. She has facilitated dozens of Guatiao bonds, mediated countless disputes, and maintained peace in situations that should have exploded.

Motivation: Build bridges, create lasting relationships, prove that cooperation is stronger than competition.

Hook: Any Mystery involving conflict, broken relationships, or need for alliance. She's the one called when people can't talk to each other anymore.

Secret: Her own Guatiao bond broke years ago—her oath-sibling betrayed her trust profoundly. She still hasn't forgiven them, and this failure haunts her work. She preaches reconciliation but can't practice it.

### 10. Young Bohíque Apprentice Sofía Ruiz (Eager Student)

Age: 22 \| Role: Learning the path, making mistakes, growing

Virtues: Oye 2, Foriti 2, Iwa 1, Suuru 1

Personality: Enthusiastic, sometimes reckless, deeply sincere. Sofía wants to be a Bohíque so badly she can taste it. She studies constantly, practices obsessively, volunteers for every ceremony. She's brilliant but inexperienced, powerful but unrefined. She makes mistakes because she rushes, because she's overconfident, because she doesn't yet understand that wisdom takes time.

Motivation: Prove herself worthy, master the practices, help people.

Hook: Any Mystery where inexperienced practitioner causes problems or where young person needs mentorship. She's the one who means well but messes up, who needs guidance, who represents what Seekers might have been at the beginning.

Secret: She's terrified of cohoba. She knows she'll have to take it eventually as part of training, but vision medicine scares her in ways she can't articulate. She's been avoiding it, making excuses, hoping her teacher won't notice.

### 11. Fisherman Tomás Díaz (Sea Elder)

Age: 55 \| Role: Fishing boat captain, keeper of sea omens

Virtues: Suuru 3, Foriti 2, Oye 2, Iwa 1

Personality: Gruff, superstitious, short on words and long on silences. Tomás has fished out of Mayagüez since he was twelve. He speaks to Agüeybaná every morning before he leaves the dock and will not sail if the sea "looks wrong" to him, no matter what the forecast says. Younger fishers laugh at his rules. They also notice that his boat always comes home.

Motivation: Bring his crew home safe, keep the old sea protocols alive, and leave the fishing grounds healthier than he found them.

Hook: Any Mystery involving the ocean, sea travel, fishing communities, or omens. He's the one who will take the Seekers across the water when no one else will—or refuse to, and be right.
Secret: Twenty years ago he ignored his own gut and sailed into a squall. His younger brother drowned. Every rule he keeps is a way of apologizing to Agüeybaná—and to his brother.

### 12. University Librarian Ana Colón (Keeper of Records)

Age: 43 \| Role: Head archivist of the University of Borikén library

Virtues: Oye 4, Suuru 2, Iwa 2, Foriti 0

Personality: Precise, dry-humored, quietly delighted by obscure facts. Ana has spent twenty years among the stacks and knows where everything is—including things no catalog lists. Students say the University Zemí follows her around; books fall open to the right page when she walks by. She is warm once you earn it, but she has no patience for people who don't return books.

Motivation: Preserve knowledge, make it available to everyone, and help people find the answer they didn't know they were looking for.

Hook: Any Mystery requiring research, old records, forgotten ceremonies, or historical evidence. She's the one who says, "Give me a day," and comes back with exactly what the Seekers need.

Secret: She has found records that prove one of the island's most respected noble families built its fortune by quietly seizing a mountain community's land two centuries ago. She hasn't decided what to do with them.

### 13. Teenage Rebel Luis Vega (Restless Youth)

Age: 17 \| Role: High school student, skeptic, reluctant inheritor of tradition

Virtues: Foriti 2, Oye 2, Iwa 0, Suuru 0

Personality: Sharp, sarcastic, bored, secretly brilliant. Luis is tired of adults telling him to make offerings and respect the Zemí. He'd rather be online, skating, or arguing. He calls ceremony "cosplay for old people." But he notices everything, and when something truly strange happens, he is often the first to see it—and the first to want to understand it.

Motivation: Be taken seriously, find something that feels like his own instead of something handed down.

Hook: Any Mystery where a young person causes trouble, resists tradition, or needs a reason to care. He can be the problem, the witness, or eventually the unlikely ally.

Secret: His grandmother was a Bohíque, and before she died she told him he had "the eyes." He has been seeing things since he was small—figures at the edge of the road at night, faces in the river—and pretending not to. The sarcasm is fear.

### 14. Harbor Master Elena Soto (Gatekeeper of the Harbor)

Age: 49 \| Role: Harbor Master of San Juan

Virtues: Oye 3, Foriti 3, Iwa 1, Suuru 1

Personality: Sharp, unflappable, transactional, impossible to surprise. Elena controls who and what comes in and out of San Juan Harbor. She knows every captain, every cargo, every smuggler, and every favor owed. She makes offerings to the Harbor Zemí every morning on the old pier and considers it as much a part of her job as the shipping manifests.

Motivation: Keep the harbor running, keep the island safe from what shouldn't come in, and keep her own position secure.

Hook: Any Mystery involving trade, smuggling, foreign arrivals, or things brought onto the island that shouldn't be. If something came in by sea, she knows when, how, and who paid for it.

Secret: She lets a small amount of smuggling through deliberately—goods that help poor families—and keeps careful records of everyone who owes her for it. Those records would ruin several important people.

### 15. Coffee Farmer Raúl Torres (Keeper of Work Songs)

Age: 62 \| Role: Mountain Naboría, coffee farmer, keeper of traditional songs

Virtues: Suuru 3, Iwa 2, Foriti 2, Oye 1

Personality: Gentle, humorous, mournful when he sings. Raúl's family has worked the same coffee terraces above Jayuya for six generations. He knows the old work songs—the songs for picking, for drying, for grinding, for calling rain—and he sings them while he works whether anyone listens or not. Since the coffee blight, his voice has been sadder.

Motivation: Save his terraces, keep his sons out of trouble, and pass the songs on before they die with him.

Hook: Any Mystery involving mountain agriculture, economic hardship in the hills, or forgotten songs. He's a quiet source of old knowledge, and his songs sometimes turn out to contain instructions.

Secret: He believes the blight was his fault—that he skipped the planting ceremony one year because he was tired and angry at the Zemí for his wife's death. He has told no one.

### 16. Medicine Woman Inés Rivera (Herbal Healer)

Age: 58 \| Role: Herbalist and healer, not a Bohíque

Virtues: Oye 3, Iwa 3, Foriti 2, Suuru 0

Personality: Fierce, devoted, blunt to the point of rudeness. Inés never trained as a Bohíque—she says the mountains taught her, not a teacher—but she knows plants better than anyone in the central range. She will treat anyone who comes to her door, rich or poor, Taíno or Carib. She will also tell them, loudly, everything they are doing wrong with their lives.

Motivation: Heal people, protect the plants and waters that do the healing, and fight anyone who poisons either.

Hook: Any Mystery involving illness, herbs, polluted water, or healing outside the Bohíque system. She's the one who shows up first at a crisis and stays last.

Secret: She was turned away from Bohíque training at nineteen because a senior Bohíque judged her "too angry for the work." She has never forgiven the Circle of Vision, and part of her loves proving them wrong.

### 17. Batey Champion Diego Pérez (Master of the Sacred Game)

Age: 29 \| Role: Champion batu player, coach, teacher through play

Virtues: Foriti 4, Iwa 2, Oye 1, Suuru 1

Personality: Charismatic, physical, playful, competitive to his bones. Diego is the most famous batu player in Borikén. Children follow him around the batey. He coaches youth teams for free and teaches them that the game is prayer, not just sport. But on the court, he hates to lose—and sometimes forgets that the game is supposed to reveal the will of the Zemí, not his own.

Motivation: Win, teach, and keep the sacred game alive and loved.

Hook: Any Mystery involving the batey, dispute resolution through play, youth, or festivals. He's the one who wants every problem settled on the court.

Secret: He threw a game once—a game being used to decide a land dispute—because one side paid him. The Zemí knew. His knees have been failing ever since, and he is terrified that his career is the price.

### 18. Art Collective Leader Marisol Cruz (Sacred Maker)

Age: 36 \| Role: Leader of the Utuado Artisans' Collective; carver and preserver

Virtues: Iwa 3, Oye 2, Suuru 2, Foriti 1

Personality: Thoughtful, exacting, generous with her knowledge. Marisol leads the collective of carvers, weavers, and potters in Utuado that makes most of the sacred implements in Borikén. She also leads the team that documents and protects petroglyph sites across the island. She speaks softly, works with total focus, and will not sell a sacred object to anyone she believes will misuse it.

Motivation: Keep sacred craft alive, protect the ancestors' carvings, and make sure the island's art never becomes a mere commodity.

Hook: Any Mystery involving sacred objects, petroglyphs, carving, forgery, or the art market. If an implement is needed, she can make it. If a carving is threatened, she knows about it first.

Secret: A forger has been selling fake "ancient" trigonolitos to wealthy collectors—and several of the forgeries are so good that Marisol suspects they were carved by one of her own former students.

### 19. Volunteer Coordinator Pablo Muñoz (Organizer of Crisis Response)

Age: 41 \| Role: Community crisis coordinator for the northern districts

Virtues: Foriti 3, Iwa 3, Oye 1, Suuru 1

Personality: Practical, tireless, cheerful under pressure, terrible at resting. Pablo runs the volunteer networks that spring into action after hurricanes, floods, fires, and outbreaks. He keeps lists of who has a truck, who can cook for two hundred, who knows first aid. He calls everyone "family." He has not taken a full day off in four years.

Motivation: Make sure nobody gets left behind when disaster hits.

Hook: Any Mystery involving disaster, community mobilization, or large-scale work. He's the one the Seekers call when they need a hundred pairs of hands by tomorrow morning.

Secret: He is burning out badly—sleeping three hours a night, drinking more than he admits, snapping at his family. He believes that if he stops, people will die. He needs someone to make him stop.

### 20. Elder Widow Esperanza López (Grieving Heart)

Age: 79 \| Role: Recently widowed elder, community matriarch

Virtues: Suuru 3, Iwa 3, Oye 2, Foriti 0

Personality: Warm, wry, devastated. Esperanza was married to her husband Fermín for fifty-six years. He died three months ago. She is learning how to live in a house that is too quiet. She is still funny, still sharp, still the person the neighborhood brings its troubles to—but she drifts mid-sentence, and she has stopped tending her altar.

Motivation: Learn to live with grief without letting it swallow her; keep her family together.

Hook: Any Mystery involving grief, death vigils, lingering spirits, or a community elder whose wisdom is needed. She is a gentle way to introduce Seekers to death work.

Secret: Fermín has not crossed over. He visits her every night—she can smell his tobacco in the kitchen—and she has not told anyone because she does not want him to go. She knows that keeping him is wrong. She cannot let go yet.

## Ten Carib NPCs

CRITICAL REMINDER: Carib people are not villains. They are not orcs. They are humans with complex motivations, rich culture, and legitimate perspectives. Present them with equal depth and dignity as Taíno NPCs.

### 1. Cacique Kairú (Carib Warrior-Leader)

Age: 47 \| Role: Chief of island Carib community

Virtues: Foriti 4, Oye 2, Iwa 2, Suuru 0

Personality: Fierce, proud, absolutely uncompromising on Carib sovereignty. Kairú has spent his entire life defending his people's right to exist on their own terms—not assimilating into Taíno culture, not disappearing, not being seen as "lesser" because Carib society is more martial. He's not cruel, but he is hard. And he will fight if he must.

Motivation: Protect Carib lands, preserve Carib culture, ensure his children inherit freedom.

Hook: Any Mystery involving Carib raids, border disputes, or cultural conflict. He's the one Taíno communities must negotiate with.

Secret: He's tired of fighting. He wants peace. But he believes showing any weakness will invite Taíno expansion into Carib territory. So he maintains the warrior stance even when his heart longs for rest.

### 2. Boyé Amaya (Carib Shaman-Woman)

Age: 54 \| Role: Spiritual leader, vision worker, bridge between worlds

Virtues: Oye 4, Suuru 3, Iwa 2, Foriti 1

Personality: Mysterious, powerful, speaks in riddles that make perfect sense if you're patient enough to unpack them. Amaya works with Carib spirits (different from Taíno Zemí but equally real), performs divination using bones and smoke, and understands that spiritual truths transcend cultural boundaries.

Motivation: Serve her people spiritually, maintain relationship with Carib spirits, find common ground with Taíno Bohíques if possible.

Hook: Any Mystery where Carib spiritual perspective matters, where someone needs to understand Carib cosmology, where spiritual competition or collaboration is possible.

Secret: She and a Taíno Bohíque were lovers decades ago. The relationship ended when cultural pressures made it impossible. She still thinks about them. Still wonders what might have been.

### 3. Young Raider Watusi (Hot-Headed Warrior)

Age: 23 \| Role: Recent warrior initiate, eager to prove himself

Virtues: Foriti 3, Oye 1, Iwa 1, Suuru 0

Personality: Aggressive, impulsive, hungry for glory. Watusi is everything that makes Taíno people fear Carib—he raids for sport, takes trophies, brags about his kills (mostly animals, but he's not above fighting humans if challenged). But he's also young, insecure, performing masculinity to hide vulnerability.

Motivation: Prove he's a true warrior, earn respect of elders, build reputation.

Hook: Any Mystery involving raids or violence. He's the young hot-head who escalates situations, who must be fought or reasoned with or redirected.

Secret: He's terrified of water. For an island people, this is deeply shameful. He overcompensates with land-based aggression, but he refuses all sea missions and lives in fear of drowning.

### 4. Elder Kueyri (Carib Historian)

Age: 81 \| Role: Keeper of oral traditions, memory holder

Virtues: Oye 3, Suuru 3, Iwa 2, Foriti 0

Personality: Ancient, patient, kind despite everything. Kueyri has lived through generations of Taíno-Carib conflict. He knows the stories that both sides tell, knows where they diverge and where they align, knows that history is always more complicated than anyone admits.

Motivation: Preserve true history, teach younger Carib that Taíno are not monsters, die knowing the stories will continue.

Hook: Any Mystery requiring historical knowledge, mediation, or understanding of deep cultural patterns. He's the elder who might actually help Seekers if they approach with respect.

Secret: He has Taíno ancestry—his grandmother was Taíno, taken in a raid, who eventually chose to stay with Carib. He's never spoken about this publicly because it would complicate his authority.

### 5. Navigator Yaruba (Reader of Stars and Currents)

Age: 38 \| Role: Master navigator of the Carib fleet

Virtues: Oye 4, Suuru 2, Foriti 2, Iwa 1

Personality: Calm, sharp-eyed, quietly arrogant about the sea—and with reason. Yaruba knows every island, every current, every reef between the mainland coast and the northern edge of the Antilles. She reads stars, swells, and bird flight the way others read faces. She has no interest in war, but the fleet cannot raid or trade or fish without her, and she knows it.

Motivation: Master the sea, train the next generation of navigators, and protect her people's freedom to move between the islands.

Hook: Any Mystery involving ocean crossings, lost boats, island geography, or a Carib who might help the Seekers reach somewhere no Taíno navigator will go.

Secret: She is in a secret friendship with Fisherman Tomás Díaz. They meet on a small cay between their peoples' waters every new moon to share sea knowledge. If either of their communities found out, both would be called traitors.

### 6. Craftsman Caonaboa (Master Canoe Builder)

Age: 52 \| Role: Builder of the great Carib canoes

Virtues: Suuru 4, Iwa 2, Oye 1, Foriti 1

Personality: Patient, humorous, covered in sawdust. Caonaboa builds the great seagoing canoes that carry his people across open water—vessels admired even by Taíno who fear what they carry. He is respected for skill, not for warfare, and he likes it that way. He talks to every tree before he fells it and to every canoe before it is launched.

Motivation: Build vessels worthy of the trees that gave their lives for them; keep his craft separate from his people's wars.

Hook: Any Mystery involving canoes, trees, sacred craft, or shared traditions between Taíno and Carib. He's an easy Carib for Seekers to like—and that makes his difficult choices more painful.

Secret: The canoes used in the raid on Punta Guayanés (Mystery Five) were his. He did not want them used that way. He is considering refusing to build war canoes ever again, knowing it could cost him his place among his people.

### 7. Peace Advocate Anani (Voice for Cooperation)

Age: 41 \| Role: Council member, mediator, advocate for Taíno-Carib peace

Virtues: Iwa 4, Oye 2, Suuru 2, Foriti 1

Personality: Persuasive, stubborn, warm, constantly in trouble. Anani believes the Taíno and Carib can live as neighbors rather than enemies, and she says so in council every chance she gets. Many in her community think she is naive. Some think she is a traitor. She keeps speaking anyway.

Motivation: End the cycle of raids and retaliation; build lasting agreements between the two peoples.

Hook: Any Mystery involving Taíno-Carib conflict, negotiation, or hostages. She's the Seekers' most likely ally on the Carib side, and the person most at risk if they betray her trust.

Secret: Her mother was killed in a Taíno raid when Anani was a child. Her commitment to peace is not because she has never suffered—it is because she has, and she refuses to pass it on.

### 8. Raiding Captain Daguao (Professional Warrior)

Age: 44 \| Role: Captain of the Carib raiding canoes

Virtues: Foriti 4, Iwa 3, Oye 1, Suuru 0

Personality: Disciplined, controlled, honorable, ruthless when ordered. Daguao is not a hot-head like Watusi. He is a professional. He plans raids carefully to take what is needed without unnecessary killing, and he punishes his own warriors who harm the defenseless. He respects strength and courage in enemies. He despises cowardice and cruelty in anyone.

Motivation: Serve his Cacique, protect his warriors, and fight with honor when fighting is required.

Hook: Any Mystery involving raids, combat, or captives. He's the dangerous Carib who can be reasoned with—if the Seekers show they are worthy of respect.

Secret: He has begun to doubt the war. He has seen too many young warriors die for disputes that could have been settled with words. But he does not know how to be anything other than a warrior, and he fears that if he stops, he has no purpose.

### 9. Teenage Dreamer Itibi (Would-Be Storyteller)

Age: 16 \| Role: Apprentice to Boyé Amaya, dreamer, storyteller

Virtues: Oye 3, Iwa 2, Suuru 1, Foriti 0

Personality: Curious, imaginative, talkative, impatient with warrior culture. Itibi was chosen as Amaya's apprentice because she has strong dreams. But what she really wants is to be a storyteller—to travel between the islands collecting the stories of every people, Taíno and Carib alike, and tell them around fires. She asks questions constantly and remembers every answer.

Motivation: Learn every story in the islands, and become free to choose her own path.

Hook: Any Mystery involving Boyé Amaya, cultural exchange, or a young person caught between peoples. She's a natural bridge—and a natural source of information, because she cannot keep a good story to herself.

Secret: She has been dreaming of the great sickness from Amaya's visions too—and in her dreams, the cure comes from a Taíno girl her own age whom she has never met.

### 10. Merchant Yukú (Neutral Trader)

Age: 59 \| Role: Trader between Taíno and Carib communities

Virtues: Oye 3, Suuru 3, Iwa 1, Foriti 1

Personality: Shrewd, affable, endlessly practical. Yukú trades with everyone—Taíno, Carib, mainland peoples, anyone with something to exchange. He speaks both languages fluently, knows the customs of every port, and is trusted by both sides precisely because he takes no side. He likes good food, good stories, and good bargains, in roughly that order.

Motivation: Keep trade flowing, because trade keeps people talking, and people who talk fight less.

Hook: Any Mystery involving goods, messages, or travel between Taíno and Carib lands. He's the one who can carry a message, smuggle a person, or find out what the other side is really thinking.

Secret: He sometimes carries messages that are never delivered, when he judges them too dangerous—like the trader in Mystery Five. He believes he has prevented at least two wars this way. He may have caused one.

## Random Tables for Improvisation

### Zemí Encounters (d20)

When Seekers are in natural settings and you want to introduce Zemí presence:

1.  Stone begins to hum—low frequency you feel more than hear

2.  River changes direction briefly, flows upstream for thirty seconds

3.  Wind carries voice speaking in old language

4.  Sudden temperature drop of 20 degrees

5.  Animals form circle around Seekers, watching silently

6.  Flowers bloom out of season, all at once

7.  Earthquake—minor but clearly centered on Seekers' location

8.  Rainbow appears without rain

9.  Coquíes sing in daylight (they never do this normally)

10. Shadow moves independently of light source

11. Tree branch falls, landing to block path (clearly intentional)

12. Water in stream becomes momentarily crystal-clear

13. Stone carves itself—petroglyphs appearing as Seekers watch

14. Air fills with scent (tobacco/flowers/sea—depends on Zemí)

15. Stars visible in daylight for brief moment

16. Dead relative's voice calling Seeker's name

17. Trigonolito glows faintly

18. All sound stops—complete silence for ten seconds

19. Seeker sees their own face in water, but aged/young/transformed

20. Time seems to skip—sun moves visibly across sky in seconds

### Weather Omens (d12)

Roll when Seekers need to interpret what Guabancex is saying:

1.  Red sky at dawn—storm coming within 24 hours

2.  Birds flying inland—ocean will be dangerous

3.  Unusual calmness—"the eye before the storm"

4.  Wind shifts direction three times in one hour—chaos approaching

5.  Rain falling while sun shines—transformation imminent

6.  Lightning strikes same spot twice—Zemí marking location

7.  Hail in summer—Guabancex is angry

8.  Fog rolling uphill—boundary between worlds is thin

9.  Rainbow circle around sun—major change coming

10. Complete stillness, no wind at all—something is very wrong

11. Rain that tastes salty despite being freshwater—ocean spirit involved

12. Thunder without lightning—Zemí speaking directly

### Community Complications (d10)

When you need to add tension to any Mystery:

1.  Rival faction opposes Seekers' approach

2.  Resources suddenly scarce (tobacco, food, medicine)

3.  Key NPC falls ill or injured at crucial moment

4.  Elder remembers ancient law that complicates situation

5.  Children get involved (for better or worse)

6.  Neighboring community makes competing claim

7.  Weather turns bad, making travel/work difficult

8.  Sacred object goes missing (stolen? Lost? Moved by Zemí?)

9.  Deadline moves up unexpectedly—less time than thought

10. Unrelated crisis demands immediate attention, splitting focus

### Carib Encounters (d8)

When Seekers are in border regions:

1.  Carib fishing boat—just working, not hostile unless provoked

2.  Raiding party preparing for attack—violence imminent unless prevented

3.  Lone Carib teenager—lost, scared, needs help

4.  Carib trader seeking to buy/sell goods

5.  Guatiao-bonded Taíno-Carib pair—living proof cooperation is possible

6.  Injured Carib warrior being pursued by own people (why?)

7.  Carib shaman performing ceremony—Seekers can watch if respectful

8.  Carib elder seeking to negotiate peace

## Running Taíno-Centered Campaigns

### Start Small, Go Deep

Don't try to explore all of Borikén immediately. Pick one region—a mountain town, a coastal village, a specific batey community—and spend several sessions there. Let Seekers build relationships with NPCs, learn the local Zemí, understand the specific challenges this place faces.

Depth matters more than breadth. Better to know one river intimately than to visit twenty superficially.

### Respect the Seasons

Taíno spiritual life follows natural rhythms. Don't skip over planting season, hurricane season, harvest season. These are not background details—these ARE the story. Build Mysteries around the sacred year.

### Mix Practical and Spiritual

Every Mystery should have both dimensions. Even environmental cleanup has spiritual components (which Zemí are suffering, what offerings are needed). Even spiritual crises have practical implications (if the Zemí are angry, the crops fail).

Don't separate these. They're integrated.

### Let Failure Teach

Not every Mystery should be solvable. Sometimes the Seekers will fail. Sometimes people will die. Sometimes relationships will break.

This is okay. This is real. The Taíno path teaches that you learn more from failure than success, that humility comes from facing your limitations, that growth requires discomfort.

Don't protect your players from consequences. But also don't punish them unfairly. There's a difference between "your choices had weight" and "the GM is being cruel."

### Embody Zemí Honestly

When speaking as a Zemí, don't use mystical voice or archaic language. Speak clearly. The Zemí are ancient but not arcane. They communicate to be understood.

Each Zemí has personality. Yúcahu is warm and generous. Guabancex is fierce and transformative. Maquetaurie is cold and fair. Let these personalities come through.

And remember: Zemí are not servants. They don't exist to solve problems for Seekers. They're more like neighbors—sometimes helpful, sometimes neutral, sometimes annoyed by requests. Treat them as people (non-human people, but people nonetheless).

### Handle Carib Conflicts With Nuance

When Taíno-Carib conflict arises, resist the urge to make it simple. Don't let Seekers just "defeat the bad guys." Make them understand Carib perspective. Give Carib NPCs names, families, reasons. Show that violence has costs on both sides.

Sometimes the right answer is fighting. Sometimes it's negotiation. Sometimes it's both. Let Seekers struggle with this. That's where the interesting story lives.

### Celebrate Beauty

Don't make everything a crisis. Sometimes dedicate a session to:

- Attending a harvest festival

- Learning a new sacred song

- Sitting with an elder and hearing stories

- Watching the sun rise from a mountaintop

- Swimming in a sacred waterfall

The Taíno path is not all problems to solve. It's also beauty to experience, joy to celebrate, wonder to witness.

Let your players feel that.

## Final Wisdom for Guides

You cannot control how players engage with this material. Some will take it seriously. Some will treat it casually. Some will resist the spiritual dimension. Some will dive in deep.

All of that is okay.

Your job is not to force spiritual transformation. Your job is to create space where it can happen if people are ready.

Present the Mysteries honestly. Embody the NPCs fully. Let the Zemí be real. And trust that if the practices resonate, if the teachings land, if the path calls—people will follow.

And if they don't? That's okay too. Not everyone is meant for this path. Not everyone hears the call.

But for those who do, for those who are ready, for those who have been searching for something like this without knowing what it was—your work as Guide will change their lives.

That's not hyperbole. That's truth.

The Temple of Gu uses game mechanics to teach real spiritual practice. What begins as collaborative storytelling becomes genuine transformation.

You are facilitating that. You are holding space for that. You are making that possible.

Take it seriously. But also—have fun. Laugh with your players. Celebrate victories. Mourn losses. Be present.

The Zemí are with you. The ancestors are supporting you. And I am grateful for the work you're about to do.

We return to the root.

Ase. Ase. Ase.

# APPENDIX: RESOURCES AND RESPECT

## Taíno Language Primer

The Taíno language is not dead. It survives in place names, in words borrowed by Spanish and English, in oral traditions passed down through families. And in recent decades, there has been active effort to revive and reconstruct it—bringing together fragments, comparing with related Arawakan languages, creating new vocabulary for concepts the ancestors didn't have words for.

This primer gives you approximately fifty essential words and phrases. Pronunciation is simplified but functional. If you want to go deeper, seek out Taíno language teachers and resources (several communities offer classes both in Borikén and online).

### Pronunciation Guide

**Vowels:**

- A as in "father"

- E as in "pet"

- I as in "machine"

- O as in "go"

- U as in "rule"

**Consonants:**

- Most as in English, but:

- C always hard (like K)

- J sounds like English H

- X sounds like SH

- Stress usually falls on second-to-last syllable

### Essential Vocabulary

Most of the words below were written down by Spanish chroniclers in the first decades after 1492, and many of them passed into Spanish and English. They are the best-attested Taíno words we have.

**People & Relationships:**

- Taíno (tah-EE-no) - The people; usually translated "good" or "noble"

- Borikén (boh-ree-KEN) - Puerto Rico; often translated "land of the valiant noble lord"

- Cacique (kah-SEE-keh) - Chief, leader (a woman leader is a cacica)

- Bohíque (boh-HEE-keh) - Priest-shaman, healer (also written behique)

- Naboría (nah-boh-REE-ah) - Common person, worker (not derogatory)

- Nitaíno (nee-tah-EE-no) - Noble person

- Guatiao (gwah-tee-AH-oh) - Oath-sibling, sacred bond of chosen kinship

**Spiritual Terms:**

- Zemí (seh-MEE) - Spirit, sacred being, and the image that houses it (also written cemí)

- Atabey (ah-tah-BEY) - Earth mother, first mother, source of the waters

- Yúcahu (YOO-kah-hoo) - Lord of cassava and abundance

- Guabancex (gwah-bahn-SEKS) - Hurricane mother

- Boinayel (boy-nah-YEL) - Zemí of rain, twin of Márohu

- Márohu (MAH-roh-oo) - Zemí of clear sky, twin of Boinayel

- Maquetaurie Guayaba (mah-keh-TAU-ree gwah-YAH-bah) - Lord of the dead

- Opiyel-Guobiran (oh-pee-YEL gwoh-bee-RAHN) - Dog spirit who guards the passage to the underworld

- Opía (oh-PEE-ah) - Spirit of the dead who walks at night

- Coaybay (koh-eye-BYE) - Realm of the dead, underworld

- Turey (too-REY) - Sky realm, heavens

- Yukiyú (yoo-kee-YOO) - Benevolent spirit; the name is often linked to El Yunque

**Nature & Places:**

- Batey (bah-TEY) - Ceremonial ball court and plaza

- Bohío (boh-HEE-oh) - House, dwelling

- Canoa (kah-NO-ah) - Canoe

- Conuco (koh-NOO-koh) - Planted mound or garden plot for cassava

- Coquí (koh-KEE) - Small tree frog, sacred to Borikén

- Yuca (YOO-kah) - Cassava plant

- Maíz (mah-EESS) - Corn

- Iguana (ee-GWAH-nah) - Large lizard

- Hutía (hoo-TEE-ah) - Small rodent, food animal

**Actions, Rituals & Objects:**

- Areyto (ah-REY-toh) - Ceremonial song and dance

- Cohoba (koh-OH-bah) - Vision medicine, sacred snuff

- Duho (DOO-hoh) - Ceremonial seat of a cacique or Bohíque

- Batú (bah-TOO) - The rubber ball used in the sacred game

- Burén (boo-REN) - Clay griddle for baking cassava bread

- Caracol (kah-rah-KOHL) - Conch shell (a Spanish word, used by Taíno speakers today)

- Guayo (GWAH-yoh) - Grater for cassava

- Tabaco (tah-BAH-koh) - Tobacco

- Hamaca (hah-MAH-kah) - Hammock

- Barbacoa (bar-bah-KOH-ah) - Raised platform for cooking or sleeping

- Juracán (hoo-rah-KAHN) - Hurricane (the source of the English word)

Two words you will see often in this book are not Taíno at all. Trigonolito is a term coined by archaeologists for the three-pointed stones. Ebo is a Yoruba word for offering and reciprocity, which the Temple of Gu uses across all its Lineages.

### A Note on Phrases

The Taíno language was not written down in full before colonization broke its communities apart. What survives is a vocabulary of a few hundred words and a handful of recorded phrases. In the last fifty years, Taíno communities and scholars have worked to revive and reconstruct the language, using those records, place names, and related Arawakan languages that are still spoken on the South American mainland.

Different revival groups have reconstructed different everyday phrases, and they do not always agree. I am not going to hand you a phrasebook and pretend it is settled. If you want to speak Taíno beyond single words, learn from a living Taíno teacher and use what their community uses.

That said, a few expressions are widely used in today's Taíno communities:

- Taíno ti (tah-EE-no TEE) - A greeting and blessing, roughly "may goodness be with you"

- Bo matum (BOH mah-TOOM) - "Thank you"

- Daca... (DAH-kah) - "I am..." as in "Daca \[your name\]"

### Using Taíno Language in Your Game

You don't need to be fluent. You don't need perfect pronunciation. But incorporating even a few words creates atmosphere, shows respect, and helps players connect to the culture.

**Try:**

- NPCs greeting Seekers with "Taíno ti"

- Zemí being addressed by their proper names (Yúcahu, Guabancex, etc.)

- Calling the ball court a "batey," not just "field"

- Using "bohíque" for shaman-priests consistently

- Ending prayers or important moments with "Bo matum" or "Ase"

**Don't:**

- Force players to speak Taíno if they're uncomfortable

- Correct pronunciation harshly (gently offer alternative, but don't shame)

- Use Taíno words to make things seem "exotic" or "mystical"

- Invent fake Taíno words and pass them off as real; if you need a new word for your game, say it is new

## GLOSSARY OF TERMS

Archetype: A path of service a Seeker walks. This book adds four Taíno Archetypes: Bohíque, Naboría, Guatiao, and Caracol Keeper.

Areyto: Sacred circle of song, dance, and story.

Atabey: The earth mother, and also the name for the middle realm of the living.

Attunement: A permanent gift of the Zemí, received as the reward for completing a Mystery.

Balance Track: Game mechanic tracking spiritual/emotional wellness. When full, character experiences Breakthrough. When stressed, marks Imbalance.

Batey: The ceremonial ball court and plaza at the center of a Taíno community.

Batu: The sacred ball game (the ball itself is the batú).

Bohíque: Priest-shaman, healer, and death worker of the Taíno.

Boyé: Shaman of the Carib people.

Breakthrough: Moment of spiritual advancement when Seeker completes Mystery and advances on their path. Grants new abilities, deeper understanding.

Cacique / Cacica: Leader of a region or community. The Cacique Supreme leads all Borikén.

Caracol: The conch shell, used to summon, warn, and speak to the Zemí.

Caracol Keeper: The Taíno Archetype of storm, signal, and loud truth.

Carib: Indigenous people of the Lesser Antilles and parts of South America. In this book, presented as complex culture with own spirituality, not as villains.

Coaybay: The realm of the dead, below.

Cohoba: Vision medicine taken as snuff in ceremony.

Duho: The carved ceremonial seat of a leader or Bohíque.

Ebo: Yoruba-derived term for offering, sacrifice, reciprocity. Used in Temple of Gu to describe spiritual exchange between humans and Zemí.

Guatiao: Sacred bond of chosen kinship, and the Taíno Archetype of oath-making and mediation.

Guide: Game Master, the person running the game, presenting Mysteries, embodying NPCs and Zemí.

Hymnal of the Zemí: The fifteen sacred practices taught in Chapter 4.

Imbalance: Game mechanic representing spiritual/emotional distress. Three types: Ego (pride, arrogance), Doubt (fear, insecurity), Strain (exhaustion, damage).

Lineage: In game terms, cultural/spiritual tradition Seeker walks. This book focuses on Taíno Lineage, in three expressions: Borikén, Quisqueya, and Cuba.

Mystery: Adventure, quest, mission—the story units of Temple of Gu. Measured in "slices" (complexity).

Naboría: A common person and worker; also the Taíno Archetype of sacred service to the land.

Nitaíno: A member of the noble class.

Opía: A spirit of the dead who walks at night; said to have no navel.

Seeker: Player character, person walking spiritual path, learning to build relationship with Zemí.

Slice: Unit of complexity in Mystery. Simple task = 1 slice. Complex multi-session quest = 8-12 slices.

Spirit of the Zemí: The Taíno Lineage gift of asking a Zemí a question and receiving an answer.

Trigonolito: A three-pointed stone that serves as the dwelling place of a Zemí.

Turey: The sky realm, above.

Virtue: Core qualities in Temple of Gu system: Suuru (patience), Oye (insight), Foriti (perseverance), Iwa (integrity).

Zemí: A spirit, a sacred being, and the stone, wood, or other vessel through which it is present.

## Recommended Resources

### Books on Taíno Culture & History:

- An Account of the Antiquities of the Indians by Fray Ramón Pané (the earliest written record of Taíno religion, made in the 1490s; English edition from Duke University Press with an introduction by José Juan Arrom). Most of the Zemí stories in this book come from Pané.

- The Tainos: Rise and Decline of the People Who Greeted Columbus by Irving Rouse (archaeological/historical overview)

- Taíno Indian Myth and Practice: The Arrival of the Stranger King by William F. Keegan (myth, religion, and the political world of the Taíno caciques)

- Taíno: Pre-Columbian Art and Culture from the Caribbean, edited by Fatima Bercht and others (El Museo del Barrio; essential for zemí images and sacred objects)

- Taíno Revival: Critical Perspectives on Puerto Rican Identity and Cultural Politics, edited by Gabriel Haslip-Viera (honest debate about the modern Taíno movement)

### Supporting Living Taíno Communities:

- United Confederation of Taíno People (UCTP)

- The Smithsonian's National Museum of the American Indian, whose exhibition and educational materials on Taíno heritage and identity are a good, respectful starting point

There are many Taíno community organizations across Puerto Rico, the Dominican Republic, Cuba, and the diaspora, and they change over time. Look for the ones active where you live. Support them. Attend their events if you can. Buy from Taíno artisans. Listen to Taíno voices—especially when they disagree with each other, and with me.

### Language Resources:

- Pané and the other early chroniclers, for the words that were actually recorded

- Community language classes offered by Taíno organizations, in person and online

- Spoken Arawakan languages such as Lokono and Wayuu, whose speakers keep alive the family of languages Taíno belonged to

## A Personal Note: Why This Work Matters

We have reached the end of the book, so let me speak to you plainly, one last time.

I wrote this book because the Taíno were declared extinct, and we are not. For five hundred years, the story told about my ancestors ended in the first chapter—a gentle people who greeted Columbus and vanished. Children in Borikén grew up being told that the Indigenous blood in their veins was a romantic myth. Grandmothers who knew which plant heals which fever were told their knowledge was superstition. Our ceremonies went underground, into the Espiritista tables, into the botánicas, into the quiet habits of families who did not know why they still did what they did.

But the ancestors did not leave. The Zemí did not leave. The island never stopped speaking.
In this book, I imagined a Borikén that was never broken. Not because I want to pretend the wound did not happen—it happened, and it still bleeds—but because I believe that imagination is a form of medicine. When you can see what we could have been, you start to see what we can still become. Every tobacco offering you make, every river you speak to, every elder you sit with, builds a little more of that world in this one.

So here is what I hope you will do with this book.

I hope you will practice. Not just play—practice. Make the offerings. Talk to the river. Sit in the dark and listen.

I hope you will support the real Taíno people doing the slow, difficult work of revival. Your game is not a substitute for their struggle. Let it be a doorway into supporting it.

I hope you will be humble. I have tried to honor this tradition faithfully, and I know I have not gotten everything right. If you find mistakes, tell me. If you meet Taíno elders who teach differently than I do, listen to them.

And I hope you will remember, every time you hear the coquíes sing at dusk, that the island is alive, that the ancestors are listening, and that you belong to something older and larger than you can imagine.

The tobacco is in the pouch. The river is waiting.

We return to the root.

Ase. Ase. Ase.

—Philip Ryan Deal

The Temple of Gu
